On a frosty January evening in the early 1870s, the celebrated Christine Nilsson stepped onto the stage of New York’s Academy of Music to sing the lead in *Faust*, and the whole of fashionable New York turned out to see her.

Even though whispers were already swirling of a grand new opera house to be built miles north, “above the Forties,” designed to match the opulence of Paris and Vienna’s most famous venues, the city’s elite were perfectly happy to crowd into the shabby, velvet-lined old Academy every winter. Conservatives adored it for being cramped and inconvenient enough to keep out the brash “new people” New York both feared and secretly longed to rub elbows with; sentimental patrons clung to decades of happy memories woven into its red and gold drapery; and opera fanatics swore its acoustics, the trickiest possible thing to get right in a new music hall, were finer than any in Europe.

It was Nilsson’s first performance of the season, and the papers were already calling the packed crowd “exceptionally brilliant.” Audience members had glided through slush-crusted streets in private broughams, sprawling family landaus, or the far more convenient Brown coupes—rental carriages so respectable that arriving in one was almost as much of a status symbol as pulling up behind your own family’s crest. Even better, they let you escape the opera as soon as the curtain fell, no waiting for your own coachman to rouse himself from the pub and rub the cold gin fog off his spectacles. The owner of the Brown livery stables had made a fortune on that single sharp insight: Americans are far more eager to dash home after a good time than they are to arrive on time for it.

When Newland Archer pushed open the door at the back of his club box, the curtain had just risen on the famous garden scene. There was no good reason he had been late: he had dined at seven sharp with his mother and sister, and only lingered afterward to smoke a cigar in the house’s Gothic library, the only room Mrs. Archer allowed tobacco, lined with black walnut glazed bookcases and chairs topped with carved wooden finials. But first, New York high society ran on unwritten rules as sacred and unnegotiable as the ancient tribal taboos of Archer’s long-ago ancestors—and arriving early to the opera was simply *not the thing*. Second, Archer was a man who savored the build-up to a small, delicate pleasure far more than the pleasure itself. And tonight, the moment he’d been daydreaming of for weeks was about to unfold.

His timing could not have been more perfect. The prima donna was just lifting her voice to trill the daisy song, plucking the petals one by one: “M’ama… non m’ama… M’ama!” Her notes fell as clear as dew on grass, proclaiming “He loves me” triumphantly into the hush of the house. Of course, she sang the lines in Italian, not the original French libretto, not her native Swedish: it was an unbreakable law of the opera world that all work must be translated into Italian for the comfort of English-speaking New York audiences, and Archer thought that every bit as natural as his own unshakable habits: combing his hair with two silver-backed brushes engraved with his monogram in blue enamel, never stepping out into society without a fresh gardenia pinned to his buttonhole.

He leaned against the back wall of the box, and let his gaze drift away from the stage to the opposite side of the house. Directly across from him was the private box belonging to old Mrs. Manson Mingott, whose legendary obesity had long ago made it impossible for her to climb the opera steps, though she always sent a younger relative to claim the best seat on any big performance night. Tonight, the front of the box was occupied by her daughter-in-law Mrs. Lovell Mingott, and her own daughter Mrs. Welland. Slightly withdrawn behind those two brocaded, matronly figures sat a young girl in all white, her wide blue eyes fixed rapturously on the stage lovers. When Nilsson’s final triumphant “M’ama!” rang out so clear even the chattering ladies in the surrounding boxes fell silent, a warm rose pink flooded May Welland’s cheeks, creeping up to the very roots of her fair braids and spilling down the soft slope of her throat to the edge of her modest tulle collar, fastened with a single tiny gardenia. She dropped her eyes to the massive bouquet of lilies of the valley resting on her knee, and Archer watched the tips of her white-gloved fingers brush the flower petals softly. He drew a slow, satisfied breath, a warm spark of pride and possessiveness blooming in his chest.

The stage set was widely agreed to be magnificent, even by men like Archer who had seen the opera houses of Paris and Vienna. The whole foreground down to the footlights was covered in rich emerald green cloth. Farther back, symmetrical mounds of fuzzy green moss edged with croquet hoops held topiary orange trees studded with giant pink and red roses, and enormous pansies that looked almost exactly like the embroidered floral pen-wipers parish women stitched for their favorite clergymen. Even the daisies were grafted onto rose branches, blooming twice as lushly as any wild garden flower. Nilsson wandered through that enchanted fake garden in a white cashmere gown slashed with pale blue satin, her heavy yellow braids arranged neatly over her muslin chemisette, pretending to have no idea that Faust’s flowery wooing was trying to lure her toward the neat little brick villa door peeking out from the right wing of the set.

*The darling*, Archer thought, his gaze darting right back to May’s soft, glowing face. She hasn’t the faintest guess what all his pretty lines are really about. His chest ached with a tender reverence for her bright, unspoiled purity, tangled up with masculine pride that he would be the one to show her the world. He had daydreams, already, of the two of them reading *Faust* side by side on the shores of the Italian lakes on their honeymoon. It had only been that very afternoon that May had let him know she returned his feelings, the sacred, proper New York way for a maiden to admit her love, and his imagination was already leaping past the engagement ring, the betrothal kiss, the grand march from *Lohengrin* at their wedding. He did not want his future wife to be a simpleton. With his gentle guidance, he was sure, she would grow to be as witty and socially graceful as the most admired young married women of their set—able to draw playful admiration from other men, but never step a single inch over the line of what was considered correct.

The rest of the men in the club box filtered in one by one, polished, white-waistcoated, every one of them with a gardenia in his buttonhole, and exchanged greetings with Archer. Archer privately knew he was more well-read, more thoughtful, more widely traveled than all of them put together. But as a group, they represented *New York*—and the unspoken code of masculine solidarity made him accept their unwritten rules on all questions of morality, because to strike out on his own would be not just troublesome, but terribly bad form.

“Well, upon my soul!” Lawrence Lefferts exclaimed suddenly, yanking his opera glasses away from the stage. Lefferts was the undisputed highest authority on “form” in all of New York, the man every single bachelor came to consult on whether you wore black tie or patent leather Oxfords or pumps for a given event, a man so naturally elegant he could wear the finest tailored clothes like he had thrown them on half-asleep. He silently passed his glasses over to old Sillerton Jackson.

Archer followed the direction of their gaze, and realized what had caused the outburst: a new figure had stepped into the Mingott box. She was a slim young woman, a little shorter than May, with dark brown hair curled tight against her temples and held back by a thin band of diamonds, that chic “Josephine” look all the fashion magazines had been whispering of. Her gown was deep dark blue velvet, dramatically gathered up under her bust with a heavy old-fashioned clasp, the kind of bold, theatrical design no respectable young matron would dare wear in public. She seemed completely unbothered by all the eyes fixed on her, pausing in the middle of the box to chat gently with Mrs. Welland about whether she should take the front right seat, before smiling softly and settling down next to Mrs. Lovell Mingott.

Old Sillerton Jackson was New York’s other great living authority: he knew every branch of every family tree, every hidden scandal that had smoldered under the city’s polished, polite surface for fifty years. He could explain the exact tangled cousinship between the Mingotts and the South Carolina Dallases, and the line between the Philadelphia Thorleys and the Albany Chiverses (never to be confused with the Manson Chiverses of University Place), he could recite the family traits everyone preferred to hush up: the legendary stinginess of the Long Island Leffertses, the Rushworth family’s fatal habit of making terrible impulsive matches, the streak of insanity that popped up every second generation in the Albany Chiverses. He was the only man alive who could tell you who the mysterious wealthy banker Julius Beaufort really was, and what had become of old Mrs. Mingott’s father Bob Spicer, who had vanished with a huge sum of trust money the very same day a famous Spanish dancer sailed out of the Battery for Cuba.

The whole club box went quiet, waiting for his verdict. He twisted his silvery moustache thoughtfully, blinked at the new arrival through his filmy old blue eyes, and spoke in a soft, deliberate tone that every man there heard perfectly clearly.

“I didn’t think the Mingotts would have tried it on.”

Newland Archer had been twisted into a strange, hot, flustered state of embarrassment by the scene playing out before him at the opera. It was infuriating that the box drawing every unapologetic stare from New York’s entire masculine elite was the one holding his betrothed May Welland, seated primly between her mother and aunt. For a long, confused moment he couldn’t place the lady in the soft, clinging Empire gown at the center of the fuss, nor work out why her presence had every seasoned society regular whispering behind their gloves. Then realization dawned, and a hot rush of indignation pricked at his cheeks. No, really—no one would have imagined the Mingotts would dare pull this stunt!

But they had. There was no denying it; the low, knowing murmurs rippling behind him left no room for doubt: the young woman was May’s cousin, the one the family only ever called “poor Ellen Olenska.” Archer knew she’d slipped back into New York from Europe just a day or two prior, had even heard from May (who hadn’t sounded the least bit disapproving) that she’d stopped by to visit the bereaved, ill-fated countess where she was staying with old Mrs. Manson Mingott.

Archer had always stood fully by the idea of family solidarity, and one of the traits he most adored about the Mingott clan was their stubborn, unapologetic defense of the very few black sheep their otherwise spotless bloodline had ever produced. There was nothing petty or mean-spirited in the young man’s heart, and he was glad his future wife would never be held back by silly, false prudery from showing kindness, in private, to her unhappy cousin. But welcoming Countess Olenska into a quiet family drawing room was one thing. Parading her out in public, of all places at the Opera, and in the very same box as May—whose engagement to him was due to be formally announced in a matter of weeks? That was crossing every unspoken line Fifth Avenue had ever drawn. He felt exactly as old Sillerton Jackson did: he never thought the Mingotts would have dared try it.

Of course he knew that whatever man dared to do within the unwritten laws of Fifth Avenue, old Catherine Manson Mingott, the clan’s fierce matriarch, dared first. He’d long admired the towering, unyielding old woman: she’d started life as plain Catherine Spicer of Staten Island, with a father whose reputation was shadowed by scandal, no money, no connections to make the world forget her humble roots, and still she’d married the heir to the vast Mingott fortune, wed two of her daughters to “foreigners” (an Italian marquis and a wealthy English banker), and topped every one of her audacious feats by building a sprawling pale cream stone house in what was then an uninhabited wilderness right next to Central Park—at a time when brown sandstone was as universal a home choice for respectable families as a frock coat for afternoon calls.

Old Mrs. Mingott’s foreign-raised daughters had become local legend. They never came home to visit, and their mother, like many sharp-witted, strong-willed people, was already too sedentary and stout to travel far, so she’d stayed put at home, unapologetic. That cream house, supposedly modeled on the private aristocratic hotels of Paris, stood as a public monument to her unshakable nerve. She held court inside it, surrounded by pre-Revolutionary antiques and trinkets from Louis Napoleon’s Tuileries (where she’d shone in her middle age), as placid as if there was nothing odd at all about living above 34th Street, or having French windows that swung open like doors instead of the stuffy old sash panes everyone else used.

Everyone, including Sillerton Jackson, agreed old Catherine had never been conventionally beautiful—a gift New York society believed justified every success, and excused a whole host of small failings. Crueler gossips said she’d carved out her success the way her namesake the Russian empress had: by sheer force of will, a hardiness of heart, and a kind of haughty, unashamed effrontery that was somehow made acceptable by the spotless decency of her private life. Her husband Manson Mingott had died when she was only twenty-eight, and had tied up his fortune tight with extra clauses born of the whole family’s inherent distrust of the Spicer line, but his bold young widow had moved through the world fearlessly anyway: mixed freely in high European society, married her daughters off in glamorous, slightly disreputable foreign circles, dined with dukes and ambassadors, counted opera singers and Madame Taglioni herself as close friends, and through it all, as Sillerton Jackson was the first to point out, there had never been a single whisper against her own reputation. The only way, he never failed to add, that she differed from the original Catherine the Great.

Old Mrs. Mingott had long since untied the strictures on her husband’s fortune, and lived in easy affluence for half a century, but the sting of her early poverty had made her stubbornly thrifty. Though every dress and piece of furniture she bought was of the finest possible quality, she could never bring herself to spend much on the fleeting, trivial pleasures of a good meal. For entirely different reasons than Archer’s own mother, her table was famously poor, and her cellar wines even worse. Her relatives complained that the stingy, pre-made dishes she served discredited the Mingott name, which had always been synonymous with generous, luxurious hospitality, but people still flocked to her parties anyway, no matter the flat champagne. When her son Lovell, who kept the best chef in New York to salvage the family name, remonstrated with her over it, she’d only laugh and say “What’s the use of two good cooks in one family, now that I’ve married the girls off and can’t even eat rich sauces anymore?”

Lost in these thoughts, Archer lifted his eyes back to the Mingott box. He saw that Mrs. Welland and her sister-in-law were facing their semicircle of staring, judgmental onlookers with that signature Mingott poise old Catherine had drilled into every member of the family, and only May Welland showed a flush high on her cheeks (likely because she knew Archer was watching her) that betrayed she understood exactly how high the stakes were. As for the cause of all the commotion: Ellen Olenska sat gracefully in the far corner of the box, her eyes fixed intently on the stage, leaning forward just far enough to show a little more bare shoulder and décolletage than respectable New York ladies ever dared to wear, especially women who supposedly had good reason to keep a low profile.

To Archer, few sins felt more unforgivable than an offense against Taste—that distant, untouchable divinity for whom Form was the only visible, unchallenged representative. Countess Olenska’s pale, serious face struck him as perfectly suited to the moment, suited to her sad, unhappy history, but the way her dress, unadorned by any lacy neck tucker, sloped away from her thin shoulders shocked him, unsettled him. He hated the thought of his sweet, unworldly May being exposed to the influence of a young woman so careless of society’s unspoken rules.

“After all,” he heard one of the younger men behind him begin—everyone always talked noisily through the Mephistopheles and Martha scenes of the opera—“what actually happened?”
“She left him, no one’s pretending that’s not true,” someone replied.
“He’s an absolute brute, isn’t he?” the young inquirer pressed, a earnest boy named Thorley who was already gearing up to leap to the countess’s defense.
“The worst sort,” Lawrence Lefferts cut in, with his usual authority on all matters of scandal and taste. “I knew him in Nice. Half-paralyzed, white, sneering creature—handsome enough head, but eyes with a ridiculous amount of dark lashes. Let me tell you what he’s like: when he wasn’t chasing women, he was hoarding fancy china. Paid absurd prices for both, from what I hear.”
A ripple of laughter went around the group, and young Thorley pushed on: “Well, then—?”
“Well then, she ran off with his secretary.”
“Oh.” The young would-be champion’s face fell.
“It didn’t last long, though. A few months later she was found living all alone, half-starved in Venice. Lovell Mingott went over to bring her home. Said she was utterly miserable. That’s all fine and good, but parading her out at the Opera? That’s something else entirely.”
“Maybe,” Thorley ventured, flushing bright red, “she’s too unhappy to be stuck alone at home.”
The line was met with a burst of irreverent snickering, and the boy blushed even harder, trying to look like he’d meant to imply some racy double entendre rather than simple sympathy.
“Well, it’s odd they brought Miss Welland along, no matter what,” someone muttered, with a pointed side-glance straight at Archer.
“Oh, that’s all part of the plan. Granny’s orders, no doubt,” Lefferts laughed. “When the old lady sets her mind to something, she sees it through to the end.”

The act drew to a close, and the whole opera house stirred. Suddenly Archer felt a wild, unshakable impulse to act. The urge to be the very first man to cross the threshold of the Mingott box, to announce to every staring gossip in the room that he stood firmly on the family’s side, to make their engagement public before the gossips could taint it further, to stand beside May and help her weather whatever awkwardness her cousin’s odd, anomalous position might drag her into: that impulse swept away every last one of his scruples and hesitations. He was hurrying down the plush red opera house corridors before he even thought twice, heading for the far side of the auditorium.

The moment he stepped into the box his eyes locked with May’s, and he saw instantly that she understood exactly why he’d come—even if the fierce family dignity they both considered a core virtue kept her from saying a single word out loud. Everyone in their world lived surrounded by faint, half-spoken implications and pale, delicate courtesies, and the quiet, wordless understanding that passed between them felt to Archer far more intimate than any grand, flowery declaration ever could. Her eyes said *You see exactly why Mamma brought me here*, and his answered *I would never, for anything in the world, have let you face this alone*.

“You know my niece, Countess Olenska?” Mrs. Welland asked as she shook hands with her future son-in-law. Archer bowed, as custom dictated for a new introduction to a lady, without offering his hand, and Ellen Olenska tilted her head in a small, quiet nod, her pale gloved hands clasped tight around a massive fan made of eagle feathers. After greeting Mrs. Lovell Mingott, the large, blonde matron whose heavy satin dress creaked softly when she moved, Archer sank down into the seat beside his betrothed, and leaned in to speak low in her ear.
“I hope you’ve told Madame Olenska that we’re engaged? I want everyone to know. I want you to let me announce it formally tonight, at the ball after the opera.”

May Welland’s face flushed pink as dawn breaking over the park, and she looked up at him with radiant, sparkling eyes. “If you can persuade Mamma,” she said, soft and bright. “But why should we change the plans we already settled?”
He didn’t answer with words, only with the look he gave her, and she smiled, more confident now, and added: “Go tell my cousin yourself. I give you leave. She says she used to play with you, when you were both very small children.”

She pushed her chair back to make space for him, and Archer, deliberately, a little ostentatiously, making sure every single person in the surrounding boxes saw exactly what he was doing, sat down right beside Countess Olenska.
“We did use to play together, didn’t we?” she asked, turning her grave, soft eyes up to meet his. “You were a horrid little boy, who once kissed me once behind a drawing room door. But it was your cousin Vandie Newland, who never once even looked at me, that I was truly in love with back then.” Her gaze drifted out, sweeping the whole horseshoe curve of boxes packed with New York’s elite. “Ah, how this place brings it all flooding back. I keep seeing every last one of them as small children, in knickerbockers and pantalettes,” she said, in that soft, slightly lilted accent she’d picked up living abroad, her eyes drifting back to his face.

Pleasant as her expression was, Archer was shocked that she could paint such an unseemly, flippant picture of the august, unforgiving society tribunal that was judging her every move at that very moment. Nothing could be worse taste than that kind of misplaced, careless lightness. He answered a little stiffly: “Yes. You have been away a very long time.”
“Oh, centuries and centuries. So long,” she said, “that I am quite sure I am dead and buried, and this dear, familiar old place is heaven.” For reasons he could not name, the line struck Newland Archer as an even more shockingly irreverent way to describe the stuffy, rule-bound New York society he’d lived in his whole life.

The ritual unfurled exactly as it did every single year. Mrs. Julius Beaufort never missed a single minute of the opera on the night of her annual ball—indeed, she deliberately scheduled the party on an opera night just to flaunt that she was entirely above fretting over household details, and that her staff of highly competent servants could pull off every last element of the entertainment without her ever stepping foot at home ahead of time.

The Beaufort brownstone was one of the rare houses in old New York that boasted a purpose-built ballroom, older even than the ones at Mrs. Manson Mingott’s and the Headly Chiverses’ estates. Back when most people still considered it merely “provincial” to lay a cheap crash mat over the drawing room floors and haul all the furniture upstairs for a party, the Beauforts’ permanent, unused ballroom was a mark of unassailable status: it sat shuttered 364 days a year, gilt chairs stacked in one corner, its grand crystal chandelier wrapped in linen storage bag, and that quiet, unarguable luxury was enough to make society overlook every last unseemly detail of Beaufort’s murky past.

Mrs. Archer, who loved turning her social philosophies into pithy little axioms, had once declared “We all have our pet common people we tuck away” — and daring as the line was, every exclusive drawing room in the city secretly agreed it rang true. But the Beauforts weren’t just common; more than one person whispered they were far worse. Mrs. Beaufort had been born Regina Dallas, a penniless exquisite beauty from one of South Carolina’s most respected old families, introduced to New York society by her well-meaning but perpetually impulsive cousin Medora Manson. If you were related to the Mansons and the Rushworths, you earned what Sillerton Jackson (who’d once frequented the old Tuileries court) called *droit de cité* — automatic, unchallenged entry to New York high society. But everyone wondered if Regina had forfeited that right the second she married Julius Beaufort.

No one was quite sure who Beaufort actually was. He passed for English: handsome, quick to laugh, sharp-tongued, wildly hospitable, and brilliant. He’d arrived in America with letters of recommendation from old Mrs. Manson Mingott’s English banker son-in-law, and had shot straight to the top of the city’s business circles in no time. But his habits were dissipated, his wit too cutting for stuffy old sensibilities, his origins completely unaccounted for, and when Medora Manson announced the engagement, everyone just sighed and added it to her long list of impulsive mistakes.

Folly works out as often as wisdom, though. Two years after the wedding, everyone agreed the young Mrs. Beaufort ran the most distinguished, sought-after household in New York. No one could work out how she pulled it off. She was quiet, almost indolent, the sharpest gossips even called her dull — but dressed like a marble idol, draped in pearls, growing blonder, softer and more beautiful with every passing year, she held court in her husband’s heavy brownstone palace and drew every notable name in the city to her doorstep without ever lifting a jeweled little finger. The people in the know whispered it was all Beaufort behind the scenes: training the servants, teaching the chef brand new recipes, telling the gardeners exactly what rare hothouse blooms to grow for the tables, picking the guest list, stirring the after-dinner punch, even dictating the short little notes his wife sent her friends. But he kept all that domestic work completely private, and presented to the world the air of a careless, wealthy millionaire strolling into his own drawing room like a casual invited guest, saying, “My wife’s gloxinias are a marvel, aren’t they? I believe she gets them sent over from Kew.”

Everyone agreed Beaufort’s whole magic was the way he could carry off absolutely anything. Even the persistent rumor that the international bank he’d worked for back in England had “helped him leave the country” rolled right off his back — and New York was every bit as strict about business morals as it was about social ones. He charmed his way past every whisper, won over the whole of New York high society, and for over twenty years people had said they were “going over to the Beauforts’” with the same unshakable confidence they said they were visiting old Mrs. Manson Mingott’s, plus the added lovely certainty that they’d get plump hot canvasback ducks and rare vintage wine, not tepid unlabeled Veuve Clicquot and stale warmed-up croquettes shipped up from Philadelphia.

As usual, Mrs. Beaufort stepped into her opera box right before the Jewel Song, and when she stood up at the end of the third act, wrapped her opera cloak around her lovely bare shoulders, and slipped out, every New Yorker in the opera house knew the annual ball would begin half an hour later. The Beaufort house was the pride of the city, the one everyone dragged their foreign guests to tour, especially on ball night. The Beauforts had been the first in New York to own their own red velvet carpet, rolled down the front steps by their own liveried footmen under their own custom awning, no need to rent it along with the supper and ballroom chairs. They’d also started the revolutionary custom of letting ladies leave their cloaks in the front hall, instead of tramping all the way up to the hostess’s bedroom to re-curl their hair over a gas jet — Beaufort had joked once that he assumed all his wife’s friends had maids to make sure their coiffures were perfect before they left home. The whole house had been deliberately laid out with the ballroom at the end of a long vista of connected drawing rooms: sea-green, crimson, and gold bouton d’or, so guests could walk solemnly down their length, see the hundreds of candlelit chandeliers glinting off the polished parquet floors, and past that the conservatory, where camellias and tall tree ferns arched their pricey fronds over black and gold bamboo seats.

Newland Archer drifted in later than most, as befitted a young man of his social standing. He handed his overcoat to a footman in pristine silk stockings (the stockings were Beaufort’s one silly, beloved indulgence), lingered a while in the library lined with Spanish leather and furnished with Buhl antiques and malachite pieces, where a handful of other men chatted and pulled on their dancing gloves, before he joined the line of guests waiting to greet Mrs. Beaufort at the crimson drawing room threshold.

He was distinctly nervous. He hadn’t gone back to his club after the opera like most young bachelors usually did; the night was mild, so he’d walked a good stretch up Fifth Avenue first before turning back toward the Beauforts’ house. He was terrified the Mingotts were pushing things too far — that Granny Mingott had ordered them to bring Countess Olenska right to the ball. From the way everyone in the club box had reacted back at the opera, he knew that would be a catastrophic social misstep. Even though he was more determined than ever to stand by his promise to support his betrothed’s cousin, he no longer felt that bright, chivalrous eagerness he’d had before their short conversation at the opera.

Wandering into the gold bouton d’or drawing room, where Beaufort had shockingly hung that controversial Bouguereau nude *Love Victorious*, Archer spotted Mrs. Welland and her daughter standing right by the ballroom doors. Couples were already gliding across the floor past them, soft candlelight catching the swirling tulle skirts, the circlets of small white blossoms on the young girls’ heads, the sparkling aigrettes and jewels in the young married women’s updos, the crisp glitter of starched shirt fronts and brand new white glacé gloves.

Miss Welland, her hand full of white lilies of the valley (she carried no other bouquet), was clearly about to join the dancers, but lingered on the threshold. Her cheeks were a little pale, her eyes bright with soft, unguarded excitement. A little circle of young men and girls had gathered around her, laughing, clasping hands, trading light pleasantries, and a little distance away Mrs. Welland watched them, beaming with the carefully calibrated air of mild parental reluctance that was considered perfectly proper for the occasion. It was obvious they were announcing their engagement right then, and Archer had been the one to insist they do it — but this was not at all how he’d imagined his happiest news being shared. Proclaiming it in the noise and crush of a packed ballroom stripped it of that soft, private bloom that ought to wrap around the most precious things in a person’s life. His joy was so deep that this small public blur didn’t touch its core, but he still ached to keep that surface sweetness untouched. To his relief, he saw May Welland felt the exact same way. Her eyes flew to his, beseeching, as if to say *We’re only doing this because it’s the right thing*.

No plea could have reached him faster. But he wished there was some grand, noble reason they had to rush this announcement, not just the messy, awkward business of poor Ellen Olenska’s unspoken scandal. The crowd around May stepped aside for him with knowing, warm smiles, and after he accepted all their congratulations, he drew his betrothed right out onto the ballroom floor, settled his arm around her waist, and they drifted away to the soft, swelling waltz of *The Blue Danube*.

She didn’t say a word at first, just smiled, her lips trembling a little, her eyes soft and faraway, as if she was looking at some perfect, unnameable vision just out of sight. “My dearest,” he whispered, pressing her closer. For the first time it hit him: even in the middle of a crowded ballroom, the first hours of being betrothed felt grave, almost sacramental. What a beautiful new life stretched out ahead of him, with this bright, pure, gentle goodness at his side.

When the dance ended, the two of them, as was proper for an engaged couple, wandered off into the conservatory. They sat behind a thick screen of tree ferns and camellias, and Newland lifted her gloved hand to his lips.
“I told you I’d do what you asked,” May said.
“Yes, I couldn’t bear to wait any longer,” he laughed softly. After a beat, he added, “I just wish we didn’t have to make the announcement in the middle of a ball, that’s all.”
“I know,” she said, meeting his gaze perfectly, as if she understood every single thought in his head. “But after all — even here, we’re alone together, aren’t we?”
“Oh my love, we always will be,” he swore.

It was clear she was always going to get it, always say exactly the right thing. The thought made his cup of bliss overflow, and he said gaily, “The worst part is I want to kiss you properly, and I can’t.” He glanced quickly around the conservatory to make sure no one was watching, pulled her close, and pressed a soft, fleeting kiss to her lips. To make up for that bold, unseemly little moment, he led her to a bamboo sofa in a less shadowed part of the room, sat down beside her, and plucked one small lily of the valley from her bouquet. She sat quiet, and the whole world outside felt like a sunlit, perfect valley stretched out at their feet.

“Did you tell my cousin Ellen about our engagement?” she asked after a little while, as if she was speaking half in a dream.

Archer jolted back to reality. He hadn’t. Some strange, unshakable reluctance to talk about something so intimate to that odd, foreign, out-of-place woman had frozen the words on his tongue before he could say them.
“No — I never got the chance, after all,” he lied quickly.
Her face fell a little, but she was gentle and determined to make him understand. “Then you have to tell her. I didn’t get a chance to tell her either, and I’d hate for her to think —”
“Of course not. But shouldn’t you be the one to tell her?”
She thought about it for a moment. “If I’d done it right away, yes. But now that there’s been a delay, you have to explain that I asked you to tell her at the opera before we told everyone else here. Otherwise she might think I forgot her. She’s family, you know, and she’s been gone for so long, she’s very sensitive.”

Archer looked at her, glowing with adoration. “You are my sweet, perfect angel. Of course I’ll tell her.” He glanced a little nervously toward the packed ballroom. “But I haven’t seen her anywhere. Did she come?”
“No, at the very last minute she decided not to,” May said.
“At the last minute?” he echoed, surprised that she’d ever even considered coming.
“She loves dancing so much,” the girl said simply. “But all of a sudden she decided her dress wasn’t nice enough for the ball, even though we all thought it was lovely. So my aunt took her home instead.”
“Oh, well,” Archer said, completely happy and unbothered.

Nothing about his new betrothed delighted him more than the way she was fully committed to the quiet ritual of pretending all unpleasant things simply didn’t exist, the same ritual they’d both been raised on. She knew perfectly well why Ellen Olenska had stayed away, he thought. But he would never, ever let the smallest sign cross his face that he was even aware there was the faintest shadow hovering over poor Ellen’s reputation.

The very next day saw the first of New York’s rigidly prescribed betrothal visits unfold, every step scripted by generations of unspoken social rule. Newland Archer accompanied his mother and sister first to call on the Welland household, before the three of them—Newland, Mrs. Welland, and his bright, blushing fiancée May—climbed into a closed carriage to drive out to old Mrs. Manson Mingott’s home, to receive the venerable matriarch’s official blessing on their match.

For Newland, a call on Mrs. Mingott was always a delightfully unorthodox treat. The house itself was a living, breathing oddity in the staid city, not nearly as ancient as the somber 1830s family homes clustered on University Place and lower Fifth Avenue, with their heavy cabbage-rose garlanded carpets, dark rosewood consoles, black marble mantels, and towering mahogany glazed bookcases. Old Mrs. Mingott had built her home later, and with characteristic boldness had cast out all the heavy, respectable furniture of her youth, weaving Mingott family heirlooms through the gilded, frilly, frivolous upholstery of France’s Second Empire. She made a habit of sitting in her ground-floor parlor window, calm as a queen watching the life and bustle of the city creep slowly north toward her isolated doorstep. She was in no rush at all: she knew the hoardings, the stone quarries, the rickety one-story saloons, the ragged wooden greenhouses, and the goat-dotted rocky lots all around her would vanish soon enough, replaced by stately homes just as grand as hers, and the rutted cobblestones the old omnibuses rattled over would one day be smooth asphalt, just like the streets of Paris she’d heard travelers gush about. Everyone worth knowing came to her, after all; she filled her drawing rooms as easily as the notoriously lavish Beaufort couple, without adding a single extra dish to her supper menu, so her geographic isolation never bothered her at all.

The massive weight that had settled on her in middle age, like a lava flow swallowing a small city, had transformed her from a plump, lively little woman with neat small feet into something vast and august, a force of nature all her own. She’d accepted the change with the same quiet philosophy she brought to every trial, and in her extreme old age, her reward was a nearly unwrinkled expanse of firm pink-and-white skin, with the small features of her younger self peeking out from the center like a relic waiting to be unearthed. A flight of smooth double chins led down to a still-snowy bosom draped in fine muslin, pinned fast by a miniature portrait of the late Mr. Mingott, while wave after wave of black silk dress billowed out over the edges of her oversized armchair, two tiny white hands poised on top of the fabric like gulls drifting over the surface of the tide. Her weight had long since made climbing stairs impossible, so she’d done something unthinkable to every upstanding New Yorker: she’d moved all her reception rooms downstairs, and set up her own private bedroom on the ground floor too. Sitting with her in the parlor window, you could catch a clear glimpse through an always-open door and a looped-back yellow damask portière of her bedroom: a huge, low bed upholstered to look like a sofa, a vanity table draped in frilly lace, set with a gilded mirror. To her visitors, the arrangement felt shockingly foreign, straight out of a French romance novel, the kind of decadent one-floor apartments lovers hid in in the old wicked European capitals. Newland, who had always imagined the steamy love scenes of his favorite forbidden novel *Monsieur de Camors* playing out in exactly that kind of room, often smiled to think of blameless, iron-willed old Mrs. Mingott living her perfectly respectable life on a stage set built for adultery. He had no doubt, if a lover had ever been what she wanted, that intrepid old woman would have had him, conventions be damned.

To the whole family’s quiet relief, the Countess Olenska was nowhere to be seen in the parlor when they arrived. Mrs. Mingott said she’d gone out, a fact that raised a few quiet eyebrows—on such a bright, sunny day, in the thick of shopping hour, for a woman with her unhappy, compromised past, it felt just a touch unseemly. But it spared them all the awkwardness of her presence, and the faint shadow her messy history might cast over May and Newland’s glowing, perfect future.

The visit unfolded smoothly, as everyone expected it would. Old Mrs. Mingott was over the moon about the engagement; the match had been foreseen by watchful relatives for years, and carefully approved of in a private family council long before Newland had even worked up the courage to propose. The engagement ring, a big thick sapphire set in nearly invisible claws, won her full, unqualified admiration.
“It’s the new setting,” Mrs. Welland had explained, with a conciliatory little side-glance at her future son-in-law. “It shows the stone beautifully, of course, but it looks just a little bare to old-fashioned eyes.”
“Old-fashioned eyes? Don’t you dare mean mine, my dear! I adore every new novelty,” the matriarch laughed, lifting the sapphire up to her small, sharp eyes, which no pair of spectacles had ever touched. “Very handsome. Very generous. Back in my day, a cameo set in pearls was thought more than enough. But it’s the hand that makes the ring shine, isn’t it, my dear Mr. Archer?” she teased, waving one of her tiny hands, nails pointed and neat, rolls of aged fat circling her wrist like carved ivory bracelets. “My own hand was modelled in Rome by the great Ferrigiani. You ought to have May’s done too, no doubt he’ll see to that, my sweet. Her hands are a little large, what with all these modern outdoor sports that spread the joints, but her skin is perfectly white.” She broke off, fixing her sharp gaze straight on Archer’s face. “And when’s the wedding?”
“Oh—” Mrs. Welland fumbled, before Newland, smiling soft at his fiancée, answered: “As soon as it can possibly be, if you’ll back me up on it, Mrs. Mingott.”
“We must give them time to get to know each other a little better, mamma,” Mrs. Welland interposed, putting on the proper, demure show of reluctance.
“Know each other? Fiddlesticks!” the old matriarch scoffed. “Everyone in New York has known everyone else since they were toddling around in pinafores. Let the young man have his way, my dear—don’t wait till the bubble’s off the champagne. Marry them before Lent, I say. I could catch pneumonia any winter now, and I fully intend to be the one to host their wedding breakfast.”
The whole group laughed, murmured the proper words of amusement, gratitude, and gentle disbelief, and the visit was about to break up on that soft, merry note when the front door swung open. There stood the Countess Olenska, still in her bonnet and fur-lined mantle, and right behind her, the unexpected figure of Julius Beaufort.
The ladies exchanged soft, cousinly murmurs of pleased surprise, and Mrs. Mingott waved the banker over to her. “Ha, Beaufort! This is a rare treat. I wish you’d drop by far more often,” she said, addressing all men by their surnames in that odd, foreign way of hers.
“Thank you. I wish I could make the time,” he replied in his easy, arrogant drawl. “I’m usually far too tied up with business, but I ran into the Countess Ellen in Madison Square, and she was kind enough to let me walk home with her.”
“Ah! Now that Ellen’s here, the house will be far gayer!” Mrs. Mingott declared, unashamed. “Sit down—sit down, Beaufort, pull that yellow armchair over. Now that I’ve got you here, I want a good gossip. I hear your ball last week was magnificent. And I hear you invited Mrs. Lemuel Struthers? Well, I’ve a mind to meet that woman myself.” She’d already completely forgotten her other guests, who were drifting slowly out into the hall under Ellen Olenska’s guidance. Old Mrs. Mingott had always admired Julius Beaufort; they shared the same cool, domineering edge, the same habit of cutting straight through stuffy convention to get what they wanted. She was eager to hear exactly what had convinced the Beauforts to extend the first invitation to Mrs. Struthers, the widow who’d made her fortune selling shoe polish, and returned from a years-long stay in Europe determined to claw her way inside New York’s tight little social citadel. “Of course if you and Regina invite her, the thing is settled. We need new blood and new money around here—and I hear she’s still very good-looking,” the carnivorous old matriarch declared.
In the hall, while Mrs. Welland and May were slipping their fur gloves and cloaks on, Archer caught Countess Olenska looking at him with a faint, questioning smile.
“Of course you already know, about May and me,” he said, answering her look with a shy laugh. “She scolded me for not telling you the news last night at the Opera—she’d given me strict orders to break the news to you then, but I couldn’t, not in that heaving, noisy crowd.”
The smile spread from her eyes to her lips, and suddenly she looked years younger, exactly like the bold, brown-eyed Ellen Mingott he’d run wild with as a boy. “Of course I know. And I’m so very glad. You don’t tell news that sweet in the middle of a crowd, after all.” The other two ladies were already at the front step, and she held out her gloved hand to him. “Goodbye. Come and see me some day soon,” she said, her eyes still fixed on his.
Settled back in the carriage rumbling down Fifth Avenue, they all talked pointedly of nothing but Mrs. Mingott: her incredible age, her sharp spirit, all her wonderful, unorthodox little quirks. Not a single person said the name Ellen Olenska out loud. But Archer knew Mrs. Welland was turning it over in her head, thinking what a terrible impression it made, for a woman with Ellen’s past to be seen parading up Fifth Avenue at the busiest shopping hour on the arm of Julius Beaufort. And Newland found himself agreeing, a little: she ought to know that a man newly engaged has no business paying calls on married women, no matter how distant the family tie. But no doubt in the wild, unorthodox European circles she’d lived in for so long, that sort of thing was perfectly normal, they did nothing else all day. Even with all his proudly cosmopolitan, liberal views, he found himself thanking heaven he was a New Yorker, and soon to be allied for life to one of his own gentle, familiar kind.

The next evening, old Mr. Sillerton Jackson arrived for dinner at the Archers’ quiet West Twenty-eighth Street home.

Mrs. Archer, a shy widow who shrank from most large gatherings, lived for the quiet thrill of knowing every small, unspoken detail of New York society’s doings. Her old friend Jackson pursued the tangled affairs of their shared acquaintance with the patience of a rare artifact collector and the precise eye of a naturalist cataloguing new species, while his unmarried sister Miss Sophy—invited to every dinner that couldn’t secure her far more sought-after brother—trotted home with scraps of minor gossip that filled every tiny gap in his meticulously curated store of lore. So whenever a story was stirring that Mrs. Archer ached to unpack, she called Jackson to dine; and since she extended those invitations to precious few, and she and her spinster daughter Janey made such rapt, unchallenging audiences, Jackson almost always came himself instead of sending his sister along in his place.

If he could have dictated every condition of the visit, he would have chosen nights when Newland was out of the house. Not that the young man was unpleasant company—the two got on famously at their club, after all—but the veteran anecdotist had long noticed that Newland had a tiresome habit of weighing and questioning his tales, a critical streak the two Archer ladies never showed. If perfection were possible on earth, Jackson would also have asked for Mrs. Archer’s cooking to be a little less bland. But every old New Yorker knew the great fundamental divide stretching back as far as anyone could remember: on one side were the Mingotts, Mansons, and all their sprawling clan, who lavished care on fine food, elegant clothes, and ample fortunes; on the other was the Archer-Newland-van-der-Luyden tribe, devoted to travel, horticulture, and the finest contemporary fiction, who looked down on all gross, bodily forms of pleasure as rather vulgar. You couldn’t have everything, after all. Dine with Lovell Mingott, and you got canvasback duck, terrapin, and decades-old vintage wine; dine with Adeline Archer, and you could debate Alpine scenery and *The Marble Faun* for hours—and luckily, the Archer family’s prized Madeira had rounded the Cape, so even the worst meal was not entirely without a saving grace. So whenever Mrs. Archer’s friendly summons arrived, the eclectic Jackson would shrug and tell his sister, “I’ve had a touch of gout since my last dinner at the Mingotts’—a light diet at Adeline’s will do me a world of good.”

Mrs. Archer, Janey, and Newland divided their home gently: Newland claimed the entire upper floor for his own use, while the two women tucked themselves into cozy, compact quarters on the ground level. Their shared tastes and interests wrapped the house in unclouded harmony: they grew ferns in glass Wardian cases, twisted macramé lace, stitched wool embroidery on linen, collected Revolutionary-era glazed pottery, subscribed to the wholesome magazine *Good Words*, and read Ouida’s novels purely for the lush Italian atmosphere. (They favored her stories of peasant life, for the sweeping scenery and soft, sweet sentiments, though they preferred novels set among their own social set, whose motives and habits felt far more comprehensible. They spoke severely of Dickens, who they insisted “had never drawn a proper gentleman,” and thought Thackeray was far less at home in high society than Bulwer-Lytton, even if the latter was beginning to be dismissed as old-fashioned.)

Mother and daughter were both great lovers of landscape, the first thing they sought out and admired on their rare travels abroad; they considered architecture and painting proper subjects for men, and only for the learned sort who pored over Ruskin’s writings. Born a Newland herself, Mrs. Archer and Janey looked so much alike people often mistook them for sisters: tall, pale, slightly round-shouldered, with long elegant noses, soft sweet smiles, and a kind of drooping, faded distinction straight out of a Reynolds portrait. Their resemblance would have been total if not for the soft embonpoint of middle age that stretched Mrs. Archer’s black brocade gowns, while Janey’s brown and purple poplin dresses hung looser and looser on her unmarried frame as the years passed.

Mentally, though, they were less alike than their shared little mannerisms suggested. Their long, mutually dependent life together had given them the exact same vocabulary, and the same habit of prefacing any new opinion with “Mother thinks” or “Janey thinks,” depending on which of them was voicing the thought. But where Mrs. Archer’s serene, unimaginative mind rested easy on all the accepted, familiar rules of their little world, Janey was prone to sudden, wild bursts of fancy welling up from long-suppressed springs of romance. They adored each other, and revered Newland entirely, and he loved them back with a tender, apologetic fondness softened by their exaggerated admiration for him, and his own quiet secret satisfaction in being the head of their little household. He thought it a very good thing for a man to have his authority respected in his own home, even if his sense of humor sometimes made him question how much real power he actually wielded.

That evening, Newland was perfectly sure Jackson would have preferred him out at the club. But he had his own reasons for staying home. Of course old Jackson wanted to talk about Ellen Olenska. Of course Mrs. Archer and Janey were burning to hear every bit of gossip he had. All three of them would be faintly awkward with Newland there, now that his engagement to May Welland had made him an official relative of the Mingott clan, and he waited, amused and curious, to see how they would dance around the subject.

They began obliquely, talking of Mrs. Lemuel Struthers.
“It’s such a pity the Beauforts asked her to their ball,” Mrs. Archer said gently. “But Regina always does exactly what he tells her, and Beaufort—”
“Certain nuances escape Beaufort,” Jackson said, poking cautiously at his broiled shad, wondering for the thousandth time why Mrs. Archer’s cook always burned the roe to a cinder. Newland, who had long shared that exact wonder, could spot the familiar melancholy disapproval in his face.
“Oh, he’s vulgar, by definition,” Mrs. Archer said. “My grandfather Newland always told my mother, ‘Whatever you do, don’t let that fellow Beaufort near the girls.’ But at least he’s associated with gentlemen, even in England, they say. It’s all so mysterious—” She glanced at Janey and paused. She and Janey knew every fold of the Beaufort backstory, but in front of an unmarried girl, Mrs. Archer always pretended such subjects were not fit for discussion.
“But this Mrs. Struthers,” she continued smoothly. “What did you say her past was, Sillerton?”
“Sprung from a mining camp saloon, to be precise. Then she toured New England with a traveling wax works show, until the police shut that down. After that, they say she lived—” Jackson glanced at Janey now, whose eyes bulged a little under her prominent lids, still eager to fill the remaining gaps in Mrs. Struthers’s story. “Then Lemuel Struthers came along. They say his advertising man used her face for the shoe polish posters, you know—her hair’s that deep black, Egyptian style. Anyhow, eventually he married her.” The space between each syllable of that “eventually” was packed full of unspoken innuendo.
“Oh well, at this point in time, it hardly matters,” Mrs. Archer said indifferently. The ladies were not truly interested in Mrs. Struthers just then—Ellen Olenska was far too fresh, far too absorbing a topic. Mrs. Archer had only named Struthers so she could pivot seamlessly to what they all really wanted to talk about.
“And Newland’s new cousin, Countess Olenska? Was she at the ball too?”
The faint touch of playful sarcasm was obvious, and Newland had expected it. Even Mrs. Archer, who rarely approved of most people’s choices, had been over the moon about his engagement. (“Especially after that silly business with Mrs. Rushworth,” she’d murmured to Janey once, referring to the old affair that had once felt to Newland like a permanent, searing scar on his soul.) There was no better match in all New York than May Welland, no matter how you looked at it. Of course such a marriage was only what Newland was owed—but young men were so foolish and unpredictable, and some women so ensnaring and unscrupulous, that it felt nothing short of a miracle to see her only son safe past the siren isle of reckless passion, anchored in the calm harbor of blameless domesticity.

Newland knew she’d been perturbed, though, by how the engagement had been announced prematurely, all because of the Mingotts’ obsession with shielding Ellen Olenska. It was partly out of tenderness for his mother, the indulgent head of the household, that he’d chosen to stay home that night. “I don’t object to the Mingotts’ fierce family loyalty,” he’d heard her grumble to Janey when they were alone, “but I can’t see why Newland’s engagement has to be mixed up with that Olenska woman’s comings and goings.” She’d behaved perfectly, of course, on their formal call on the Wellands—but Newland had noticed how both she and Janey had nervously scanned every doorway for Ellen Olenska to appear the whole visit, and after they left, she’d allowed herself to murmur, “I’m so thankful Augusta Welland received us alone.”

Newland knew her quiet, lawful little revenge for that small stress would be to draw Jackson out on every last detail about the Countess, and now that he’d done his public duty as the Mingotts’ soon-to-be relative, he had no objection to hearing the lady discussed in private—even if the subject was already starting to bore him a little.

Jackson helped himself to a slice of tepid filet the mournful butler handed him with a look as skeptical as his own, rejected the mushroom sauce after a barely perceptible sniff, and leaned back in his chair, glancing up at the candle-lit portraits of Archers, Newlands, and van der Luydens hung in their heavy dark frames on the walls.
“Ah, your grandfather Archer loved a good dinner, my dear Newland!” he said, nodding at the portrait of the plump young man in a blue coat and starched stock, a white-columned country house visible behind him. “Well, well… I wonder what he would have said to all these foreign marriages!”
Mrs. Archer ignored the jab at her family’s ancestral disregard for fine food. “So she wasn’t at the ball?”
“Ah—” Mrs. Archer murmured, the tone heavy with relief that Ellen had retained at least that much decency.
“Maybe the Beauforts don’t even know her,” Janey suggested, with artless little malice.
Jackson took a faint sip of his wine, as if tasting a vintage Madeira. “Mrs. Beaufort may not. But Beaufort certainly does. Half of New York saw her walking up Fifth Avenue with him this afternoon.”
“Mercy,” Mrs. Archer moaned, clearly giving up on the hope that a foreigner like Ellen would follow the unspoken rules of delicacy.
“I wonder if she wore a round hat or a bonnet in the afternoon,” Janey mused. “At the opera, you know, she had on that dark blue velvet, perfectly plain and flat—just like a nightgown.”
“Janey!” her mother chided, and Janey blushed, trying to look audacious.
“At any rate, it was far better taste not to go to the ball,” Mrs. Archer continued.
A perverse little urge to push back made Newland speak up. “I don’t think it was a matter of taste for her. May said she planned to go, then decided the dress she had wasn’t smart enough.”
Mrs. Archer smiled, relieved that lined up with her own assumption. “Poor Ellen,” she said softly. “We have to remember what an eccentric upbringing Medora Manson gave her. What can you expect of a girl who was allowed to wear black satin to her own coming out ball?”
“Ah, I remember her in that exact dress!” Jackson said. “Poor girl,” he added, in the tone of a man who’d thoroughly enjoyed the spectacle at the time, and knew exactly what it had portended.
“It’s odd,” Janey said. “She kept such an ugly name as Ellen. I would have changed it to Elaine.” She glanced around the table to see how the line landed.
Her brother laughed. “Why Elaine?”
“I don’t know. It sounds more… more Polish,” Janey said, blushing.
“It sounds more conspicuous,” Mrs. Archer said distantly. “That can hardly be what she wants right now.”
“Why not?” Newland broke in, suddenly argumentative. “Why shouldn’t she be conspicuous if she chooses? Why should she slink about as if she’s the one who disgraced herself? She’s poor Ellen, all right—she had the rotten luck to make a terrible marriage. But that’s no reason for her to hide her head like she’s the culprit.”
“I suppose that’s the line the Mingotts have decided to take,” Jackson said speculatively.
Newland reddened. “I didn’t need the Mingotts to tell me that, if that’s what you’re implying, sir. Madame Olenska has lived a very unhappy life. That doesn’t make her an outcast.”
“There are rumors,” Jackson began, glancing pointedly at Janey.
“Oh, I know—the secretary,” Newland cut him off. “Nonsense, Mother, Janey’s grown up. They say, don’t they, that the secretary helped her get away from that brute of a husband who kept her practically a prisoner? Well, what if he did? I hope there isn’t a man among us who wouldn’t have done the exact same thing.”
Jackson turned to the sad butler hovering nearby. “Actually… that sauce, just a little after all,” he said, and once the man had served him, he added casually, “I’m told she’s looking for a house. She’s planning to settle here for good.”
“I hear she’s planning to get a divorce,” Janey said, boldly.
“I hope she will!” Newland exclaimed.

The words landed like a bombshell in the quiet, pure, tranquil air of the Archer dining room. Mrs. Archer lifted her delicate eyebrows in that very specific curve that meant “the butler is still in the room”—and Newland, suddenly mindful of how vulgar it was to discuss such intimate family matters in front of staff, hastily steered the conversation over to his recent visit with old Mrs. Mingott, the famously matriarchal invalid.

After dinner, following their ancient, unchanging custom, Mrs. Archer and Janey dragged their long silk skirts up to the drawing room, where the gentlemen stayed behind to smoke. They sat beside a carved glass Carcel lamp, facing each other across a rosewood worktable with a green silk bag tucked under it, stitching at opposite ends of a tapestry band of wild field flowers meant to adorn an occasional chair in the future drawing room of young Mrs. Newland Archer.

Down in the Gothic library, Newland settled Jackson in an armchair by the fire and handed him one of his own fine cigars. Jackson sank into the chair with deep satisfaction, lit his cigar knowing it would be perfectly good, and stretched his thin, gouty old ankles out toward the flames.
“You say the secretary only helped her escape, my dear fellow? Well, a year later he was still helping her, then. Someone spotted the two of them living together in Lausanne.”
Newland flushed bright red. “Living together? Well, why not? Who on earth has the right to run her life for her, if she hasn’t? I’m sick of this hypocrisy that would bury a woman of her age alive just because her husband chooses to live with his mistresses.” He turned away angrily to light his own cigar. “Women ought to be free—as free as we are,” he declared, making a grand new discovery he was far too riled up to measure the terrible consequences of.

Sillerton Jackson stretched his ankles even closer to the fire, and let out a sardonic little whistle.
“Well,” he said after a pause, “it seems Count Olenski agrees with you. For I’ve never once heard of him lifting a single finger to get his wife back.”

VI. That evening, after Mr. Jackson had taken his leave and the ladies had slipped off to their bedroom draped in soft chintz curtains, Newland Archer climbed slowly up the stairs to his private study, lost in thought. As usual, a careful, attentive hand had kept the hearth crackling and trimmed the lamp wick to a warm, steady glow; the room, lined from floor to ceiling with books, with its two little bronze statuettes of fencers posed on the mantel and its rows of cherished prints of famous old master paintings, felt impossibly soft, familiar, and welcoming.
When he sank into his worn armchair pulled close to the fire, his gaze fell straight to the large framed photograph of May Welland, the one she’d pressed into his hands in the giddy first weeks of their courtship, now set dead center on his side table, no other portraits crowding it out. He stared at it with a new, quiet awe: that bright unguarded forehead, those earnest, serious eyes, that laughing, innocent mouth. He was to be the custodian of that girl’s entire soul, the keeper of every part of her soft untested life. That strange, almost terrifying product of the world he’d always loved and trusted—the young woman who knew nothing of life’s sharper edges and expected only good things—looked back at him like a stranger, even behind May’s beloved, familiar features. It hit him all at once that marriage was never the safe, still harbor he’d been raised to expect. It was a voyage out across completely uncharted seas.
The case of Countess Olenska had stirred up every settled old conviction he’d ever held, sending them drifting wildly, dangerously through his mind. That line he’d blurted out earlier—*“Women should be free, just as free as we are”—*struck at the root of a problem his entire social circle had agreed to pretend did not exist. Good, respectable women, no matter how wronged, would never dare to claim that kind of freedom, and generous-minded men like himself were therefore free to play the chivalrous hero and grandstand about granting it to them, even as all those pretty words did nothing but hide the iron, unyielding conventions that bound everyone tight to the same old patterns. Now he was pledged to defend his betrothed’s cousin for choices that would have him calling down every thunder of Church and State on his own wife, had she made them. Of course, the dilemma was purely hypothetical: he was no cruel, blackguard Polish nobleman, and it was absurd to wonder what his wife’s rights would be if he *were*. But Archer was too imaginative not to see that the bond between him and May could chafe for far quieter, subtler, far less obvious reasons. What did the two of them really know of each other, anyway? It was his duty as a “decent fellow” to hide every scrap of his own past missteps from her, just as it was her lot, as a properly raised marriageable girl, to have no past of any kind to conceal. What if, for some small, quiet reason neither of them could name, they grew tired of each other? Misunderstood each other? Irritated each other in ways they could never speak of?
He ran through his friends’ marriages, the ones everyone called perfectly happy, and saw not a single one that came even close to the tender, passionate comradeship he’d dreamed of sharing with May for the rest of their lives. He realized that dream assumed she had the experience, the flexibility, the freedom of judgment that every one of her trainers—her mother, her aunts, her governesses—had carefully taught her to never, ever possess. A cold shiver of foreboding ran through him: his own marriage could easily turn out to be just like every other one around him: a dull, convenient union of shared social and material interests, held together by her total ignorance of the world and his own careful hypocrisy.
Lawrence Lefferts popped into his head, the high priest of social form who had perfected that exact “ideal” of a marriage. He’d shaped his wife Gertrude so completely to his own convenience that even during the most public, obvious moments of his many affairs with other men’s wives, she wandered around smiling, completely unknowing, telling everyone “Lawrence is so frightfully strict!” She’d even been known to blush indignantly and turn her face away when anyone hinted that Julius Beaufort, that foreigner of murky origins, kept a second household across town.
Archer tried to comfort himself with the thought that he was not nearly such a fool as Larry Lefferts, and May was not nearly such a simpleton as poor Gertrude. But the difference was only a matter of intelligence, not of the unspoken rules they both were raised to follow. In reality, they all lived in a kind of coded, hieroglyphic world, where the real truth was never said, never done, never even thought—only represented by a set of arbitrary, agreed-upon signs. Mrs. Welland, for example, knew perfectly well why Archer had begged her to announce May’s engagement at the Beaufort ball (and had fully expected him to do no less), yet she’d still felt obliged to pretend to hesitate, to act as if Archer had forced her hand—exactly like the stories of primitive brides being dragged shrieking from their parents’ tents in the books advanced cultured people were starting to read that year.
The end result of all this elaborate, collective trickery was that the young girl at its center only grew more inscrutable the more frank and unguarded she was. May was frank, his poor darling, because she had nothing to hide. She was confident, because she had no idea what to be on guard against. And with no better preparation than that, she was about to be plunged overnight into what people euphemistically called “the facts of life.”
Archer was sincerely, placidly in love. He delighted in May’s radiant good looks, her bright health, her easy grace on horseback, the quickness of her hand at lawn tennis and croquet, and the shy new interest in books and ideas she was just starting to develop under his gentle guidance. (She’d advanced far enough to join him in mocking the *Idylls of the King*, but not far enough yet to feel the strange, soft magic of Tennyson’s *Ulysses* and *The Lotus Eaters*.) She was straightforward, loyal, brave, and had a sharp sense of humor—mostly proven by the way she laughed so hard at his bad jokes. Deep in her innocent, wide-open soul, he suspected there was a warm, glowing passion that would be the greatest joy of his life to wake. But after he’d traced every wonderful part of her, he’d always circle back to that discouraged thought: all this frankness and innocence was an artificial product. Untrained human nature was not frank or innocent. It was full of twists, defenses, small instinctive guiles. He felt oppressed by this carefully manufactured thing of factitious purity, stitched together by a whole conspiracy of mothers, aunts, grandmothers, and long-dead ancestresses, made exactly to fit what society told him he wanted, what he had a “right” to—so he could exercise his lordly little pleasure of smashing it to pieces like an image carved out of snow.
These thoughts were nothing new; every young man had them in the weeks leading up to his wedding. But they usually came hand in hand with a sense of guilt and self-abasement that Archer felt no trace of. He could not bring himself to mope like Thackeray’s heroes (who always exasperated him so much) and mourn that he did not have a blank page to offer his bride in exchange for the unblemished one she was handing him. He could not escape the fact that if he’d been raised the exact way she had, the two of them would be no more fit to navigate the world than the Babes in the Wood. And for all his anxious turning over of the problem, he could not find a single honest reason—unconnected to his own fleeting momentary pleasure and silly masculine vanity—why his bride should not be allowed the same freedom to experience life he’d been granted.
These questions were bound to drift through his head at that hour, but he knew their uncomfortable, persistent sharpness was entirely the fault of Ellen Olenska turning up at the worst possible time. Right at the height of his betrothal, a moment that was supposed to be full of pure, cloudless hope, he’d been tossed headfirst into a mess of scandal that dragged up every difficult problem he’d been happy to leave buried. “Hang Ellen Olenska,” he grumbled under his breath, as he slid the fire screen over the embers and started to undress. He could not for the life of him see why her fate should have any bearing on his own quiet, happy future, yet he had a dim, uneasy feeling that he’d only just started to measure the risks of the defense his engagement had forced him to take up.
A few days later, the first bolt fell.
The Lovell Mingotts had sent out cards for what was known as a “formal dinner”: three extra footmen on staff, two dishes for every course, a frothy Roman punch served mid-meal to refresh the guests, and “To meet the Countess Olenska” printed at the very top of every invitation, in that warm hospitable American custom that treats strangers as if they were royalty, or at least royal ambassadors.
The guest list had been chosen with a boldness and shrewd discrimination everyone in on the joke immediately recognized as the firm hand of old Catherine the Great, Mrs. Manson Mingott. Alongside the perennial invites—the Selfridge Merrys, who got asked everywhere simply because they always had been, the Beauforts, who were related to the family, and Sillerton Jackson and his sister Sophy, who went wherever her brother told her to go—they’d invited some of the most fashionable, irreproachable members of the dominant young married set: the Lawrence Leffertses, the lovely widow Mrs. Lefferts Rushworth, the Harry Thorleys, the Reggie Chiverses, young Morris Dagonet and his wife, who had been a van der Luyden. The whole company was perfectly assorted, all belonging to that tight little inner group that ran around together every single night and day of the New York season, apparently with undiminished energy.
Forty-eight hours later, the unbelievable happened: every single guest sent their regrets, except the Beauforts, old Mr. Jackson, and his sister. Even the Reggie Chiverses, who were part of the Mingott clan, had turned them down. The intended slight was made even crueler by the fact that not a single one of the notes included the ordinary, polite courtesy lie of a “previously existing engagement.” New York society was far too small in those days, every part of it—even the livery stable keepers, butlers, and cooks—knew exactly which evenings everyone was free on, so every recipient of Mrs. Lovell Mingott’s note could make their determination not to meet the Countess Olenska perfectly, unmistakeably clear.
The blow was completely unexpected, but the Mingotts met it gallantly, the way they always did. Mrs. Lovell Mingott confided the whole mess to Mrs. Welland, who confided it to Newland Archer. Furious at the unprovoked outrage, he argued passionately to his own mother, who went through a short, painful stretch of inward resistance and outward temporizing before she caved to his pleading, the way she always did. She immediately embraced his cause with an energy doubled by her earlier hesitation, pulled on her grey velvet bonnet, and said: “I’m going to go see Louisa van der Luyden.”
The New York of Newland Archer’s day was a small, slippery pyramid, hardly a single fissure or foothold for outsiders carved into its smooth sides. At its base was a firm foundation of what Mrs. Archer called “plain people”: an honorable, obscure majority of respectable families, many of whom (the Spicers, the Leffertses, the Jacksons, for example) had climbed above their original station by marrying into one of the ruling clans. Mrs. Archer always said people weren’t as particular as they used to be, and with old Catherine Spicer ruling one end of Fifth Avenue and Julius Beaufort the other, the old traditions weren’t going to last much longer.
Narrowing steadily upward from that wealthy, unremarkable base was the compact, dominant group the Mingotts, Newlands, Chiverses, and Mansons all represented. Most people thought that group was the very apex of the pyramid, but they themselves—at least the members of Mrs. Archer’s generation—knew better. In the eyes of a proper professional genealogist, only a tiny handful of families could lay claim to that topmost eminence.
“Don’t tell me all that modern newspaper rubbish about a New York aristocracy,” Mrs. Archer would say to her children. “If there even is one, neither the Mingotts nor the Mansons belong to it—no, nor the Newlands or the Chiverses either. Our grandfathers and great-grandfathers were just respectable English or Dutch merchants, who came to the colonies to make their fortune, and stayed here because they did so well. One of your great-grandfathers signed the Declaration, and another was a general on Washington’s staff, who received General Burgoyne’s sword after the Battle of Saratoga. Those are things to be proud of, but they have nothing to do with real rank or class. New York has always been a commercial community, and there are not more than three families in it who can claim an aristocratic origin in the real sense of the word.”
Mrs. Archer, her son, and her daughter, like everyone else in their circle, knew exactly who those privileged families were: the Dagonets of Washington Square, descended from an old English county family allied with the Pitts and Foxes; the Lannings, who had intermarried with the descendants of Count de Grasse; and the van der Luydens, direct descendants of the first Dutch governor of Manhattan, tied by pre-revolutionary marriages to half a dozen branches of French and British aristocracy.
The Lannings were only represented by two very old, lively Miss Lannings who lived cheerfully surrounded by family portraits and Chippendale furniture. The Dagonets were a large, respected clan, tied to all the best old families in Baltimore and Philadelphia. But the van der Luydens, who stood above all of them, had faded into a kind of super-terrestrial twilight, from which only two figures emerged with quiet, imposing weight: Mr. and Mrs. Henry van der Luyden.
Mrs. Henry van der Luyden had been Louisa Dagonet, and her mother had been the granddaughter of Colonel du Lac, of an old Channel Island family that had fought under Cornwallis and settled in Maryland after the war with his bride, Lady Angelica Trevenna, fifth daughter of the Earl of St. Austrey. The tie between the Dagonets, the du Lacs of Maryland, and their aristocratic Cornish kinsfolk the Trevennas had always remained close and warm. Mr. and Mrs. van der Luyden had more than once paid long visits to the current head of the Trevenna house, the Duke of St. Austrey, at his country seat in Cornwall and his home at St. Austrey in Gloucestershire, and the Duke often talked of returning their visit one day (without the Duchess, who was terrified of crossing the Atlantic).
The van der Luydens split their time between Trevenna, their estate in Maryland, and Skuytercliff, the massive grand property on the Hudson that had been one of the original Dutch colonial grants to the first governor, making Mr. van der Luyden the last living Patroon. Their large, solemn house on Madison Avenue was almost never opened, and when they did come into town, they only invited their most intimate, cherished friends inside.
“I wish you’d come with me, Newland,” his mother said, pausing suddenly at the door of their Brown coupe. “Louisa is very fond of you. And of course, I’m taking this step for dear May—but also because if we don’t all stand together right now, there won’t be a single scrap of real Society left for any of us.”

Mrs. Henry van der Luyden sat perfectly still, her fine pale hands folded in her black velvet lap, and listened in unbroken silence to her cousin Mrs. Archer’s breathless account of the insult done to Countess Olenska.
Newland had told himself a dozen times beforehand that silence was simply Mrs. van der Luyden’s default state: that she was slow to commit to any opinion, but unfailingly gentle to those she counted among her intimates. Even so, the chill that wrapped around visitors the moment they stepped over the threshold of her high-ceilinged, white-walled Madison Avenue drawing room still made his skin prickle. The pale brocade armchairs were still draped in their summer dust covers, fine gauze veiled every gilded ormolu trinket on the mantel, and even the rich carved frame of the Gainsborough portrait of Lady Angelica du Lac hung half-shrouded, as if the room was never quite meant to be lived in at all.
Huntington’s portrait of Mrs. van der Luyden herself hung across from her ancestress, painted in black velvet and Venetian point lace that still defined every edge of her social persona, twenty years after the canvas was finished. Any onlooker would have sworn the living Mrs. van der Luyden, sitting straight under her own painted likeness, was the twin of the fair, soft-faced young woman drooping against a gilt armchair in the frame. Her fair hair had faded to a milky ash without ever turning grey, parted in flat, sharp overlapping points across her forehead, and the straight nose dividing her pale blue eyes was only just faintly more pinched at the nostrils than it had been the day she sat for the portrait. Newland often thought she looked as if she had been perfectly preserved for decades in the airless, spotless atmosphere of a life without a single misstep, the same way bodies trapped in glacial ice keep a rosy, deathless hue for generations.
