He and all his family esteemed her, admired her, deferred to her—but her soft, sweet bend of the neck felt far more unapproachable than the gruff, unyielding severity of his mother’s old spinster aunts, who said “No” before you had even finished stating your request. Mrs. van der Luyden never said no outright. She would waver, her lips curving into the ghost of a smile, and always reply, “I shall first have to talk this over with my husband.”
The two were so perfectly matched, so identical in their quiet, unassailable reserve, that Newland had often wondered how after forty years of marriage their merged identities could ever pull apart long enough to hold a “talking over” on any controversial topic. But as neither of them ever made a single decision without that secret, sacred conclave, Mrs. Archer and her son settled back, resigned to wait for that familiar line.
Mrs. van der Luyden surprised them. She reached out her long, slim hand and pulled the bell-rope.
“I think,” she said, “I should like Henry to hear what you have told me.”
A footman appeared, and she said gravely, “If Mr. van der Luyden has finished reading the newspaper, please ask him to be kind enough to come.” She spoke the words “reading the newspaper” with the reverence a prime minister’s wife would use to reference a cabinet meeting, not out of arrogance, but out of a lifetime of habit that had cast every small, mundane action of her husband as something nearly sacred.
Her speed made it clear she considered the matter every bit as urgent as Mrs. Archer did, but she softened the implication that she had already made up her mind with a sweet, warm smile. “Henry always enjoys seeing you, dear Adeline. And he will wish to congratulate Newland on his engagement.”
The heavy double doors swung open, and Mr. Henry van der Luyden stepped through: tall, spare, in a crisp frock coat, with the same faded fair hair, the same straight nose, the same look of frozen, unassailable gentleness in pale grey eyes where his wife’s were pale blue. He greeted Mrs. Archer with cousinly warmth, murmured his congratulations to Newland in the same soft, measured tone, and sank into one of the brocade armchairs with the easy, unapologetic dignity of a reigning sovereign.
“I had just finished reading *The Times*,” he said, folding his long fingertips together. “In town my mornings are so occupied, I find it far more convenient to read the papers after luncheon.”
“Ah, my uncle Egmont used to say exactly the same thing,” Mrs. Archer replied eagerly. “He said he found the news far less agitating if he waited till after dinner to read it.”
“Yes, my father abhorred hurry,” Mr. van der Luyden said, his tone slow and deliberate as he glanced around the shrouded, silent room that was the perfect mirror of its owners. “But nowadays the whole world seems to rush headlong.”
“But you had quite finished your reading, Henry?” his wife put in gently.
“Quite, quite,” he assured her.
“Then I should like Adeline to tell you—”
“Oh, it’s really Newland’s story to tell,” his mother laughed, and proceeded to recount the entire outrageous tale of Lawrence Lefferts snubbing Mrs. Lovell Mingott’s dinner party to protest Countess Olenska’s invitation. “Augusta Welland and Mary Mingott both agreed, of course—especially now that Newland is engaged to May—that you and Henry absolutely had to know what was going on.”
“Ah—” Mr. van der Luyden said, and drew a long, slow breath.
The monumental ormolu clock on the white marble mantel ticked so loud in the silence it sounded like the boom of a minute gun out at sea. Newland stared at the two faded, elegant figures sitting side by side, rigid as viceroys, the living voices of some old ancestral authority they had never asked to wield, who would far rather be hidden away in their country estate at Skuytercliff, pulling invisible weeds from their perfect lawns and playing patience together after dinner.
Mr. van der Luyden turned to Newland. “You are quite certain this is Lawrence Lefferts doing this on purpose?”
“Absolutely certain, sir,” Newland said. “Larry’s been more reckless than usual lately—cousin Louisa will forgive my saying it—he’s been carrying on a very obvious affair with the postmaster’s wife in their village, and whenever poor Gertrude starts to suspect something, he cooks up a big moral fuss like this to prove how impossibly upstanding he is, yelling about the impertinence of inviting his wife to meet people he doesn’t approve of. He’s only using Madame Olenska as a lightning rod to draw attention away from his own scandal. I’ve seen him pull this exact trick half a dozen times before.”
“The Leffertses!” Mrs. van der Luyden breathed.
“The Leffertses!” Mrs. Archer echoed, horrified. “What would Uncle Egmont have said of Lawrence Lefferts pronouncing judgement on anyone’s social position? It shows exactly what our society has come to.”
“We must hope it has not quite come to that,” Mr. van der Luyden said firmly.
“Ah, if only you and Louisa went out more!” Mrs. Archer sighed, then froze, horrified at her own misstep. The van der Luydens were notoriously sensitive to any criticism of their secluded lives. They were the final court of appeal for all New York society fashion and manners, they knew it, and they accepted the weight of that duty—but they were shy, quiet people who would far rather have hidden from public life entirely, and when they did come to town they turned down every invitation, citing Mrs. van der Luyden’s delicate health.
Newland hurried to his mother’s rescue. “Everyone in New York knows exactly what you and cousin Louisa stand for. That’s why Mrs. Mingott wouldn’t let this petty slight against Countess Olenska pass without consulting you first.”
Mrs. van der Luyden glanced at her husband, and he glanced back.
“It is the principle of the thing I dislike,” Mr. van der Luyden said. “As long as a member of a well-known family is backed by their kin, their place in society should be considered final. No one else has the right to question it.”
“It does seem that way,” his wife said, as if the thought had only just occurred to her.
“I had no idea things had deteriorated this far,” Mr. van der Luyden continued. He paused, looked at his wife again. “It occurs to me, my dear, that Countess Olenska is already a distant relation of ours—through Medora Manson’s first husband. And of course, she will be far more than that once Newland marries May.” He turned to Newland. “Have you read this morning’s *Times*, Newland?”
“Why, yes, sir,” Newland said, who usually skimmed half a dozen papers over his morning coffee.
The husband and wife looked at each other again, their pale eyes locked in a long, silent consultation no words were needed for. Then a faint, secret smile flickered across Mrs. van der Luyden’s face. She had guessed his plan, and approved.
Mr. van der Luyden turned back to Mrs. Archer. “If Louisa’s health allowed her to dine out,” he said, “please tell Mrs. Lovell Mingott that the two of us would have been happy to step in and fill the empty seats left by the Lawence Leffertses at her dinner. But as you well know, that is impossible.” Mrs. Archer murmured her understanding. “But Newland tells me he has read this morning’s *Times*, so he will already have seen that Louisa’s cousin, the Duke of St. Austrey, arrives next week on the *Russia*. He is coming to enter his new sloop, the *Guinevere*, in next summer’s International Cup Race, and to shoot canvasback ducks at our place Trevenna, in Maryland. Before we take him down there, we are hosting a small dinner for a few close friends to meet him, followed by a small reception. I am quite sure Louisa will be as delighted as I am if Countess Olenska will allow us to include her among our guests.”
He stood up, bent his long frame in a stiff, friendly gesture toward his cousin. “I think I speak for Louisa when I say she will drop the invitation off herself with our cards, on her drive this afternoon.”
Mrs. Archer knew this was the polite hint that the van der Luydens’ famous seventeen-hand chestnut horses were waiting at the door, never kept waiting a second, and she stood up, stammering her thanks. Mrs. van der Luyden beamed at her, the smile of Esther stepping forward to intercede with King Ahasuerus for her people. But Mr. van der Luyden held up a gentle, sovereign hand.
“There is nothing to thank me for, dear Adeline. Nothing at all. This sort of petty cruelty has no place in New York society. It never will, as long as I can help it,” he said, as he steered his cousins toward the front door.
Two hours later, half of old New York had already heard that the famous C-spring barouche Mrs. van der Luyden rode in all year round had been seen pulling up to old Mrs. Mingott’s door, where a large, square cream envelope was handed in to the servants. That evening at the opera, Sillerton Jackson could announce to the entire club box that the envelope contained a personal, hand-delivered invitation for Countess Olenska to attend the van der Luydens’ exclusive dinner the following week, in honor of the Duke of St. Austrey.
A group of young men leaning against the edge of the box exchanged quiet grins, glancing sideways at Lawrence Lefferts, who was slouched carelessly in the very front of the box, twisting his long fair moustache. As the soprano paused between arias, he spoke up in his most authoritative tone: “No one but Patti should ever dare attempt the lead in *La Sonnambula*.”

It was the universal consensus of old New York society that the Countess Olenska had lost her looks.
Years before, in Newland Archer’s boyhood, she had first appeared in the city as a brilliant, flame-pretty little girl of nine or ten, of whom every acquaintance declared she “ought to be captured on canvas.” Her parents were lifelong continental wanderers, and after a childhood spent traipsing from one European capital to the next, she lost them both, and was placed in the care of her aunt Medora Manson: another unrepentant wanderer, who had returned to New York to “settle down” for the seventh time. Poor Medora, repeatedly widowed, always came home to plant roots (each time in a smaller, less expensive house), dragging with her a new husband or newly adopted child—only to part from her husband or quarrel with her ward a few months later, sell the house at a steep loss, and vanish back onto the open road. Because her mother had been a Rushworth, and her last ill-fated marriage tied her to one of the famously eccentric Chivers clan, New York looked on her oddities with indulgence; but when she stepped off the steamer with her little orphaned niece, whose parents had been well-loved despite their regrettable taste for constant travel, the whole family sighed that it was a tragedy to leave that sweet pretty child in Medora’s erratic care.
Everyone was ready to be kind to little Ellen Mingott, even when her dusky red cheeks and tight springy curls lent her a giddy, unruly air that seemed wholly improper for a child who ought still to be wearing mourning black for her parents. Flouting the ironclad unwritten rules of American mourning was just one of Medora’s many peculiarities: when they arrived, her crape veil for her dead brother was a full seven inches shorter than her sisters-in-law’s, while little Ellen was dressed all in crimson merino strung with amber beads, looking less like a grieving orphan than a gipsy foundling. New York had long grown used to Medora, so only a handful of elderly matrons shook their heads over Ellen’s gaudy clothes, and all the rest of her relatives fell quickly under the spell of her bright color and unselfconscious high spirits. She was a fearless, unshy little thing who asked disconcertingly direct questions, made comments far too old for her years, and could do all sorts of outlandish tricks: dance a rousing Spanish shawl dance, and sing smoky Neapolitan love songs while playing accompaniment on a guitar. Under Medora’s guidance—who, having acquired a Papal title, had reverted to her first husband’s last name to style herself the Marchioness Manson, because in Italy she could twist it to sound like Manzoni—Ellen received an expensive, thoroughly incoherent education, one that included “drawing from the live model,” a practice no proper New York girl had ever dreamed of, and playing piano in quintets with professional European musicians.
Of course no good could come of it. A few years later, poor mad Chivers died in an asylum, his widow wrapped in bizarre foreign mourning weeds, packed up their things and left New York again, taking Ellen with her—now a tall, lanky girl with eyes so bright they were impossible to ignore. For a long time no one heard a word of them, then news drifted back: Ellen had married an immensely wealthy Polish nobleman of legendary repute, met at a ball at the Tuileries, who was said to own palaces in Paris, Nice and Florence, a yacht docked at Cowes, and thousands of acres of hunting grounds in Transylvania. She vanished in a kind of sulphurous, gilded apotheosis, and when Medora returned to New York some years later, subdued, penniless, mourning a third husband and searching for an even smaller cottage to rent, everyone wondered why her fabulously rich niece hadn’t lifted a finger to help her. Then came the news everyone had whispered of behind fans: Ellen’s marriage had collapsed in ruin, and she was coming home at last, to seek rest and anonymity among her own kinsfolk.
All of these memories drifted through Newland Archer’s head a week later, as he watched the Countess Olenska step into the van der Luyden drawing room on the night of that fateful, much-anticipated dinner. It was an occasion of immense gravity, and he found himself nervous, wondering if she would manage to carry herself through the scrutiny of New York’s most exclusive, unforgiving circle. She was a little late, one hand still ungloved, fumbling to fasten a bracelet around her wrist, yet she crossed the threshold with no trace of haste or embarrassment, right into a room where all of New York’s most chosen guests sat assembled like a jury.
She paused dead in the center of the drawing room, her mouth grave, her eyes soft and smiling, and in that single instant Archer rejected the whole city’s verdict on her looks. It was true her early girlish radiance was gone. The bright rose flush had faded from her cheeks, she was thin, worn, a little older-looking than her age, which could not be more than thirty. But there hung about her the quiet, unassailable authority of real beauty: a sureness in the way she held her head, the slow, deliberate movement of her eyes, that was not the least bit theatrical, yet struck him as finely honed, brimming with conscious, gentle power. She was simpler in her manners than almost all the other ladies in the room, too, and many guests (as he heard his sister Janey report later) were bitterly disappointed she was not more “stylish”—for stylishness was the highest virtue New York knew. Archer suspected it was because her old quick vivacity had burned away: she was so quiet, quiet in her movements, quiet in her low, warm, unhurried voice. New York had expected something far louder, far more dramatic from a young woman with such a checkered past.
The dinner was a formidable affair at best. Dining with the van der Luydens was never a light, casual experience, but dining there with a Duke who was their direct cousin was little short of a religious solemnity. Archer smiled to himself, pleased that only a born old New Yorker could spot the fine distinction: any stray foreign nobleman might be received politely, but the van der Luydens’ Duke was welcomed with a warm, old-fashioned reverence no visiting lord would ever mistake for mere awe of their title. It was for these tiny, unspoken social nuances that Archer cherished his old New York, even as he laughed at its absurdities.
The van der Luydens had pulled out every stop to mark the occasion. The rare du Lac Sevres china was on the table, alongside the Trevenna George II silver, the van der Luyden’s prized East India Company Lowestoft set, and the Dagonet family Crown Derby. Mrs. van der Luyden looked more than ever like a Cabanel portrait, and Archer’s own mother, decked in her grandmother’s seed pearls and emeralds, reminded him of a delicate Isabey miniature. Every lady wore her finest heirloom jewels, all set in the heavy, old-fashioned mountings the family treasured, and even the reclusive old Miss Lanning, who had been persuaded at great length to attend, wore her mother’s carved cameos and a fine Spanish blonde shawl.
The Countess Olenska was the only young woman at the dinner, yet as Archer glanced around at all the smooth, plump elderly faces framed by diamond necklaces and towering ostrich plumes, they all struck him as curiously shallow, curiously immature, next to hers. It frightened him, a little, to try to imagine all the sorrow, all the joy, all the wild experience that had gone into making her eyes look the way they did.
The Duke of St. Austrey, seated at his hostess’s right hand, was supposed to be the star of the evening. But if the Countess Olenska was less dramatic than everyone had hoped, the Duke was almost entirely invisible. He was too well-bred to show up in a shooting jacket like a recent ducal visitor to the city, but his evening clothes were shabby and baggy, and he wore them with such an air of being no different from homespun that, paired with his stooped posture and the thick beard that spilled over his shirtfront, he hardly looked like he was dressed for dinner at all. He was short, round-shouldered, sunburnt, with a thick nose, small twinkling eyes and a warm, sociable smile, but he rarely spoke, and when he did his voice was so low that even during the frequent expectant silences around the table, no one but his immediate neighbors could hear a word he said.
When the men joined the ladies in the drawing room after dinner, the Duke walked straight over to the Countess Olenska, and the two sat down in a far corner and launched into animated, easy chatter. Neither of them seemed to notice that etiquette dictated the Duke ought to have first paid his respects to the elder matrons Mrs. Lovell Mingott and Mrs. Headly Chivers, and that the Countess ought to have sat perfectly still in her chair, waiting for the amiable hypochondriac Mr. Urban Dagonet—who had broken his decades-long rule of never dining out between January and April just for the chance to meet her—to come and call on her. They chatted for nearly twenty minutes before the Countess stood up, walked alone clear across the width of the drawing room, and settled herself right on the sofa beside Newland Archer.
It was no custom in New York drawing rooms for a lady to get up and leave one gentleman’s side to seek out the company of another. Etiquette demanded she sit immovable as an idol, while suitors and callers took turns sitting beside her. But the Countess seemed utterly unaware she had broken any rule at all. She sat perfectly at ease, a soft, unguarded smile on her face, and looked at him with the kindest, warmest eyes.
“I want you to talk to me about May,” she said.
Instead of answering, he asked: “You knew the Duke before, then?”
“Oh, yes—we used to see him every winter at Nice. He’s very fond of gambling—he used to come to the house all the time.” She said it as simply as if she had said “he is very fond of wildflowers,” then added candidly a moment later: “I think he is the dullest man I have ever met in my life.”
It pleased him so much that he forgot the faint, sharp shock her offhand remark had given him. It was undeniably, thrillingly exciting to meet a woman who found the van der Luydens’ own duke dull, and dared to say so out loud. He ached to question her, to hear all about that wild, far-off European life her careless words had given him such a vivid, glowing glimpse of, but he feared to brush against the painful memories of her failed marriage, and before he could frame a careful question she had drifted back to her original subject.
“May is a darling. I have not seen any young girl in New York so handsome and so clever. Are you very much in love with her?”
Archer reddened and laughed. “As much as any man can be.”
She regarded him thoughtfully, tilting her head as if she did not want to miss a single shade of meaning in his words. “Do you think, then, that there is a limit?”
“To being in love? If there is, I have not found it.”
Her face lit up with soft, unfeigned sympathy. “Ah—so it really and truly is a romance?”
“The most romantic of all romances,” he smiled.
“How wonderful. And the two of you found it all out for yourselves? It was not arranged for you at all?”
Archer blinked at her, a little incredulous. “Have you forgotten that in our country we do not let other people arrange our marriages for us?”
A dark dusky blush rose to her cheek, and he regretted his words instantly.
“Yes,” she said, her voice soft. “I’d forgotten. Forgive me if I make these mistakes sometimes. I do not always remember that everything that was bad where I came from is good, and right, here.” She looked down at the Viennese fan made of eagle feathers in her hand, and he saw her lips tremble just a little.
“I am so sorry,” he said, leaning forward impulsively. “But you are among friends here, you know that.”
“Yes. I know. Wherever I go tonight, that is the feeling I get. That is why I came home. I want to forget everything else, to become a complete American again, like the Mingotts and Wellands, and you and your delightful mother, and all the other good people here tonight. Ah, here is May arriving now,” she added, but she did not move to stand. Her eyes drifted from the door, where May had just appeared, and rested soft and steady on his face.
The drawing rooms were filling up with the rest of the after-dinner guests, and following her gaze, Archer saw May Welland step in with her mother. Dressed in white and silver, a wreath of tiny silver blossoms twisted in her hair, the tall girl looked like Diana just alighted from a night’s hunt in the woods.
“Oh,” Archer laughed. “I have so many rivals. Look, she is already surrounded. The Duke is being introduced to her now.”
“Then stay with me a little longer,” Madame Olenska said in a low, quiet tone, and the tip of her feather fan brushed, light as a breath, against his knee. It was the faintest possible touch, but it thrilled him straight through like a caress.
“Yes, let me stay,” he answered, his voice soft, hardly knowing what he was saying—just then Mr. van der Luyden came up to them, followed by old Mr. Urban Dagonet. The Countess greeted them with her grave, gentle smile, and Archer, feeling his host’s quiet admonitory glance on him, rose and surrendered his seat.
Madame Olenska held out her hand to him as if to say goodbye.
“Tomorrow, then, after five. I shall expect you,” she said—and then turned to make space for Mr. Dagonet on the sofa beside her.
“Tomorrow,” Archer heard himself repeating, even though there had been no engagement, no prior hint all through their conversation that she wanted to see him again.
As he wandered away, he saw Lawrence Lefferts, tall and resplendent in his evening wear, leading his wife up to be introduced, and heard Gertrude Lefferts beam at the Countess with her large, unperceiving smile, gushing that they must have gone to dancing school together as little girls. Behind her, waiting their turn to introduce themselves, Archer spotted all the stubborn couples who had refused to meet the Countess at Mrs. Lovell Mingott’s dinner party a few weeks prior. As his mother had often said: when the van der Luydens chose to make a point, they knew exactly how to teach all of New York a lesson. The wonder was that they chose to do it so rarely.
A soft hand touched his arm, and he looked up to see Mrs. van der Luyden looking down at him, radiant in black velvet and the family diamonds. “It was so good of you, dear Newland, to devote yourself so unselfishly to Madame Olenska. I told your cousin Henry he absolutely had to come to the rescue.”
He smiled at her vaguely, and she added, as if indulging his natural shyness: “I have never seen May looking lovelier. The Duke thinks she is the handsomest girl in the whole room.”
Archer nodded, but his mind was already far away, fixed on the light brush of eagle feathers against his knee, and the quiet, unspoken promise of five o’clock the next afternoon.

Countess Olenska had said she would be free “after five”; and when the half-hour chimed, Newland Archer pressed his thumb to the bell of the peeling stucco house far down West Twenty-third Street, its frail cast-iron balcony all but swallowed whole by a giant, tangled wisteria vine. The neighbourhood was an odd choice for a woman of her family’s standing: her nearest neighbours were tiny dressmakers, bird stuffers, and ragtag “people who wrote”, and further down the unkempt cobblestone lane Archer spotted the weathered wooden cottage his acquaintance Winsett the journalist had once pointed out to him on a late-night stroll. He’d shivered then, wondering if all the great capitals of the world housed their thinkers in such shabby, mean little shelters. Ellen’s home was only barely better than Winsett’s, its modest front redeemed by a fresh coat of pale paint around the window frames, and Archer’s chest tightened as he thought: that Polish count of hers must have stolen not just her fortune, but every last one of her illusions too.

It had been a thoroughly rotten day. He’d lunched with the Wellands, hoping to steal May away for a walk in Central Park, to tell her how stunning she’d looked the night before, how proud he was to be hers, and beg her to push their wedding date forward. But Mrs. Welland had shaken her head firmly, reminding him their round of family calls was barely half done, and when he’d mentioned moving the wedding up, she’d sighed reproachfully, “Twelve dozen of everything, all hand-embroidered, Newland, we can’t rush that.” They’d piled into the family landau and trundled from one brownstone doorstep to the next, and by the end of the afternoon Archer felt like some well-trained wild animal, trotted out for every distant cousin’s approval. He knew his anthropology books were making him over-critical of what was, after all, just a loving family’s fuss over their daughter, but the thought of waiting all the way until next autumn to marry her, of months more of this endless tedious social parade, made his spirits sink cold and damp. “Tomorrow we do the Chiverses and the Dallases!” Mrs. Welland had called after him as he left, and he realised they were working their way alphabetically through every related household in New York, and they hadn’t even gotten past the first quarter of the alphabet.

He’d meant to tell May that Ellen had asked—no, almost commanded—him to call that afternoon, but there had never been a quiet spare minute to mention it. Besides, it felt absurd to bring up: May had been the one so eager for him to be kind to her cousin, hadn’t she? That very wish was what had pushed their engagement announcement forward weeks ahead of schedule. It gave him a strange, unnameable little jolt to think that if Ellen Olenska had never come back to New York, he might still be a free man, or at least less irrevocably tied to this path. But May had wanted it this way, so he felt almost relieved of the weight of keeping her in the dark about the visit.

When the door opened to a swarthy, grinning Sicilian maid who gestured him wordlessly through a narrow hall to a low drawing room lit only by a crackling fire, curiosity was thrumming under his skin. He couldn’t puzzle out the tone of her summons; she was far less simple and straightforward than the polite, melancholy woman she’d presented as at the van der Luydens’ dinner. The maid left him standing there for so long he began to wonder if she thought he’d come to wind the visibly stopped mantel clock, until she came back with a lamp and Archer fumbled through half-remembered lines of Dante to ask after her mistress, and got the giddy reply: “La signora e fuori; ma verra subito—she’s out, but she’ll be here any minute.”

By the lamp’s glow he drank in the room, soft and shadowy and nothing at all like any New York parlour he’d ever stepped into. The little bits of “wreckage” Ellen had brought back from her life abroad were scattered everywhere: slim dark wood side tables, a tiny delicate Greek bronze on the mantel, a stretch of rich red damask nailed straight to the water-stained wallpaper behind two old, unidentifiable Italian paintings. Archer prided himself on his knowledge of Italian art, had devoured Ruskin and Pater and Symonds, but these paintings confused him, didn’t fit the “approved” Italian school works every other household hung on their walls. For a second he felt foolish, waiting alone in a strange lady’s house at dusk, and a flicker of worry shot through him: what if May turned up to call on her cousin, and found him here like they were old intimate friends? But he sank into the armchair by the fire anyway, stretched his boots out to the logs, and let the weird, warm atmosphere wrap around him. The shabby rented house, that would have held nothing but pampas grass and Rogers statuettes in Medora Manson’s care, had been transformed with a few small touches into something intimate, foreign, steeped in old romantic secrets. He tried to parse the magic: the way the furniture was grouped, the single vase holding just two Jacqueminot roses (no New York hostess ever bought less than a dozen), the faint, heady scent hanging in the air that wasn’t the usual lavender sachet on handkerchiefs, but a mix of Turkish coffee, ambergris, and dried rose petals you’d only find in some far-off bazaar.

His mind wandered to the house the Wellands had already picked out for him and May, the new build on remote East Thirty-ninth Street, faced in that ghastly greenish-yellow stone the younger architects were championing as a rebellion against all New York’s uniform cold chocolate-brown brownstone. The plumbing was perfect, they’d told him, and he could have the library lined with plain Eastlake furniture and glassless bookcases, but the rest of it stretched before him like a life sentence: every evening he’d walk through those cast-iron railings, into the Pompeian vestibule, past the varnished yellow wood wainscoting, and sit in a drawing room draped in purple satin and yellow tufting, full of sham Buhl tables and gilt vitrines crammed with modern Saxe figurines. He’d daydreamed of a long honeymoon travelling, of even wintering in Egypt, but the Wellands were insistent they needed a proper New York house to come home to, and he knew he had no real way to argue.

The maid came back to stir the fire, draw the curtains, and murmur consolingly “Verra—verra, she’s coming,” before slipping out again. Archer was just standing up, half ready to leave before he made a fool of himself, when he heard the clink of hooves on the cobblestones outside. He pulled the curtain aside and peered out: Julius Beaufort’s neat English brougham was pulled up at the curb, the banker stepping down to hand Ellen out of the carriage. They stood talking a moment, Beaufort tipping his hat to her before he climbed back into his cab, and she turned to mount the steps.

When she walked into the room, there was no trace of surprise on her face at finding him there—surprise seemed an emotion she simply never bothered to waste. “How do you like my funny little house?” she asked, untying the ribbon of her velvet bonnet and tossing it and her cloak onto a side table, her eyes soft and meditative as she looked at him. “To me, it’s heaven.”

“It’s delightful, the way you’ve arranged it,” Archer said, even as he winced at how flat the words sounded.

“Oh, it’s a tiny shabby place, all my relations despise it,” she laughed, sinking into the chair opposite his by the fire. “But at least it’s not as gloomy as the van der Luydens’.”

The words jolted him like an electric shock. No one in New York dared call the van der Luydens’ grand, stately mansion gloomy—everyone who was ever allowed inside shivered in its halls and called it “handsome”. But suddenly Archer was glad she’d said what every single one of them had only ever thought. “It’s perfect, what you’ve done in here,” he corrected himself.

“I love that it’s mine,” she said, her voice dropping so low he almost missed the words. “That I can be alone here, and not have anyone watching every move I make.”

“You like being alone that much?” he asked, awkward, before he could stop himself.

“As long as my friends don’t let me get lonely,” she smiled. She leaned back, tucking her hands behind her head, and her lids drooped as she stared into the fire. “This is the hour I like best, don’t you think?”

“I was afraid you’d forgotten I was coming,” he said, a little stiff. “Beaufort must have been keeping you very occupied.”

She looked amused. “Have you waited long? He took me around to look at other houses, because apparently no one in New York will let me stay in this one. I’ve never been in a city where everyone turns their nose up at living in a ‘fun’ neighbourhood. What does it matter where you live? They say this street is respectable, after all.”

“It’s not fashionable,” Archer told her.

“Fashionable? Do all of you care that much about it? Why can’t you make your own fashions?” She sighed, softening. “But I suppose I’ve lived too freely for too long. All I want now is to fit in here, to feel safe, like people are looking after me.”

Archer’s chest ached, just like it had the night before when she’d said she was desperate for guidance. “That’s exactly what all your friends here want for you,” he said, with a flicker of sarcasm. “New York’s the safest place in the world.”

“Isn’t it?” she said, missing the mockery entirely, her face lighting up. “It feels just like a holiday you get when you’ve been a very good little girl and finished all your lessons.”

The analogy didn’t sit quite right with him. He didn’t mind making fun of New York himself, but he hated hearing anyone else dismiss it so lightly. He wondered if she realised just how powerful the unspoken rules of the city were, how close she’d come to total social ruin before the van der Luydens had stepped in to rescue her. He suspected she still didn’t see any of it, that her view of New York was still soft and blurry, and that thought nettled him.

“Last night New York turned itself inside out to welcome you,” he said. “The van der Luydens don’t do anything by halves.”

“They were so kind! It was such a lovely party,” she said, as if she’d been at a small tea at the old Miss Lannings’ instead of the most exclusive event of the season.

“The van der Luydens are the most powerful people in New York society,” Archer explained, feeling pompous even as he said it. “Unfortunately, because of Mrs. van der Luyden’s health, they almost never host.”

She lifted her thin dark brows, looking at him thoughtfully. “Isn’t that why they’re so powerful? Because they make themselves so rare?”

Archer coloured, stared at her, and burst out laughing. She’d pricked the grand myth of the van der Luydens with one tiny, simple line, and the whole thing had deflated into nothing.

The maid Nastasia carried in the tea tray then, with handleless Japanese cups and little covered sweet dishes, and Ellen leaned forward to hand him his cup, her fingers brushing his for half a second. “You’ll explain all these little unspoken things to me, won’t you? Tell me everything I ought to know.”

“Actually, you’re the one opening my eyes,” he said. “I’ve looked at all these things my whole life, I stopped seeing them years ago.”

She unclasped a small gold cigarette case strung to her bracelet, held it out to him, and took one for herself, reaching for a long spill off the mantel to light them from the fire. “Ah, then we can help each other. But I need your help far more. You have to tell me exactly what to do.”

He’d been about to blurt out “Don’t let anyone see you driving around with Beaufort,” but the warm, foreign, faraway atmosphere of the room wrapped around him, and the warning felt absurd, like trying to tell someone haggling for attar of roses in Samarkand that they ought to pack galoshes for a New York winter. From where he sat, New York felt smaller than Samarkand, viewed through the wrong end of a telescope, tiny and distant.

“I know everyone else is lining up to tell me what to do,” she said, her voice softening. “My aunts, old Granny Mingott, all of them. They’re all cross with me for trying to make my own choices. Granny wanted me to stay locked up in her house forever, but I had to be free.” Her shoulders shook, and suddenly tears were spilling down her cheeks. “Does no one here want to know the truth, Mr. Archer? The real loneliness is living surrounded by all these kind people who only ever ask you to pretend.”

“Ellen—don’t, Ellen,” he heard himself say, jumping up to kneel beside her, wrapping his hand around hers to warm it, murmuring soft reassuring things. She pulled back a moment later, wiping her wet lashes with a little laugh, and started to straighten her loosened braids. “Does no one cry here either? I suppose you never need to cry in heaven, do you?”

He was still reeling from the fact that he’d called her Ellen, twice, and she hadn’t even blinked. Far off, at the other end of that inverted telescope, he could just make out May Welland’s soft white figure, waiting for him in New York.

Then Nastasia poked her head in, chattering something in rapid Italian, and the Duke of St Austrey trundled into the room, steering a huge woman in overflowing furs, black wig and massive red plumes atop her head. “My dear Countess! I’ve brought an old friend of mine who’s been dying to meet you—Mrs. Struthers. She wasn’t invited to the party last night, and she’s simply desperate to make your acquaintance.”

“Of course I want to meet you, my dear!” Mrs. Struthers boomed, her voice as rich and loud as her plumes. “I love everyone young and charming and interesting. The Duke tells me you adore music—you play piano yourself, don’t you? Well, you simply must come to my house tomorrow evening, to hear Sarasate play. I host every Sunday night, when all of New York hasn’t a clue what to do with itself, so I invite them over to have fun. Bring your young gentleman along too,” she added, turning to Archer and shaking his hand warmly. “I can’t place you, but I know we’ve met somewhere—Paris, London, all the diplomats come to my parties. You like music too, right? Duke, make sure you bring him.”

The Duke rumbled “Rather” deep in his beard, and Archer bowed stiffly, feeling like a self-conscious schoolboy as he took his leave. He wasn’t upset the visit had ended, only wished the interruption had come a little sooner, so he wouldn’t have had to waste all that emotion leaning in so close to Ellen Olenska, calling her name like that. When he stepped out into the cold wintry night, New York felt vast and real again, and May Welland the most beautiful woman in the whole of it. He stopped off at his usual florist to send her the daily box of lilies-of-the-valley he was mortified to realise he’d forgotten that morning.

As he waited for an envelope, his eye fell on a cluster of yellow roses, so bright and sun-gold he’d never seen anything like them before. His first thought was to send them to May instead of the lilies—but they were too rich, too fiery, too bold, nothing at all like the soft, sweet girl he was engaged to. On a sudden, unthinking rush of feeling, he gestured for the florist to tuck the roses into a second long box. He slipped an envelope addressed to Countess Olenska under it, but at the very last second, he pulled his own calling card out of the fold, leaving the envelope blank. “These will go out right away?” he asked, pointing to the roses.

The florist assured him they would.

The next day, Newland Archer coaxed May Welland out for a walk in Central Park right after luncheon, a small, gentle rebellion against the old Episcopalian New York ritual that saw every respectable family troop to Sunday afternoon church services. Mrs. Welland didn’t protest the small truancy — she’d just spent the morning convincing May that a long engagement was nothing but a blessing, giving her plenty of time to stitch the hand-embroidered trousseau that would hold exactly the proper number of dozens of every linen, lace and ribbon the old society dictated a bride must own.

It was a perfect winter day. The bare, vaulted branches arching over the Mall stretched against a sky so vivid it might have been cut from solid lapis lazuli, and the snow under their boots glinted like shards of crushed crystal. The crisp, bright air lit May from the inside out, her cheeks flushed pink as a young maple’s frost-kissed leaves, and Archer swelled with quiet pride at the sidelong glances passersby cast her way. For a little while, the simple warm joy of being her suitor chased all his tangled, unnameable misgivings clean away.
“Isn’t it lovely?” she breathed, squeezing his arm. “Waking up every morning to smell lilies-of-the-valley on your dressing table! You sent them late yesterday, I know — you didn’t have time to drop them off before the morning rush —”
“But it means more than a standing order that leaves them on your doorstep at 8 a.m. sharp, doesn’t it?” he laughed. “Unlike Gertrude Lefferts, who got hers like clockwork every day right when her music teacher showed up, for all the world to see.”
“Exactly!” May giggled, nudging his shoulder. He glanced sideways at her soft, peach-ripened cheek, feeling uncharacteristically bold, and added, “Yesterday afternoon, when I dropped off your lilies, I spotted the most gorgeous bunch of yellow roses. I sent them over to Madame Olenska. Was that the right thing to do?”
“How sweet of you!” she said, lighting up. “She’d adore that. It’s so odd she didn’t mention it, though — she lunched with us today, and all she talked about was Mr. Beaufort sending her rare orchids, and cousin Henry van der Luyden’s whole hamper of carnations from Skuytercliff. She acts like no one’s ever sent her flowers before. She thinks it’s such a charming custom, and she says people never do that sort of thing casually in Europe.”
Archer’s jaw tightened, a sharp prick of irritation spiking through him. “No wonder my roses got completely overshadowed by Beaufort’s flashy gifts,” he muttered, and immediately kicked himself. He’d forgotten he hadn’t slipped a card in with the roses. Now he couldn’t very well admit he’d called on Ellen Olenska the day before, when she’d clearly not mentioned his visit to May — and keeping the secret made the whole thing feel far more sordid and mysterious than he wanted it to. To shake off the awkward lull, he steered the conversation to their own plans, the long engagement Mrs. Welland was insisting on.
“Long?” May blinked, laughing a little. “Isabel Chivers was engaged for two whole years, Grace and Thorley for almost a year and a half! We’re hardly waiting any time at all, compared to them.”
It was the exact, scripted line every nice young woman of their set was supposed to say, and Archer felt a flicker of ashamed pity. She wasn’t being dishonest, he told himself — she just echoed the lines the world had written for her. She was nearly twenty-two. At what age, he wondered, were “nice” women ever allowed to speak a thought that was entirely their own?
Never, if men like him never let them, he mused, remembering the heated thing he’d blurted out to Sillerton Jackson not long before: that women ought to be as free as men were. Soon, he told himself, it would be his job to peel the soft bandage from May’s eyes, to show her the whole wide world beyond the narrow bounds of New York society. But how many generations of her female ancestors had been buried in their family vaults, still wearing that same blindfold? He shivered, remembering a passage from his science books, the famous case of the Kentucky cave fish that had grown no eyes at all, because there was never any light for them to see. What if, when he told May to open hers, all she could do was stare blankly at a blank, empty world?
“We could be so much happier than that,” he said softly. “We could be together right away. We could travel, see all the places people only read about.”
Her face lit up. “That would be lovely,” she said, “but mother would never understand why we’d want to do things so differently. It’s just not how it’s done.”
“Shouldn’t doing things differently be the whole point?” he pressed.
“Newland, you’re so original!” she exclaimed, beaming at him.
His heart sank. He was saying exactly the same silly romantic lines every other young suitor in their set said, and she was reciting the exact scripted response tradition had taught her — even down to calling him original.
“Original?” he laughed bitterly. “We’re all cut from the same folded paper doll pattern, stencilled onto the same wall over and over again. Can’t we just break away from it, you and I, May?”
He stopped walking and turned to face her, and she looked back at him, bright-eyed and unclouded, full of nothing but adoration.
“Mercy — are you suggesting we elope?” she laughed.
“If you want to,” he said.
“Oh Newland, you do love me!” she sighed, soft and happy.
“Then why not be even happier?” he pushed.
“We can’t act like people in cheap novels, though, can we?”
“Why not?”
She looked a little bored by his insistence. She knew perfectly well they couldn’t, and it was tiresome to have to explain why. “I’m not clever enough to argue with you,” she said, relieved when the perfect excuse popped into her head. “That sort of thing is rather vulgar, isn’t it?”
Archer flinched. “Are you that afraid of being a little vulgar?”
She stared, thrown by the question. “Of course I hate it — and so do you,” she said, a little sharp around the edges.
He fell quiet, tapping his walking stick nervously against the toe of his boot. She’d clearly won the argument, and she brightened immediately, changing the subject. “Oh! Did I tell you I showed Ellen my engagement ring? She says it’s the most beautiful setting she’s ever seen, that there’s nothing half so lovely in all the Rue de la Paix in Paris. I love you so much for being so artistic, Newland.”

The next afternoon, Archer sat sullen in his study, smoking before dinner, having skipped his usual stop at the club on his way home from his law office. The slow, lazy, unchanging routine of it all was gnawing at his bones — sameness, endless sameness, the same faces behind the plate glass windows, the same conversations that always went exactly the same way. He didn’t even need to walk in to know they’d be dissecting the visiting Duke, and gossiping about Fanny Ring, the golden-haired mistress who’d been spotted driving her canary-yellow brougham down Fifth Avenue at the fashionable hour, a sight that had sent half the matrons of New York scurrying for their shawls. He could already hear Lawrence Lefferts holding forth, loud and self-righteous, about the total collapse of old society.
His sister Janey wandered in, hovering by his desk like a little Cassandra. He pretended to be deep in Swinburne’s new *Chastelard*, like he hadn’t noticed her come in.
“Mother’s very angry,” she announced, after sighing over his shelf of old French story books.
“Angry? About what?” he groaned.
“Miss Sophy Jackson just dropped by. She says her brother Sillerton is coming over after dinner to tell us all the details — he’s with cousin Louisa van der Luyden right now.”
“Janey, for heaven’s sake, start over. No human being on earth could follow that.”
“It’s no time to be irreverent, Newland! Mother was upset enough that you skipped church yesterday.”
He groaned and dropped his head back into his book.
“Newland, listen! Your friend Madame Olenska went to Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s party last night. She went with the Duke and Mr. Beaufort.”
A hot, senseless surge of anger boiled up in his chest. He laughed, loud and sharp, to smother it. “So what? I knew she planned to go.”
Janey paled, her eyes going wide. “You knew? And you didn’t stop her? Warn her?”
“Warn her? I’m not engaged to marry the Countess Olenska,” he said, and the words sounded absurd, too loud, hanging in the air between them.
“But you’re marrying into her family! You pushed for the engagement to be announced early so we could all help hold her up.”
“Uphold what? The stuffy old rules no one even wants to follow?” he jeered.
“Newland — don’t you care about Family?”
“Not a brass farthing.”
“Nor what cousin Louisa van der Luyden will think?”
“Not half of one, if all she thinks is silly old maid’s rubbish.”
“Mother is not an old maid,” Janey snapped, her lips pinching tight.
He bit back the retort bubbling up in his throat — that all of them, the van der Luydens, his own mother, everyone in their whole set, were nothing but old maids at heart, too scared to ever let real life brush against them. But Janey’s gentle face was already crumpling into tears, and he softened, ashamed. “Don’t be a goose, Janey — I’m not her keeper.”
“No, but cousin Henry only invited Ellen to the Duke’s dinner to do you a favor, to make you happy. Now they’re so upset they’re leaving for Skuytercliff tomorrow. You need to come downstairs. You have no idea how badly mother feels.”
Archer found his mother in the drawing room, her brow creased over her needlework. “Janey told you?” she said, when he walked in.
“Yes. And I can’t take it very seriously. They’re upset over a silly little thing like Ellen going to a party at a house they think is common?”
“Common? Mrs. Struthers lets people sing those terrible Paris songs on top of tables,” she said, horrified. “There’s smoking, champagne, all kinds of ungodliness on a Sunday night.”
“New York’s not the only place in the world, mother. The rest of the world hasn’t collapsed over parties like that.”
“Ellen Olenska came back here to get away from exactly that kind of fast European life! She’s a New Yorker. She should respect our ways.”
Before he could argue further, the butler pulled back the portières and announced Mr. Henry van der Luyden.
Archer’s mother scrambled to straighten her cap, and Janey jumped to push an armchair forward.
“We were just talking about you, sir,” Archer said, stepping forward to greet him.
Mr. van der Luyden drew off his gloves to shake their hands, smoothing his tall top hat shyly. “Ah — and about the Countess Olenska, I expect. I just came from visiting her. What a charming woman. She has a gift for arranging flowers, you know. I sent her some carnations from Skuytercliff, and instead of shoving them all in one big stiff bouquet like our gardener does, she scattered them loose all around her drawing room, every little nook. The Duke told me I had to come see how cleverly she’d decorated the place, and he was right. I do wish Louisa could go visit her too, if the neighborhood wasn’t so dreadfully unfashionable.”
A dead silence fell over the room.
“The truth is,” he went on, stroking his long grey leg, the old Patroon signet ring on his finger glinting, “I went over to thank her for her sweet note about the flowers, and to give her the gentlest little warning about letting the Duke drag her off to parties with him. You’ve heard the silly story about her going to Mrs. Struthers’s last night? Sillerton Jackson came to tell Louisa all about it, and she was quite upset. But I went straight to Ellen, dropped her the softest hint in the world, and she understood immediately. She was so grateful for the guidance.”
He looked around the room, his face soft with that mild, benevolent expression the whole society knew so well. “I told Sillerton Jackson before I left that there’s to be no gossip, no snubbing. I don’t invite anyone to my house that I don’t like, and the Countess Olenska will be welcomed anywhere she pleases to go. We’re off to dine early now, to take the Duke to the opera. Louisa’s waiting for me.”
When the door had closed behind him, no one spoke for a long minute.
“Gracious — how romantic!” Janey blurted out at last.
His mother sighed, shaking her head. “I just hope it all turns out for the best. Newland, you stay in and see Sillerton Jackson if he comes tonight. I shan’t know what to say to him.”
Archer laughed, leaning down to kiss the frown off her forehead. “Don’t worry, mother. He won’t come.”
Somewhere, far in the back of his mind, he was thinking of the yellow roses he’d sent Ellen, still with no card, still unmentioned. For the first time in months, that endless, stifling sameness of his life felt like it had cracked open, just a little, letting in a sliver of something bright and new. He didn’t know yet if it was a good thing.

Some two weeks later, Newland Archer slouched in the quiet dim of his tiny private nook at the law offices of Letterblair, Lamson and Low, half lost to idle, wandering thoughts, when the clerk rapped at his door. The head of the firm wanted to see him at once.

Old Mr. Letterblair, the longstanding legal custodian of three full generations of New York gentility, was throned behind his heavy mahogany desk, his tight-clipped white whiskers stroked absently as he ran a hand through the rumpled grey hair above his jutting brows. To his irreverent young junior, he looked exactly like the old family physician, thoroughly vexed by a patient whose symptoms refused to fit any known, neat classification.

“My dear sir” — he always addressed Archer as “sir,” in formal deference to their shared clientele — “I’ve sent for you to handle a delicate matter. One I’d prefer not to mention to Mr. Skipworth or Mr. Redwood for the time being.” Those two were the other named senior partners, of course, long dead these thirty years, a quirk of old New York’s oldest legal firms that made Mr. Letterblair, professionally speaking, his own grandson.

He leaned back, brow furrowed. “It’s a family matter.” Archer looked up. “The Mingott family. Mrs. Manson Mingott called on me yesterday. Her granddaughter, the Countess Olenska, wishes to sue her husband for divorce. Certain papers have been placed in my hands.” He drummed his fingers lightly on the polished desk. “Given your forthcoming alliance with the family, I’d like to consult you, talk this case through before we take any further steps.”

Warm blood surged hot to Archer’s temples. He had only seen Ellen Olenska once since that quiet visit to her fire-lit parlor, and that had been at the opera, tucked away in the Mingott family box. Over these past weeks, the sharp, vivid weight of her had faded from the forefront of his mind, pushed gently back to make space for May Welland, bright and radiant and uncomplicated, reclaiming her rightful place. He had written off the little whispers of her wanting a divorce as silly, unfounded gossip. Theoretically, the very idea of divorce sat as poorly with him as it did with his own mother, and he felt a flare of quiet irritation that old Mrs. Mingott had clearly prodded Letterblair to rope him into this business, before he was even formally a member of the Mingott clan by marriage. Surely there were plenty of Mingott men better suited to it. He waited for the older man to go on.

Mr. Letterblair unlocked a drawer and pulled out a sealed packet. “If you would run your eye over these papers —”

Archer frowned. “I beg your pardon, sir. But precisely because of that prospective family tie, I would far rather you turned this over to Mr. Skipworth or Mr. Redwood.”

Mr. Letterblair looked surprised, faintly offended. No junior ever turned down a favour of this sort from the head of the firm. “I respect your scruple, sir. But in this case, true delicacy demands that you take this on. The suggestion wasn’t mine, you see — it came from Mrs. Manson Mingott, and her son Lovell Mingott. I spoke to Mr. Welland, too. They all named you, specifically.”

Archer felt his temper rising. He had drifted along lazy and unresisting for the last fortnight, letting May’s soft, sweet beauty blot out the Mingott clan’s pressing, unspoken claims on him, but this new demand made him suddenly acutely aware of exactly what the family thought they were entitled to extract from a soon-to-be son-in-law, and he chafed at the assigned role.

“Her uncles ought to handle this,” he said.

“They have. The whole family has gone over the matter. They are firmly opposed to the Countess’s plan — but she refuses to back down, and insists on getting a formal legal opinion.”

Archer went quiet, the packet still unopened in his hand. “Does she intend to remarry?”

“Word has been passed around that that is the case, but she denies it.”

“Then —”

“Oblige me, Mr. Archer, by reading through these documents first. Once we have talked the case through, I will give you my full opinion.”

Archer left his superior’s office reluctantly, the unwelcome packet tucked under his arm. He had spent the past fortnight half-consciously working to shake free of the lingering burden of Madame Olenska. That quiet, intimate hour they had shared by her fireside had drawn them into a momentary closeness that the Duke of St. Austrey’s perfectly timed arrival, accompanied by Mrs. Lemuel Struthers, had providentially broken. Two days later, he had watched the farce of her being welcomed back into the van der Luydens’ good graces, and told himself, with a faint sharpness, that a woman so skilled at thanking the most powerful of New York’s elders for a bouquet of flowers had no need of a young man of his modest standing to champion her or offer her private comfort. That neat, detached perspective had simplified his own life immeasurably, shining a new, warm light on all the quiet domestic virtues he valued. He could not imagine May Welland, in any possible crisis, parading her private hurts about the world and pouring her confessions out to strange men, and she had never seemed finer, more perfect, than in the week that followed. He had even given in to her wish for a long engagement, after she gave him that one unanswerable, gentle reply to his pleas to marry soon.

“You know your parents have always let you have your way, ever since you were a tiny girl,” he had argued, and she had looked at him with that clear, soft smile of hers and said: “Yes. That’s why it feels so cruel to refuse them the very last little thing they will ever ask of me as their little girl.”

That was the true, old New York note, the kind of answer he had always wanted to know his wife would give. When you had breathed that rarefied, crystal air your whole life, anything less pure seemed stifling.

The papers he sat down to read told him very little on the surface, but they plunged him into an atmosphere so thick and ugly he felt as if he could barely breathe. They were mostly a back-and-forth of letters between Count Olenski’s solicitors and the French legal firm the Countess had approached to sort out her financial affairs, plus one short, brutal note from the Count himself to his wife. The second he finished it, Newland Archer jumped to his feet, stuffed all the papers back into their envelope, and walked straight back into Mr. Letterblair’s office.

“Here are the letters, sir. If you wish, I will go and see Madame Olenska,” he said, his voice tight and constrained.

“Thank you — thank you, Mr. Archer. Come dine with me tonight, if you are free, and we can go over the matter afterwards. That way, you can call on our client tomorrow, if you wish.”

Archer walked straight home that afternoon, no detours. It was a winter evening of perfect, transparent clarity, a soft pale new moon hanging innocent above the brownstone rooftops, and he wanted to breathe deep of that clear, cold radiance, not speak a single word to anyone until he and Mr. Letterblair were closeted together after dinner. There was no other possible choice he could have made. He had to see Madame Olenska himself, rather than let her most private, painful secrets be bared to the eyes of strangers. A great wave of compassion had swept away all his former indifference and irritation. She stood before his mind now as a raw, exposed, pitiable figure, who had to be saved at any cost from wounding herself further in her reckless fight against fate.

He remembered Mrs. Welland’s request, that the family be spared any hint of anything “unpleasant” in Ellen’s history, and winced, suddenly wondering if that very attitude was the thing that kept New York’s air so artificially pure. “Are we nothing but a crowd of Pharisees, after all?” he wondered, lost, struggling to reconcile his instinctive revulsion at the ugliness of the Count’s crimes with his equal instinct to pity her for her weakness.

For the first time, he realized how shockingly simple, how unexamined, his own core principles had always been. He had the reputation of being a young man unafraid of a little risk, and his secret brief affair with silly, vain little Mrs. Thorley Rushworth had never been quite secret enough, lending him a very becoming air of gentle adventure. But Mrs. Rushworth was “that sort of woman,” naturally clandestine, far more charmed by the thrill of sneaking around and the danger of being caught than she had ever been by any charm he possessed. When that truth had dawned on him it had nearly broken his heart, but now it felt like the only redeeming part of the whole affair. It was the exact kind of trivial, foolish dalliance nearly every young man of his acquaintance had gone through, walking away with perfectly clear consciences, still fully believing in the vast, uncrossable chasm between the women you loved and respected, and the women you enjoyed — and pitied. Their mothers, aunts, and all the other older women of their circle had carefully nurtured that belief, all sharing Mrs. Archer’s quiet certainty that when “such things” happened, the man was only foolish, but the woman was always, fundamentally, guilty. Every elderly lady Archer knew regarded any woman who fell in love imprudently as cunning and unscrupulous, and the poor simple man as helpless in her clutches. The only sensible thing to do was to get him married off to a nice girl as soon as possible, and trust her to keep him out of trouble.

Archer found himself guessing now, that love problems in the old, tangled communities of Europe could never be so simple, never so easily sorted into neat little categories. Rich, idle, decorative old societies must breed so many more messy, unfair situations like this one — situations where a naturally sensitive, reserved woman could, through nothing but utter defencelessness and overwhelming loneliness, get tangled up in a bond that conventional society would never, ever forgive.

When he got home, he scribbled a short note to the Countess Olenska, asking what hour the next day she might be free to see him, and sent it off with a messenger boy. The boy returned a short while later with a reply: she was leaving for Skuytercliff the next morning to spend the weekend with the van der Luydens, but he could find her home and all alone that very evening, right after dinner. The note was written on a slightly crumpled half-sheet of paper, no date, no address, but her handwriting was bold, firm, unapologetically free. He smiled to himself, amused by the thought of her spending a whole weekend in the stately, frigid silence of Skuytercliff — and then immediately felt a sharp twinge of sympathy. That, of all places, was where she would feel the chill of a dozen minds all deliberately averted, all determined not to see anything “unpleasant” at all.

He showed up at Mr. Letterblair’s house promptly at seven, glad of the excuse to slip away as soon as dinner was over. He had already formed his own full opinion from the papers he had been given, and did not particularly want to hash the whole thing out with his senior partner. Mr. Letterblair was a widower, so they dined alone, slowly and copiously, in a dark, shabby dining room hung with yellowing old prints of *The Death of Chatham* and *The Coronation of Napoleon*. On the sideboard, between two fluted Sheraton knife cases, stood a decanter of Haut Brion, and another of the famous old Lanning port — a gift from a client, that Tom Lanning the wastrel had sold off only a year or two before his mysterious, disgraceful death in San Francisco, a scandal the family had found far less humiliating than the sale of their entire wine cellar.

After a smooth, velvety oyster soup came shad and cucumbers, then a young broiled turkey served with corn fritters, followed by canvasback duck with currant jelly and celery mayonnaise. Mr. Letterblair, who only ever ate a sandwich and tea for lunch, took his dinner slowly and very seriously, and insisted his guest do the same. When the last of the courses were cleared away, the tablecloth removed, and their cigars lit, Mr. Letterblair leaned back in his chair, pushed the decanter of port toward Archer, and settled his back comfortably against the glowing coal fire behind him.

“The whole family is against this divorce. And I think they are entirely right.”

Archer felt himself bristle, instantly taking the opposite side of the argument. “But why, sir? If there was ever a case that justified it —”

“Well, what good would it do? She’s here, he’s there, the Atlantic is between them. She will never get a single extra dollar of her own money back than what he has already voluntarily given her. Those damned heathen European marriage settlements see to that. As things stand over there, Olenski has behaved generously. He could have turned her out of the house with nothing at all.”

Archer knew that was true, and fell silent.

“From what I understand, though,” Mr. Letterblair went on, “she doesn’t care a thing about the money. So as the family says, why not leave well enough alone?”

An hour before, Archer had walked into this house in full agreement with that exact sentiment. But hearing it spoken out loud by this well-fed, supremely indifferent, selfish old man, it suddenly turned into the very voice of the Pharisees, a society entirely focused on building walls to block out anything unpleasant.

“I think that ought to be her decision to make.”

“Hmm. Have you thought through what will happen if she does choose divorce?”

“You mean the threats in her husband’s letter? How much weight do those carry? It is nothing more than the rambling accusations of an angry blackguard.”

“Yes. But he might choose to formally contest the suit, and that would stir up a great deal of very unpleasant talk.”

“Unpleasant!” Archer burst out, before he could stop himself.

Mr. Letterblair lifted his eyebrows at him, curious. Archer, aware it was useless to try to explain the mess of tangled, roiling feelings in his head, bowed his head in apparent acquiescence as his senior went on. “Divorce is always unpleasant.”

“You agree with me, then?” Mr. Letterblair asked, after a short pause.

“Naturally,” Archer said.

“Well then. I can count on you. The Mingotts can count on you, to use your influence to talk her out of this idea?”

Archer hesitated. “I cannot promise you anything until I have spoken to the Countess Olenska,” he said finally.

“Mr. Archer, I do not understand you. Do you want to marry into a family with a messy, scandalous divorce suit hanging over its head?”

“I do not think that has any bearing on this case.”

Mr. Letterblair set down his port glass, fixing his young partner with a careful, wary stare. Archer knew he risked having his mandate to see Ellen taken away from him, and for some unnameable reason, he hated the thought. Now that the job had been put in his hands, he had no intention of giving it up. To make sure that did not happen, he knew he had to reassure this unimaginative old man, who was the Mingotts’ entire living legal conscience.

“You may rest assured, sir, I will not commit to any course of action before I report back to you. All I meant was that I would prefer not to give a final opinion until I have heard exactly what Madame Olenska has to say.”

Mr. Letterblair nodded approvingly, at that excess of caution perfectly in line with the very best old New York traditions. Archer glanced at his watch, pleaded a prior engagement, and took his leave, hurrying out into the crisp cold night, his heart already light with the quiet, unspoken thrill of seeing Ellen Olenska again.

Old-fashioned New York still dined sharp at seven o’clock, and the old custom of after-dinner calls—dismissed as dreadfully dowdy by Archer’s fast young set—lingered stubbornly across every brownstone thoroughfare. Crisp winter air nipped at Newland Archer’s cheeks as he strolled up Fifth Avenue from Waverley Place: the long, wide street was nearly empty save for a line of glossy carriages idling outside the Reggie Chiverses’ townhouse, waiting to ferry guests home after the grand dinner held in the visiting Duke’s honor, and the occasional elderly gentleman swaddled in heavy wool overcoats and silk mufflers, climbing marble steps to vanish into the warm glow of gas-lit front halls.

He spotted old Mr. du Lac turning in to call on his cousins the Dagonets as he crossed Washington Square, and a few steps further saw Mr. Skipworth, from his own law firm, heading purposefully for the Miss Lannings’ door. Halfway up Fifth, the bold, wealthy Julius Beaufort stepped out of his front doorway, darkly silhouetted against a blaze of parlor gaslight, and climbed into his private brougham to roll off toward a destination no respectable society matron would dare name. It was no opera night, no private party, so his outing was unmistakably clandestine. Archer’s mind drifted at once to the little little clapboard house beyond Lexington Avenue, the one that had lately sprung up with beribboned lace curtains and bright flower boxes on the sills, where Fanny Ring’s famous canary-yellow carriage was always seen lingering outside the freshly painted door.

Outside the tiny, tight-knit pyramid of Mrs. Archer’s social world lay a nearly unmapped quarter, populated by artists, musicians, and “people who wrote.” These ragtag scattered souls had never showed the slightest interest in being folded into old New York’s rigid social structure. Odd as their habits were, most were said to be perfectly respectable, but they preferred to keep to themselves. Medora Manson, back in her prosperous days, had tried to launch a “literary salon” — but it had died almost at once, when the literary crowd refused to step foot in it. Other hosts had tried the same trick, none more successfully than the eccentric Blenker household, ruled by a voluble, fervid matron and her three blowsy, imitative daughters, where you could rub elbows with Edwin Booth, Adelina Patti, the new Shakespearian sensation George Rignold, magazine editors, and leading literary and musical critics.

Mrs. Archer and her circle regarded these people with quiet, fluttery timidity. They were odd, unpredictable, with secrets hidden in the background of their lives and minds that no proper old New Yorker could ever parse. Literature and art were deeply respected in Archer’s set, of course; Mrs. Archer never tired of telling her children how much more gracious cultivated society had been back when it counted Washington Irving, Fitz-Greene Halleck, and the poet of *The Culprit Fay* as regular guests. Those celebrated authors of her youth had all been gentlemen — who knew if the unknown newcomers had gentlemanly hearts? Their origins, their unruly hair, their easy intimacy with the stage and the opera made every one of old New York’s unspoken rules of social labeling useless. “When I was a girl,” Mrs. Archer would sigh, “we knew every soul between the Battery and Canal Street, and only the people you knew had carriages. You could place anyone in five minutes. Now? I don’t even try.”

Only old Catherine Mingott, with her total lack of petty moral prejudices and her parvenu indifference to tiny social distinctions, could have bridged that gap — but she had never opened a book in her life, never looked twice at a painting, and cared for music only as a reminder of gala nights at the Italian opera in the days when she’d been feted at the Tuileries. Beaufort, bold as Mrs. Mingott and just as unbound by petty rules, might have pulled off the fusion too — but his grand marble townhouse and silk-stockinged footmen were too formal for the easy, casual sociability the bohemian crowd preferred. Worse, he was as illiterate as old Mrs. Mingott, and dismissed “fellows who wrote” as nothing more than paid entertainers there to amuse rich men. No one rich enough to change his mind had ever bothered to disagree.

Newland Archer had known all this his whole life, accepted it as a fundamental part of his world’s architecture. He had read travel essays and novels about far more glittering societies, where painters, poets, novelists, scientists, and even great actors were sought after as eagerly as foreign dukes. He had spent hours daydreaming of what it would be like to move through the drawing rooms of Paris and London, where the talk was dominated by Prosper Mérimée — whose *Lettres à une Inconnue* was one of his most tattered, well-loved possessions — Thackeray, Browning, William Morris. But such things were unthinkable in New York, and dwelling on them only made his small, familiar world feel shakier. He knew most of the local “fellows who wrote,” the musicians and the painters; he met them for drinks at the Century Club, or at the small new theatrical and musical cropping up across the city. He enjoyed their company there, but found them tedious at the Blenkers’ parties, where they were passed around between fervid, dowdy hostesses like exotic captured curiosities. Even after his most sparkling, exciting late-night talks with Ned Winsett, he always left feeling that his own world was small, and theirs was equally small — and the only way to make either bigger was to reach a place of easy, unguarded manners where the two groups merged naturally.

He was turning this thought over in his mind as he walked up to Countess Olenska’s door, and remembered the strange, disorienting way she had of turning all his long-held values upside down. He would have to wrap his head around a life far different from anything he’d ever known if he was going to help her through her current mess.

Nastasia, her maid, opened the door with a mysterious little smile. On the hall bench lay a sable-lined overcoat, a folded dull silk opera hat with a gold “J. B.” stitched into its lining, and a soft white silk muffler. There was no mistaking who they belonged to: Julius Beaufort.

Archer burned with sudden hot anger. He almost scribbled a note on his card and stormed right back out into the street — then remembered that in his last letter to Madame Olenska, he had been so careful of proprieties he never actually said he wanted to see her privately. He had no one but himself to blame if she had welcomed other callers. He stepped across the threshold, doggedly determined to make Beaufort feel like an unwanted third wheel, and outstay him.

The banker was leaning against her mantel, which was draped in an ancient piece of hand-embroidered tapestry held in place by brass candelabra stuck full of yellowish old church candles. He puffed out his chest, shoulders braced against the mantel, all his weight resting on one large polished patent leather foot. He was grinning down at his hostess when Archer walked in. Ellen Olenska sat on a sofa placed at a right angle to the chimney, half-reclining, her head propped on one hand, her wide sleeve falling back to leave her arm bare all the way to the elbow. A table heaped with flowers was set behind her like a screen; Archer recognized the orchids and azaleas at once, they were the signature expensive tributes from Beaufort’s own private hot-houses.

Every other proper lady receiving callers in the evening wore a stiff “simple dinner dress” — a whalebone-armored sheath of silk, slightly open at the neck with lace ruffles filling the gap, tight sleeves that flounced just far enough back to show a gold Etruscan bracelet or a velvet band at the wrist. But Madame Olenska ignored every rule of local tradition, dressed in a long clinging robe of deep red velvet, trimmed all around the chin and down the front with glossy black fur. Archer remembered seeing a portrait by the new Parisian painter Carolus Duran on his last trip abroad, the sensation of that year’s Salon, where a lady wore exactly that kind of bold, sheath-like dress, her chin nestled deep in fur. It was perverse, almost provocative, to wear fur in a heated drawing-room in the evening, pairing a swaddled, warm throat with a bare, cool arm — but the effect was breathtakingly lovely.

“Good Lord, three whole days at Skuytercliff!” Beaufort was drawling in his loud, sneering tone as Archer stepped in. “You’d better pack all your furs, and a hot-water bottle to boot.”

“Is the house that cold?” she asked, holding out her left hand to Archer in a way that made it clear she expected him to kiss it.

“Not the house,” Beaufort said, nodding carelessly at the young man in greeting. “The missus.”

“But I thought she was so kind!” Ellen protested. “She came to invite me herself. Granny says I absolutely must go.”

“Granny would say that, of course. And I say it’s a crying shame you’re going to miss the little oyster supper I planned for you next Sunday at Delmonico’s. Private room, Campanini and Scalchi, a whole crowd of jolly people — even a Steinway they’ll play for us all night.”

She glanced doubtfully from the banker to Archer. “Oh, that does tempt me! I haven’t met a single artist since I’ve been here, not even that night at Mrs. Struthers’s.”

“What kind of artists?” Archer cut in boldly. “I know one or two very good painters, decent fellows. I could bring them round to call on you, if you’d like.”

“Painters? Are there even painters in New York?” Beaufort scoffed, his tone implying no painter could possibly exist if he didn’t buy their work.

Madame Olenska smiled her grave, soft smile at Archer. “That would be charming. But I was really thinking of dramatic artists, singers, actors, musicians. My husband’s house was always full of them.”

She said the words “my husband” as if there were no dark, sinister associations clinging to them, her tone almost soft with longing for the lost, glittering delights of that married life. Archer stared at her, perplexed, wondering if this was lightheartedness or careful dissimulation — how could she touch that painful past so easily, when she was risking everything to break free of it?

“I do think,” she went on, speaking to both men, “that the unexpected adds so much to one’s joy. It’s such a mistake to see the same faces every single day.”

“It’s unutterably dull, anyhow,” Beaufort grumbled. “New York is dying of boredom. And just when I try to liven it up for you, you turn me down. Come, think better of it! Sunday’s your last chance. Campanini leaves for Baltimore and Philadelphia next week.”

“How delicious,” she said, amiable but with the faintest sharp hint of dismissal in her voice. “May I think it over, and write you my answer tomorrow morning?”

Beaufort felt it at once. No one ever dismissed him. He stared at her, a stubborn line forming between his brows. “Why not decide right now?”

“It’s too serious a question to settle this late,” she said, meeting his gaze coolly. “Because I still have a little business to discuss with Mr. Archer.”

“Ah,” Beaufort snapped. There was no arguing with that tone.
