He shrugged, recovered his composure, took her hand and kissed it with a practiced, effortless air, and called over his shoulder from the threshold: “I say, Newland, if you can persuade the Countess to stay in town, you’re welcome at the supper too.” He left the room, his heavy, important footsteps echoing down the hall.

For a second Archer thought Mr. Letterblair must have warned her he was coming — but her next, completely unrelated remark proved him wrong. “You know painters, then? You live in their world?” she asked, eyes bright with interest.

“Not exactly. I don’t think the arts have a proper world of their own here, any of them. It’s more like a very thinly settled outskirt on the edge of society.”

“But you care for those things?”

“More than I can say. Whenever I’m in Paris or London, I never miss an exhibition. I do my best to keep up.”

She looked down at the tip of her little satin boot peeking out from the hem of her long velvet dress. “I used to care immensely, too. My whole life was full of those things. But now I want to try not to.”

“Try not to?”

“Yes. I want to cast off all my old life, to become just like everybody else here.”

Archer’s face flushed hot. “You’ll never be like everybody else.”

She lifted her straight eyebrows a little. “Oh, don’t say that. If you knew how I hate being different!” Her face went sombre, as still and tragic as a carved mask. She leaned forward, thin hands clasped around her knee, gaze drifting past him into some dark, faraway memory. “I want to get away from all of it.”

“I know,” Archer said, clearing his throat. “Mr. Letterblair told me. That’s why I came. He asked me to handle this for you — you see, I’m part of the firm now.”

She looked faintly surprised, then her face lit up. “You mean you can manage it for me? I can talk this through with you instead of stuffy old Mr. Letterblair? Oh, that will be so much easier!”

Her soft tone touched him, and his confidence swelled. He realized she had mentioned “business” to Beaufort purely to get rid of him — routing Beaufort, the boldest man in New York, felt like a quiet, secret triumph. “I’m here to talk it all over,” he repeated.

She sat silent for a minute, her head still propped on the arm draped over the back of the sofa. Her face looked pale, almost dimmed, against the rich glow of her red dress. Suddenly she struck Archer as a terribly pathetic, even pitiful figure. Now we’re getting to the hard facts, he thought, and he felt that same instinctive recoil from messy, uncomfortable situations that he had spent years mocking in his mother and her friends. He had so little practice dealing with anything out of the ordinary! The very words for this kind of situation belonged to novels, to the stage, not to his quiet, predictable life. He felt as awkward and flustered as a schoolboy.

After a long pause, Madame Olenska spoke out with sudden, fierce vehemence: “I want to be free. I want to wipe every last trace of the past away.”

“I understand that.”

Her face softened, warming. “Then you’ll help me?”

“First —” he hesitated. “Perhaps I ought to know a little more.”

She looked surprised. “You know about my husband? About the life I lived with him?”

He nodded.

“Well then — what more is there to know? In this country, are such things even tolerated? I’m a Protestant — our church doesn’t forbid divorce in cases like mine.”

“Of course it doesn’t.”

Silence fell between them, and Archer could almost see the spectre of Count Olenski’s short, vicious letter grinning hideously between their two chairs. It was only half a page long, exactly what he had described it as to Mr. Letterblair: the vague, cruel accusations of an angry blackguard. But how much truth lay behind those words? Only Count Olenski’s wife could say.

“I’ve read all the papers you gave Mr. Letterblair,” he said at last.

“Well — can there be anything more abominable than what I’ve already told you?”

“No.”

She shifted a little, lifting a hand to shade her eyes from the firelight. “Of course you know,” Archer went on, “that if your husband chooses to fight the case — as he’s threatening to —”

“Yes?”

“He can say things — things that might be very unpleasant for you. He can say them publicly, in open court, so the stories spread far and wide, and they will damage you — even if every last word of them is completely untrue.”

“Even if?”

“I mean, no matter how unfounded the accusations are.”

She was quiet for so long that Archer, not wanting to stare at her shaded face, had time to memorize the exact shape of her other hand, the one resting on her knee, every tiny detail of the three rings on her fourth and fifth fingers. He noticed, with a little jolt, that her wedding ring was not among them.

“What harm could those kinds of accusations do to me here?”

He had been about to burst out My poor child — far more harm here than anywhere else in the whole world! But instead, his voice came out as dry and formal as old Mr. Letterblair’s. “New York society is a very small world, compared to the one you came from. And for all appearances, it’s ruled by a tiny handful of people with very, very old-fashioned ideas.”

She said nothing, so he kept going. “Our laws are lenient enough about divorce, but our social customs are not.”

“Never?”

“Not if the woman, no matter how injured, no matter how perfectly blameless she is, has even the faintest hint of unconventional action hanging over her, giving anyone the chance to spin ugly, offensive insinuations.”

She drooped her head a little lower. Archer waited, hoping desperately for a flash of indignation, or even a short, sharp cry of denial. Nothing came. A little travelling clock on the mantel ticked soft and purring by her elbow, and a log in the fire cracked in two, sending a shower of golden sparks up the chimney. The whole hushed, still room seemed to wait silently beside him.

“Yes,” she murmured at last. “That’s what my family has been telling me.”

“It’s not unnatural —” Archer began.

“Our family,” she corrected him gently, and Archer’s face burned. “You’re going to be my cousin soon, after all.”

“I hope so.”

“And you take their side?”

He stood up, wandered across the room, stared blankly at a painting hanging against the old red damask wall, then came back irresolutely to her side. How could he say Yes, if what your husband hints is true, or if you have no way of disproving it?

“Sincerely,” she prompted, when he hesitated to speak.

He stared down into the glowing embers of the fire. “Sincerely, then — what could you

The velvet-draped stalls of Wallack’s Theatre hummed with the kind of warm, packed energy that turns a regular show into a shared, unforgettable ritual that night. Dion Boucicault’s *The Shaughraun* was well into its peak popularity, every seat filled to the rafters: gallery audiences whooped and cheered unreservedly at every dramatic beat, while the society crowds in the boxes and stalls smiled knowingly at the familiar, overwrought lines and beloved tropes, and lost themselves in the story all the same.
There was one scene above all others that held the whole house breathless, floor to ceiling. It was the quiet parting between Harry Montague and Ada Dyas, where after a sparse, almost wordless exchange of goodbyes, the young lover turned for the door. Ada Dyas, standing framed by the stage mantel, her face tilted down toward the fake hearth fire, wore a plain gray cashmere gown no fashion plate would have praised—untrimmed, unlooped, moulded to her tall frame, the hem pooling soft at her feet. A narrow black velvet ribbon circled her throat, its two ends slipping loose down her back. When her suitor turned away, she braced her arms on the mantel shelf and buried her face in her hands. He paused on the threshold, staring back at her, then stole silently across the room, lifted one trailing end of that velvet ribbon, pressed a soft kiss to it, and slipped out the door before she could hear him move. The curtain fell on that still, silent frame, and the whole theatre exhaled as one.
Newland Archer had come to *The Shaughraun* half a dozen times just for that scene. He thought the pair’s wordless grief finer than any grand, overacted performance he’d ever seen in Paris or London, the quiet reticence of it stabbing deeper than the most thunderous theatrical monologue.
That night, though, the scene hit different. For no clear reason he could name, it pulled his thoughts straight back to his private meeting with Madame Olenska, ten days prior. There was no logical resemblance between the two situations: he was no handsome romantic stage lead, Ellen Olenska was nothing like the tall, pale, stately redhead playing Ada Dyas, and the two of them had not been star-crossed lovers parting—they had been a lawyer and his client, going over the terrible, unvarnished details of her petition for divorce.
But Ellen Olenska carried a strange, unnameable weight with her, a quiet aura of all the tragic, moving possibilities that lay far outside the tidy, predictable bounds of New York society. She never spoke a word that would draw attention to it, but it clung to her, as if she had been plucked straight from the roiling maelstrom of a scandalous European life so full of drama she had long ago stopped being surprised by its cruelties. Archer had always believed people drew fate to themselves far more than fate chose them, and he had sensed that quiet tendency in Ellen from the first moment they met: no matter how hard she shrank from conflict, no matter how carefully she tried to hide, trouble would find her.
When he had left her that day, he’d come away certain that Count Olenski’s cruel accusation was not unfounded: the “secretary” who had helped her escape her husband’s cruel household had likely been more to her than a rescuer. What could be more natural, after all, than for a young, terrified, desperate woman to be grateful to the person who pulled her from a life too awful to name? The tragedy was that that very gratitude, in the eyes of New York law and New York society, put her on the exact same moral level as the monstrous husband she had fled.
He had been bound to tell her the truth, that the simple, kindhearted New York family she’d counted on for mercy would never indulge her, that a divorce would drag every last private, messy secret of hers out for the world to gawk at. To watch her accept that fact with quiet, unprotesting resignation had gutted him. He felt drawn to her by a tangled mess of pity and sharp, unnameable jealousy, as if that small, humbling secret she’d laid at his feet had made her soft and vulnerable, impossible to turn away from. He’d told the whole family she’d dropped her divorce plans of her own accord, no messy scandal for them to endure, and they had all sighed in relief: Mrs. Welland bragged endlessly about her clever future son-in-law, and old Mrs. Mingott had cackled and called Ellen a silly little goose for wanting to pretend she was a plain old maid instead of a married countess.
By the time the curtain fell that night, Newland’s eyes were stinging with tears, and he stood at once to leave, to carry that tender, silent parting image home with him before it faded. As he turned to make his way up the aisle, he glanced back toward the boxes, and his breath caught. There she was, Ellen Olenska, seated in the Beaufort box surrounded by Lawrence Lefferts, Sillerton Jackson, and a handful of other familiar faces.
They had not spoken alone since their meeting, and he had been deliberately avoiding her in public, but their eyes locked the second he turned, and Mrs. Beaufort gave him that lazy, perfect little gesture of invitation she reserved for her favorite guests. There was no polite way to refuse. He squeezed past Beaufort and Lefferts to take a seat directly behind Ellen, while Jackson murmured a long, confidential story to Mrs. Beaufort about Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s recent scandalous Sunday reception, where gossip swore guests had dared to dance. No one was paying them the slightest mind.
Ellen turned, her voice low and soft enough that only he could hear it, and nodded toward the dark, empty stage. “Do you think he’ll send her a bunch of yellow roses tomorrow morning?”
Newland’s face burned, his heart leaping straight into his throat. He had sent her two unmarked boxes of yellow roses, no card included, after each of his first two visits to her home. He’d been fully certain she’d never guessed they were from him. Now she had named the flowers, tied that small, secret gift to the tender, wordless kiss the actor had pressed to the velvet ribbon, and he was flooded with a giddy, agitated joy he could not name.
“I was just thinking that exact thing,” he said. “I was about to leave right then to carry that picture away with me before it faded.”
A soft, dusky blush rose to her cheeks, almost as if she was fighting not to let it show. She twisted the mother-of-pearl opera glass in her smoothly gloved hands, and paused for a long beat before asking, “What do you do, while May is away?”
He felt a faint, irrational prickle of annoyance, but explained: the Wellands had left town the week prior for St. Augustine, per the unshakable annual ritual built around Mr. Welland’s “delicate bronchial tubes.” The quiet, mild-mannered patriarch could not find his own hairbrushes or stamp his own letters without his wife’s guidance, and the whole adoring family would never dream of letting him travel south alone, even though May’s two lawyer brothers could not leave New York in the winter to join him till Easter. The engagement had been announced earlier than planned, but no one would ever dare disrupt Mr. Welland’s routine. Newland had daydreamed of joining them, of lazy weeks boating in the Florida sunshine with his betrothed, but he knew the entire Mingott clan would label him frivolous if he asked for a mid-winter holiday, and he had already accepted that quiet, unprotesting resignation would be the backbone of his married life.
Ellen looked up at him from beneath lowered lids, her voice soft and abrupt. “I did what you advised. I dropped the idea.”
“Ah— I’m so glad,” he fumbled, embarrassed that she would bring up such a private, heavy subject in a crowded theatre box.
“You were right,” she went on, her breath catching a little. “But sometimes life is so hard. So perplexing.”
“I know,” he said.
“I wanted you to know that I’m grateful. That I see you were right,” she finished, lifting her opera glass quickly to her eyes just as Beaufort’s loud, resonant laugh boomed across the box, breaking the quiet bubble around them.
Newland stood up at once, and left the box, left the theatre entirely, the sharp cold New York night air stinging his hot cheeks. Only the day before, he had received a letter from May, so bright and candid, begging him to be kind to Ellen while they were away. She had written that Ellen admired him more than anyone, that none of the family saw how lonely she was, that Granny and the uncles all thought she craved nothing but fancy dinners and new gowns, and did not understand she missed the music, art, and clever conversation she had been surrounded by in Europe, the exact kind of things Newland loved too. He had thought he would ignore that request, determined as an engaged man not to draw too much attention to Ellen, convinced she had more than enough protectors in Beaufort, the van der Luydens, and a dozen other fawning society men. But every time he spoke to her, he realized May’s sweet naivety held a kind of quiet divination.
Ellen Olenska was lonely. She was unhappy. And no one else in all of New York seemed to see it.

The frosty hush of the opera lobby wrapped around Newland Archer as he stepped out of the auditorium, and the first face he recognized through the crowd of fur-collared, top-hatted patrons was that of Ned Winsett—his only friend among what his sister Janey called his "clever set" who cared to dig past the lazy, banal chatter of club smoking rooms and chop-house dinners to talk about things that actually mattered. He’d caught a glimpse of Winsett’s shabby, round-shouldered back across the opera house earlier, and had noticed the journalist’s dark, curious eyes lingering on the Beaufort box where Countess Ellen Olenska sat.

The two men shook hands, and Winsett proposed they duck around the corner to a little German café for a cold bock. Archer, too tangled up in thoughts of Ellen to slip into the usual meandering conversation, declined on the excuse that he had work waiting for him at home. "Oh, fine, so do I," Winsett laughed. "I’ll be the Industrious Apprentice right alongside you." They strolled side by side down the lamplit sidewalk, and after a minute Winsett leaned in, curious. "Look, the real thing I wanted to ask—who’s that dark lady in that fancy Beaufort box? The one your friend Lefferts was practically drooling over?"

A sharp, unaccountable flicker of annoyance pricked Archer. Why was Ned Winsett, a man who never bothered with society gossip, asking after Ellen Olenska? And why tie her name to Lawrence Lefferts of all people? "You’re not after an interview for the papers, I hope?" he joked, a little too sharp.

"Not for the press, for me," Winsett said. "She’s my neighbor, you see—funny sort of neighborhood for a woman that beautiful to land in. My little boy was chasing his kitten down our front area the other day, tripped and cut his knee to ribbons. She came rushing out bareheaded, scooped him right up, carried him inside and bandaged him all gentle, so warm and sweet that my wife was too flustered to even ask her name."

A soft, warm glow spread through Archer’s chest. Any woman might have helped a hurt child, but it was so perfectly Ellen to not care a fig for her bonnet or what the neighbors might think, to rush in unthinking and kind, and leave the poor Winsetts too dazzled to remember to get her name. "That’s the Countess Olenska. She’s old Mrs. Mingott’s granddaughter."

Winsett whistled. "A countess? I never would’ve guessed countesses were that neighborly. The rest of the Mingotts certainly aren’t."

"They would be, if you gave them half a chance," Archer smiled, and the two slipped into their old familiar, unresolvable argument about how the city’s intellectual set deliberately avoided the upper crust, even as both of them knew there was no point in hashing it out again. "I wonder how a countess ends up living in our scrappy little block," Winsett mused.

"Because she doesn’t care a hang about any of our silly little social signposts, where the rest of us tiptoe around every rule," Archer said, a quiet, private pride curling in his chest.

"Huh. Seen far bigger, fancier places, I expect," Winsett shrugged. "Well, this is my corner." He slouched off across Broadway, leaving Archer standing on the curb, staring after him, turning that last line over in his head. Ned Winsett always had those sudden, sharp flashes of insight—they were the best part of him, and they always made Archer wonder why a man that bright had settled for quiet, uncomplaining failure when most men his age were still fighting to make something of themselves.

Archer had always known Winsett had a wife and son, but he’d never met them. They always ran into each other at the Century Club, or at those tiny, smoky restaurants full of journalists and actors. Winsett had mentioned his wife was an invalid, which might have been true, or might just have meant she had no taste for performative social niceties, or no nice evening gowns—very possibly both. Winsett loathed every stuffy social ritual with a ferocity Archer found almost ridiculous, but he still felt invigorated every time he talked to the man.

Winsett wasn’t a journalist by choice. He was a man born to write beautiful, delicate literary criticism, a hundred years too late for anyone to care. He’d published one small volume of essays once—only 120 copies sold, 30 given away, the rest pulped by the publishers to make room for more profitable books—before he’d given up and taken a dead-end sub-editor job at a women’s weekly called *Hearth-Fires*, where fashion plates and paper patterns ran side by side with saccharine New England love stories and ads for temperance tonics. He was endlessly funny complaining about the paper, but under the jokes there was a quiet, bitter emptiness of a man who’d given up on his dream too soon. Their conversations always left Archer staring down at the shape of his own life, all soft and comfortable and carefully planned, wondering how much of it he was actually living.

"Life doesn’t fit either of us," Winsett had told him once. "I’m down and out, nothing to be done about it. I’ve only got one thing I can make, and no one’s buying it, not in my lifetime. But you? You’re free, you’ve got money, you’ve got all the chances I never did. Why don’t you actually do something? Get into politics. That’s the only way."

Archer had laughed, amused. Everyone in his world knew a gentleman didn’t sully himself with American politics. He couldn’t say that outright to Winsett, though, so he dodged: "Look at what happens to honest men in American politics! They don’t want us there."

"Who’s ‘they’? Why don’t all of you band together and be ‘they’ yourselves?"

Archer’s smile had softened into a little condescending one. There was no point arguing. Everyone knew what happened to the few gentlemen foolish enough to dip their nice clean linen shirts into New York’s municipal politics. The day for that sort of thing was long gone, the city run by party bosses and immigrant constituents, leaving decent, respectable folks to retreat into sport, or culture, or the quiet, predictable rut of their inherited lives.

"Culture? If only we had real culture," Winsett had pushed back. "We’ve just got a few tiny, dying little pockets of it, left over from what your ancestors brought over from Europe, no tending, no competition, no audience. You’re all just portraits of gentlemen hanging on the walls of an empty, deserted house. You’ll never be anything at all until you roll up your sleeves and wade right into the muck. Or emigrate… God, if I could emigrate."

Archer had shrugged it off, turned the conversation back to books. Emigrate? A gentleman didn’t abandon his own country, any more than he waded into the grime of party politics. A gentleman stayed home, and kept himself apart. But even as he thought it, he’d realized that the bohemian, glittering little literary circles he moved in were no bigger, no less monotonous than the staid world of Fifth Avenue society—just two different tiny boxes, running on the same invisible rails.

The next morning, Archer tramped all over Manhattan searching for yellow roses, the bright, sunlit blooms Ellen had paused beside at the theatre, and came up empty. He arrived late to his stuffy law office, run by Mr. Letterblair, where most of the work was managing old wealthy families’ vast estates and "conservative" investments, and noticed no one even cared he was late. A sharp, hot exasperation bubbled up in his chest at the sheer useless pointlessness of the life he’d been set to inherit. Why wasn’t he on the sands of St. Augustine right now, with his fiancée May Welland? No one was fooled by his pretense of serious professional ambition. All the other young, well-off men at the firm did the same trivial little tasks, or sat at their desks reading the newspapers all day. The law was a respectable, gentlemanly profession, but none of them really wanted to advance in it, and Archer could already see the soft, green mould of routine creeping over all of their lives, slowly smothering any spark of real passion. He shuddered to think it might be creeping over him, too.

He sent a messenger with a note to Ellen Olenska’s house that afternoon, asking if he might call, begging her to leave her reply for him at his club. But there was no note waiting there, not that day, not the next. The silence stung him far more than it should have, and when he passed a florist’s window the following morning draped in a perfect cluster of yellow roses, he walked right past, leaving them there.

It wasn’t until the third day that her letter came, postmarked Skuytercliff—the van der Luydens’ remote, exclusive upstate estate, where visitors were rarely invited, and even the most privileged got no more than a chilly weekend stay.

"I ran away," she wrote, no fancy polite opening, no flowery preamble, the words as blunt and unguarded as she was. "The day after I saw you at the play. These kind friends the van der Luydens took me in. I wanted quiet, to think things through. You were right to tell me how kind they are. I feel so safe here. I wish that you were with us." She signed off with a spare, conventional "Yours sincerely," with no mention at all of when she planned to return to the city.

Archer stared at the note, his heart thrumming. What was Ellen running from? Why did she need to feel safe? His first silly thought was that some dark threat from her terrible old European husband had followed her across the ocean, then he laughed at himself. She was probably exaggerating, the way all women did, and her English was still a little stilted, like she was translating every word from French. *Je me suis évadée*—I escaped—might not mean anything more than that she’d run away from the endless, suffocating round of social calls and dinners society kept pushing on her.

He thought of how the van der Luydens, so rarely willing to open their gilded doors to anyone, had swept Ellen off to their private estate after rescuing her from the cold shoulders of society, just like the kind man in that French play he’d seen in Paris who kept rescuing the young stranger he’d pulled out of a glacier. They’d made rescuing Ellen their little gentle, stubborn mission now.

A sharp, bright disappointment coursed through him when he realized she was miles away from the city—then he remembered, all at once, that he’d turned down an invitation to spend the coming Sunday with the Reggie Chiverses at their house on the Hudson, barely a few miles south of Skuytercliff. He’d had his fill of their loud, boisterous parties, full of sleigh rides, ice boating, silly mild jokes and meaningless little flirting, and had planned to spend the quiet Sunday at home with the new stack of books his London bookseller had just sent him. But now, he walked straight to the club’s writing room, scribbled a hasty telegram to Mrs. Reggie, and told the servant to send it off immediately. He knew she’d never mind a last-minute change of plans—there was always an extra guest room waiting in her big, sprawling house. He didn’t stop to unpack what he was doing, the quiet, unnameable pull drawing him miles out of the city, just to be close to the place Ellen Olenska was staying.

Newland Archer arrived at the Chiverses’ door at Highbank on that crisp Friday evening, and for the whole of Saturday he moved through the expected week-end rituals as if wading through warm honey, half his attention already drifting far from the crowd around him. He spun across frozen lake ice in the hostess’s sail boat with the other hardy, red-faced guests, the wind stinging his cheeks until his eyes watered, and he scarcely registered the thrill of the speed. He tramped the farm paths with Reggie Chivers in the afternoon, nodding politely through long, meandering lectures on horse conformation in the elaborately carved stalls of the stables, not hearing a single word of the technical jargon. After tea, he sat in a shadowed corner of the fire-lit hall listening to a young society girl gush over her own upcoming marriage, the same girl who’d cried publicly that his engagement to May would break her heart only a month prior. By midnight, he was drawn into the usual silly pranks: tucking a goldfish bowl into a visiting aunt’s bed, dressing a friend up as a bumbling burglar to jump out and scare that same nervous old aunt in the bathroom, and tumbling through a raucous pillow fight that spilled from the top-floor nurseries all the way down to the basement.

By Sunday, the second the last fork was set down after luncheon, he borrowed a sharp little one-horse cutter and urged the mare into a fast trot toward Skuytercliff.

The old Van der Luyden estate had always been passed off as a proper Italian villa to New York society, a story believed just as strongly by many travelers who’d actually visited Italy as those who’d never left the Hudson valley. It was a great square wooden house, tongued-and-groove walls painted soft pale green and white, topped with a grand Corinthian portico, fluted pilasters standing stiff between every window. From the high rise of land it stood on, a series of balustraded terraces dotted with marble urns sloped down in the stiff, formal style of a steel engraving to a small, irregular lake edged in asphalt, overhung with rare, drooping evergreen trees. On either side, famously weedless lawns dotted with “specimen” trees each of a distinct, exotic variety rolled away toward stretches of long grass crowned with ornate cast-iron ornaments, and far below in a huddle at the bottom of the hill sat the tiny four-room stone cottage the very first Patroon had built on the land granted to him in 1612.

Against the unbroken sheet of winter snow and the soft grey overcast sky, the so-called Italian villa loomed almost grim, severe even in high summer when no bright coleus bed ever dared to plant itself closer than thirty feet to its intimidating front door. When Archer rang the bell, the high, clear tinkle echoed so far down empty halls it felt as if he’d rung at the door of a mausoleum. The butler who finally pulled the heavy oak open looked as shocked as if he’d been woken from his own grave.

Luckily, Archer was considered family, so no questions were raised at his unannounced arrival, and the butler informed him Countess Olenska was out, having driven to afternoon church service with Mrs. Van der Luyden exactly three quarters of an hour prior. “Mr. Van der Luyden is in, sir,” the butler added, “but I believe he is just finishing his nap, or else catching up on yesterday’s Evening Post. He said after morning service he intended to read the paper all through luncheon. If you’d like, sir, I can tiptoe to the library door and listen to see if he’s awake—”

Archer thanked him and waved the offer away, saying he would simply walk out to meet the ladies on their way back. The butler looked so obviously relieved not to have to disturb his employer that he closed the door behind Archer with a stately, grand finality.

A groom led the cutter away to the stables, and Archer cut straight through the snow-dusted park toward the main high road. Skuytercliff village was only a mile and a half away, but he knew Mrs. Van der Luyden never walked a step if she could ride, so he kept to the roadway to intercept their carriage. Before long, however, he spotted a small, slight figure in a bright red cloak coming down a footpath that crossed the highway, a great shaggy dog bounding ahead of her through drifts. He hurried forward, and Madame Olenska stopped short, a bright, surprised smile spreading across her face.

“Ah, you’ve come!” she said, pulling her hand out of her fur muff to meet his.

The red cloak made her look gay and vivid, the wild, untamed Ellen Mingott he’d remembered as a bright, mischievous little girl. He laughed as he wrapped his gloved fingers around hers. “I came to see what you were running away from.”

Her face clouded over for a heartbeat, but she answered, “Ah, well—you will see, presently.”

Puzzled, he leaned a little closer. “Wait—do you mean whoever it is has already caught up to you?”

She shrugged her shoulders with a small, familiar movement he’d once seen her Russian maid make back at her New York home, then laughed to lighten the tone. “Shall we walk on? My feet are half frozen after that long sermon. And what does it matter, now you’re here to protect me?”

Warm blood rushed up to his temples, and he caught a loose fold of her cloak. “Ellen—what is it? You have to tell me.”

“Oh, not yet—let’s run a race first, my toes are freezing solid to the ground,” she cried, gathering up the hem of her cloak and darting off across the snow, the dog leaping around her, barking playfully as he chased her heels. Archer stood still for a moment, watching that flash of red burn bright against the endless white, delight warming his chest, before he broke into a run after her. They collided, panting and laughing, at a small wicket gate leading back into the park’s grounds.

She looked up at him, cheeks pink from the cold, and smiled. “I knew you’d come!”

“That only proves you wanted me to,” he returned, absurdly, disproportionately giddy over their silly, meaningless banter. The bare boughs of the snow-laden trees glittered around them, spilling a soft, mysterious brightness through the air, and the packed snow under their boots seemed to hum softly as they walked.

“Where did you come from?” Madame Olenska asked him.

He told her about the Chiverses’ week-end, and added, “I came because I got your note.”

There was a faint, perceptible chill in her voice when she spoke again. “May asked you to take care of me.”

“I didn’t need any asking.”

“You mean—I’m so obviously helpless and defenseless? What a sad, pitiable thing you must all think me! But none of the women here ever seem to need protecting, the way you’d need it if the world was chasing you. They’re as safe and unneeding as the blessed souls in heaven.”

He lowered his voice, leaning in a little. “What kind of need are you talking about, Ellen?”

“Ah, don’t ask me! I don’t speak your language,” she retorted, a little petulant.

The words hit him like a sharp blow, and he froze in the path, staring down at her. “Then what am I doing here, if I don’t speak yours?”

“Oh, my friend—” She laid her hand lightly on his arm, and he pleaded earnestly, “Ellen—why won’t you tell me what’s happened, what’s bothering you?”

She shrugged again. “Does anything ever happen in heaven, anyway?”

They walked on in silence for a few dozen yards, until she spoke. “I will tell you—but where? Where on earth can we talk? That great big house up there is like a seminary, all the doors flung wide open, every minute a servant popping in with tea, or a new log for the fire, or a newspaper. Is there nowhere in an American house where a person can be alone for five minutes? You’re all so shy about showing emotion, and yet every single part of your lives is on public display. I keep feeling like I’m back in the convent again— or on a theater stage, standing in front of a dreadfully polite audience that never claps.”

“Ah, you don’t like us!” Archer exclaimed, stung.

They were just passing the tiny old Patroon cottage now, its squat stone walls and small square windows clustered tight around a central chimney. The shutters were propped wide open, and through one of the newly washed panes, Archer caught the warm glow of a kitchen fire.

“Why—this little house is open!” he said.

Madame Olenska stopped short. “Only for today, at least. I wanted to see it, so Mr. Van der Luyden had them light a fire and air it out this morning, so we could stop in on our way back from church.” She ran up the low steps and jiggled the door handle. It turned freely. “It’s still unlocked—how lucky! Come in, we can talk in peace. Mrs. Van der Luyden drove over to visit her elderly aunts in Rhinebeck, no one will notice we’re gone back at the big house for at least an hour.”

He followed her through the narrow little front passage, and his earlier melancholy lifted in an irrational leap of joy. The cozy little cottage felt like it had been conjured by magic just for them, its dark wood panels and polished brass fixtures glinting in the firelight. A great bed of embers still glowed in the kitchen hearth under an iron pot hanging from an ancient iron crane. Two rush-bottomed armchairs faced each other across the tiled hearth, rows of hand-painted Delft plates lined shelves nailed to the walls. Archer leaned down to toss a new log on the embers, sending sparks dancing up the chimney.

Madame Olenska dropped her red cloak on a chair and sank into one of the armchairs by the fire. Archer leaned against the mantel, looking down at her. “You’re laughing now, but when you wrote me that note, you sounded so unhappy,” he said.

“Yes.” She paused, her eyes soft in the firelight. “But I can’t feel unhappy when you’re here.”

“I won’t be here long,” he returned, his lips going stiff with the effort to hold himself back, to not say the words he’d been turning over in his head for weeks.

“No; I know. But I’m a foolish, improvident person— I only live for the moment when I’m happy.”

The words stole through him like a temptation he had no defense against, and he stepped away from the hearth to stare hard out the front window at the dark tree trunks standing out against the white snow. Even with his back half turned, he could still see her, leaning forward over the fire with that soft, lazy smile of hers, between him and the glass. His heart was beating so hard it felt like it would burst right through his ribs. What if she had been running away not from a man, but from him? What if she had waited to tell him that, right here in this secret little room, just the two of them?

“Ellen, if I’m really a help to you—if you truly wanted me to come—tell me what’s wrong, tell me what it is you’re running away from,” he insisted, not moving from his spot by the window, not even turning to look at her. If that unspoken, impossible thing between them was going to happen, it would happen this way, with the whole width of the small room between them, his eyes still fixed on the snow outside.

For a long moment she was silent, and in that quiet, Archer could almost imagine her, could almost feel her, stealing up behind him to wrap her light, soft arms around his neck. He waited, every nerve thrumming with the miracle he thought was about to happen, when his eyes caught sight of a heavily coated man with his fur collar turned all the way up to his ears, tramping up the snowy path toward the cottage.

It was Julius Beaufort.

Archer burst out in a sharp, bitter laugh.

Madame Olenska jumped to her feet and darted to his side, slipping her hand tight into his, but one glance through the window made her face go deathly pale, and she shrank back a step.

“So that was what you were running from?” Archer said, his voice sharp and derisive.

“I didn’t know he was here,” Madame Olenska murmured, her hand still clinging to his. But Archer pulled gently away from her, walked out into the passage, and threw the cottage front door wide open.

“Hallo, Beaufort—over here! Madame Olenska was just expecting you,” he called.

On his train ride back to New York the next morning, Archer re-lived every last minute of that hour at Skuytercliff, the memory wearing on him so sharply he felt exhausted. Beaufort, clearly annoyed to find Archer alone with Madame Olenska, handled the situation with his usual brash, high-handed ease. His habit of simply ignoring people who inconvenienced him made Archer feel almost invisible, like a ghost walking beside them as the three of them tramped back through the snow toward the big house. Humiliating as that invisibility was to his pride, it let him watch and observe without being noticed. Beaufort was smiling and casual as ever, but the deep vertical line between his eyebrows refused to smooth out. It was painfully clear Madame Olenska had not known he was coming, even if she’d hinted to Archer that someone might turn up; she’d obviously not told him where she was going when she left New York, and her unexplained disappearance had left him seething with frustration. His stated reason for showing up was that he’d stumbled on a “perfect little house” the night before, not even listed for sale, that was exactly the right size for her, that someone else would snap it up instantly if she didn’t put an offer in that day. He complained loudly, mock-reproaching her for leading him on such a wild goose chase running off to the country right when he’d found the perfect place for her.

“If only this newfangled telephone they keep talking about was a little bit more reliable, I could have told you all this from town, and been toasting my feet in front of my club fire right now, instead of tramping through all this snow after you,” he grumbled, hiding his very real irritation under a veneer of playful complaining. At that, Madame Olenska smoothly steered the conversation to the absurd, fantastic idea that one day people might be able to talk to each other across the streets, or even — impossible as it sounded — across entire states. The three of them traded references to Edgar Allan Poe and Jules Verne, making small, safe platitudes about the wild new invention, and the topic of the telephone carried them all the way back to the big villa without any more awkward moments.

Mrs. Van der Luyden had not yet returned when they got back. Archer said his goodbyes and walked off to fetch his cutter, while Beaufort followed Madame Olenska indoors. Archer knew perfectly well the Van der Luydens barely tolerated unannounced visitors, but Beaufort would no doubt wheedle an invitation to stay for dinner, then catch the nine o’clock train back to the city. There was no chance he’d be asked to spend the night; the Van der Luydens would never imagine a gentleman would turn up to their estate with no luggage and expect a bed, and they would never offer such hospitality to someone on such limited, formal terms as Beaufort. Beaufort knew all that, too, so the fact that he’d made the long, cold journey just for that tiny sliver of time alone with Ellen showed just how impatient and determined he was. There was no question he was openly chasing the Countess Olenska. His dull, childless home had bored him for years, and on top of his other long-term affairs, he was always hunting for a new amorous adventure among the families of New York high society. That was the man Ellen was running from. The only question left hanging in Archer’s mind was whether she’d fled because his advances disgusted her, or because she didn’t trust herself to resist them — or if all her talk of running away had just been a trick, and she’d left New York intentionally to meet him.

Archer didn’t truly believe the last part. Even though he’d only known Ellen for a few weeks, he thought he could read the shifts in her face, the nuances of her voice, and both had shown real, unfeigned dismay when Beaufort appeared out of the snow. But then again, he thought bitterly, wasn’t that worse? If she’d run away just to meet him, she’d be no more interesting than any other silly woman sneaking around for an affair. But if she knew Beaufort was vulgar, uneducated, and arrogant with his money, and still found herself drawn to him anyway, because he knew the wider world she’d come from, because he didn’t care about all the stupid, petty prejudices that bound New York society tight — how could any woman who’d lived across two continents not feel that pull? Ellen had told him, angry, that they didn’t speak the same language, and Archer knew that was true. But Beaufort spoke her language perfectly, his whole worldview just a coarser, cruder version of the same life she’d lived with her husband in Europe. Archer knew she thought she’d turned her back on all that old, messy European life — but the parts that had charmed her there would still charm her, even against her will.

He walked home through the next days turning the facts over in his head, with painful, impartial clarity, making the case for why Ellen might be drawn to Beaufort anyway. The urge to pull her aside, to warn her, to enlighten her about what Beaufort really was, burned hot in his chest, and more than once he imagined all she wanted was for him to be the one to pull her away from that temptation.

That night, he unpacked the crate of new books he’d ordered from London. There was a new volume of Herbert Spencer’s essays, a new collection of Alphonse Daudet’s sharp, sparkling short stories, and a novel called *Middlemarch* that all the literary reviews had been raving about lately. He’d turned down three separate dinner invitations just to spend the evening curled up with his new books, but though he turned the pages with the soft, loving delight of a lifelong book lover, he couldn’t absorb a single paragraph. Book after book slipped from his fingers onto the couch. Then, under the stack, he found a small thin volume of poetry he’d ordered just because the name on the spine had caught his eye: *The House of Life*. He picked it up, and immediately he was plunged into an atmosphere no

The pale warm sand of St. Augustine’s main street swallowed the soles of Archer’s leather boots as he strode toward the house the local livery hand had pointed out as the Wellands’ winter rental, and the second he spotted May Welland lingering under a broad-leafed magnolia, sun tangled thick in her loose fair hair, he kicked himself for wasting so many weeks cooped up behind his Manhattan desk. This was it: the unvarnished, warm reality he’d been craving, the life that was meant to be his, and he’d hidden away out of a stupid, cowardly fear of what his stuffy social set might mutter about him stealing an unplanned holiday.

Her first exclamation was soft, surprised: “Newland—has anything happened?” He half-joked to himself that a more “naturally feminine” girl would have read the raw, aching longing in his eyes the second he rounded the corner, but the words slipped out before he could catch them: “Yes—I found I had to see you.” The pink flush that bloomed across her sun-kissed cheeks chased the chill right off her surprise, and he knew he was already forgiven. Even mild, proper Mr. Letterblair’s faint disapproval would melt away fast under the Wellands’ gentle, tolerant roof.

It was still far too early for formal public displays of affection on the busy main street, where linen-clad tourists trundled past in mule-drawn buggies, and Archer ached to pull May aside somewhere private to pour out every last scrap of his pent-up tenderness and impatience. They still had an hour before the Wellands kept their famously late breakfast, so instead of inviting him inside, May suggested they walk out to the abandoned old orange grove past the town limits. She’d just come back from a row on the river, and the golden sun that stitched little sparks across the wave tops seemed to have wrapped her in its light too. Strands of wind-tousled hair across her warm brown cheek glinted like fine silver wire, and her eyes looked softer, paler, bright with the limpid clarity of youth. She walked beside him with that loose, long, unhurried stride of hers, her face wearing the calm, uncomplicated serenity of a young marble statue of an athlete, and to Archer’s frayed, overstretched nerves the sight was as soothing as the wide blue Florida sky and the slow, lazy drift of the river.

They settled on a weathered slat bench under the orange trees, heavy with tiny green unripe fruit that scented the whole air with soft blossom, and he looped an arm around her waist to kiss her. It felt like drinking from a cold, clear spring dappled with sunlight—but his hold was more desperate, more vehement than he’d meant it to be, and the blood rushed to her face so fast she drew back like he’d startled her. “What is it?” he laughed, and she blinked at him, confused, and said “Nothing.” A faint awkwardness settled between them, her hand slipping out of his, and he realized this was only the second time they’d kissed on the lips, after that furtive, quick embrace in the Beaufort’s conservatory weeks before. She was flustered, knocked clean out of that cool, boyish composure she usually carried so easily.

“Tell me what you do all day out here,” he said, tipping his head back and pushing his hat down over his eyes to block the sun’s glare. Letting her ramble about small, familiar things let his own tangled thoughts unspool unhurriedly, and he sat half-listening as she gushed about swims in the shallow clear inlet, bareback pony rides, and the occasional small dance at the rickety waterfront inn when a navy man-of-war docked in the harbor. A cluster of pleasant families from Philadelphia and Baltimore were staying at the inn for the winter, and the Selfridge Merrys had come down for three weeks because Kate Merry had come down with bronchitis. They were trying to lay a lawn tennis court out on the sand, but only Kate and May owned racquets, and most of their new friends had never even heard of the game. She’d been so busy, she said, she’d barely had time to flip through the little vellum copy of *Sonnets from the Portuguese* he’d sent her the week before—but she’d memorized every line of “How They Brought the Good News from Ghent to Aix,” because it was one of the first poems he’d ever read to her, and she laughed as she told him Kate Merry had never even heard of Robert Browning.

Presently she jumped up, exclaiming they’d be late for breakfast, and they hurried back to the tumble-down little rental house, with its lopsided pointless porch and the unpruned hedge of deep blue plumbago and bright pink geraniums spilling over the front walk. Mr. Welland hated the slovenly discomfort of the local southern hotels, so every winter Mrs. Welland went to immense trouble and expense to cobble together a household half made up of discontented New York servants, half hired from local Black families, to make sure her husband never felt like he was away from his own home. “The doctors say he’ll never get better if he doesn’t feel settled,” she explained to every sympathetic new winter visitor, and Mr. Welland, beaming across a breakfast table piled high with every fancy delicacy he could have ordered from New York, was soon teasing Archer: “You see, my dear fellow, we’re camping—literally camping. I keep telling the girls I’m trying to teach them how to rough it.”

The Wellands were just as surprised as May by his sudden unannounced arrival, but Archer had explained he’d woken up feeling like he was coming down with a terrible nasty cold, and that was more than enough excuse for Mr. Welland, who believed no duty was worth risking a winter cold turning into something worse. “You can’t be too careful this close to spring,” he said, heaping his plate high with golden straw-colored griddle cakes and drowning them in thick amber syrup. “If I’d been half as prudent at your age, May would be dancing at the New York Assemblies right now instead of spending her winters out in this wilderness with an old invalid.”

“Oh, but I love it here, Papa,” May protested. “If only Newland could stay I’d like it a thousand times better than New York.”

“Newland must stay until he’s completely shaken off that cold,” Mrs. Welland smiled indulgently, and Archer laughed, saying he supposed he couldn’t abandon his work forever. But after a quick flurry of telegrams back to the firm, he managed to stretch his “cold” out for a full week, and he smiled to himself at the irony of it all: Mr. Letterblair’s generosity was half down to the fact that his brilliant young junior partner had wrapped up the messy Countess Olenska divorce matter perfectly, to the entire family’s satisfaction. Mr. Letterblair had even written to tell Mrs. Welland that Archer had “rendered an invaluable service” to the whole clan, and old Mrs. Manson Mingott had been especially thrilled with how things had turned out.

One afternoon, when May had driven off with her father in the town’s only rickety one-horse buggy, Mrs. Welland took the chance to bring up a topic she always carefully avoided in her daughter’s hearing. “I’m afraid Ellen’s ideas are not at all like ours,” she said gently, rocking on the porch swing beside him. “She was barely eighteen when Medora Manson dragged her back to Europe—do you remember that awful excitement when she showed up in full black at her own coming-out ball? Another of Medora’s silly fads, but it feels almost prophetic now, doesn’t it? That was at least twelve years ago, and she’s never set foot in America since. No wonder she’s completely Europeanized.”

“But European society hardly favors divorce,” Archer heard himself say, surprised the words had slipped out. “Countess Olenska thought she’d be following American ideas when she asked for her freedom.” It was the first time he’d spoken Ellen’s name out loud since he’d left Skuytercliff, and he felt heat rise up to his cheeks.

Mrs. Welland smiled compassionately. “That’s exactly the sort of ridiculous thing foreigners make up about us! They think we dine at two o’clock and openly approve of divorce. That’s why I’ve always thought it so foolish to entertain them when they come to New York. They eat our food, accept our hospitality, and then go home and spread those silly made-up stories about us.”

Archer said nothing, and Mrs. Welland went on. “But we all do so thoroughly appreciate you persuading Ellen to give the whole idea up. Her grandmother and her uncle Lovell couldn’t get through to her at all—they both wrote to say her changing her mind was entirely down to your influence, in fact Ellen told her grandmother that herself. She has an unbounded admiration for you. Poor Ellen—she was always a wayward, wild little child. I do wonder what will become of her.”

He bit his tongue hard to stop himself from snapping back what he was thinking: If you’d all rather she ended up as Beaufort’s mistress than marry a decent man who loved her, you’ve certainly gone the right way about making that happen. He wondered what Mrs. Welland would do if he said that out loud, how that soft, placid face of hers would crumple, the face shaped by a whole lifetime of worrying over nothing but tiny trivial social rules to give her a false air of unassailable authority. She still had faint traces of the same fresh, bright beauty May had, and he found himself wondering if May’s face would thicken into that same middle-aged mask of deliberate, invincible innocence, the kind that seals your mind shut against imagination and your heart shut against any messy real experience. He hated that thought more than he could say.

“I truly believe,” Mrs. Welland continued, “that if that horrible business had leaked to the newspapers it would have been my husband’s death blow. I don’t want to know a single detail of it, I told poor Ellen that when she tried to talk to me. With an invalid to care for, I have to keep my mind bright and happy, no dark ugly things to worry over. But Mr. Welland was terribly upset—he ran a slight fever every single morning the whole time we were waiting to hear what you’d decided. It was the horror of the thought of his daughter learning that such ugly things existed in the world—though of course, dear Newland, you felt that same horror too. We all knew you were thinking of May the whole time.”

“I’m always thinking of May,” Archer said, standing up abruptly to cut the conversation short. He’d planned to use this quiet private talk with Mrs. Welland to beg her to move their wedding date forward, but he couldn’t think of a single argument that would budge her, and he sighed in quiet relief when he spotted Mr. Welland and May driving up the drive.

His only remaining hope was to plead his case to May herself, and on the day before he was due to leave, he walked with her out to the crumbling, overgrown ruins of the old Spanish Mission. The weathered stone walls draped in wild jasmine felt exactly like the European scenes he’d been daydreaming of, and May, looking softer than ever under a wide-brimmed hat that cast a soft mysterious shadow over her usually too-clear eyes, lit up with excitement as he told her stories of Granada and the Alhambra. “We could see all of it this spring,” he urged, exaggerating his plans a little to win a bigger concession. “Even the Easter ceremonies in Seville.”

“Easter in Seville?” She laughed. “But Lent starts next week! You don’t have weddings in Lent, Newland.”

He backtracked fast. “Of course I don’t mean that, dearest—but soon after Easter, so we can sail at the end of April. I know I can arrange it at the office, I promise.”

She smiled dreamily at the idea, but he could tell the fantasy was enough for her, like the beautiful stories he read her out of his poetry books that never actually happened in real ordinary life. “Oh do go on, Newland, I love hearing you describe it all.”

“But why should they only be stories? Why can’t we make them real right now?”

“We will, dearest, of course—next year,” she said, her voice soft and slow over the words.

“Don’t you want them to be real sooner? Can’t I persuade you to break away from all the silly waiting rules now?”

She bowed her head, hiding her face under the wide brim of her hat. “Why waste another whole year?” he pressed. “Look at me, dear! Don’t you understand how badly I want you to be my wife?”

For a second she was perfectly still, then she lifted her eyes to his, so clear and earnest and full of a quiet sadness that he loosened his hold on her waist like he’d been caught doing something wrong. Then her expression shifted, deepening into something he couldn’t read. “I’m not sure if I do understand,” she said. “Is it—Newland, is it because you aren’t sure you’ll keep caring for me? Is there someone else?”

Archer jumped up from the bench like he’d been burned. “My God—maybe—I don’t know,” he broke out, angry and bewildered.

May stood up too, and as they faced each other she seemed to grow taller, steeper, full of a quiet womanly dignity neither of them had ever seen in her before. They stood silent for a long moment, both stunned by the turn their conversation had taken, until May spoke in a soft low voice. “Let’s talk frankly, Newland. Sometimes I’ve felt a difference in you—especially since our engagement was announced.”

“Dear, that’s utter madness!” he protested, scrambling to recover.

She gave him a faint, gentle smile. “If it is, it can’t hurt us to talk about it. Even if it’s true—why shouldn’t we speak of it? You could so easily have made a mistake.”

He dropped his head, staring at the pattern of black oak leaves scattered across the sun-warmed path at their feet. “Mistakes are always easy to make—but if I’d made the kind of mistake you’re thinking of, is it even likely I’d be begging you to rush our marriage?”

She looked down too, nudging the leaf pattern apart with the tip of her sunshade, struggling to find the right words. “Yes,” she said at last, very quiet. “You might want to settle the question once and for all. It’s one way to make yourself do the right thing.”

Her quiet lucidity startled him, but he could see the faint pallor of her profile under the hat brim, the tiny tremor in her nostril above her resolute, steady lips, and he knew she wasn’t as unaffected as she seemed. “Well?” he asked, sitting back down on the bench and looking up at her, forcing a playful frown to his face.

She sank back down beside him. “You mustn’t think a girl knows as little as her parents all pretend we do. We hear things, we notice things, we have our own feelings and ideas. Long before you ever told me you cared for me, I knew there was someone else you’d cared for—everyone was gossiping about it two years ago at Newport. I even saw you two sitting together on the verandah at a dance once, and when she walked back into the house her face was so sad I felt sorry for her. I remembered that moment, later, when we got engaged.”

Her voice had dropped almost to a whisper, and she sat twisting her hands around the handle of her sunshade, and Archer laid his hand gently over hers. His chest swelled with a huge, unnameable relief. She was thinking of that old, long-dead fling he’d had with Mrs. Thorley Rushworth, not Ellen Olenska.

“My dear child—was that all it was? If you only knew the truth!”

She lifted her head fast. “Then there’s a truth I don’t know?”

“I meant the truth about that old story you’re talking about,” he said, keeping his hand over hers. “There’s no pledge, no obligation at all of the kind you’re imagining. Those situations don’t always play out quite as simply as people think—but that doesn’t matter. I love how generous you are, because I feel the same way you do about those things—every case should be judged on its own, no matter what stupid public opinion says. Every woman deserves her own liberty, no matter what the rules say.” He pulled himself up short, startled by where his thoughts had wandered, and smiled at her. “Since you understand all that so well, dearest, can’t you go just a little further and see how silly it is for us to submit to this other stupid old convention, and wait another whole year to marry? If there’s no one and nothing standing between us, that’s all the more reason to marry quickly, not drag our feet.”

A bright, happy flush spread over her face, and she lifted her mouth to his, happy tears brimming in her eyes. But the next second, that brave, mature, wise version of her vanished, and she shrank back into the soft, timid, sheltered girl everyone had always known May Welland to be. The effort of speaking so frankly had drained every last scrap of her courage, and she sank back into the safety of all the old familiar rules like a child running to hide in her mother’s arms.

Archer didn’t have the heart to keep pleading with her. He was too soft, sad, and disappointed to see that strange, bright, deep glimpse of the real May slip away again, the May who had stared at him with those clear, unflinching eyes just a few minutes before. May seemed to sense his quiet disappointment, but she had no idea how to fix it, so they stood up in silence, and walked slowly home together, the sun dipping low over the river to paint the orange groves pink and gold all around them.

“Your cousin the Countess called on mother while you were away,” Janey Archer chirped, as Newland hung up his fur-lined overcoat in the front hall, his coat still holding the faint, sun-warmed scent of orange blossoms from his trip down to St. Augustine. The three of them were dining alone that evening, the gas lamps gilding the edges of the dining room’s mahogany sideboard, and Newland glanced up in surprise to catch his mother’s gaze demurely fixed on her plate, as if she were determined not to let him think a visitor could have caught her unawares in her quiet, cloistered routine. Newland smiled to himself, guessing she was a little irked that he could be surprised Ellen Olenska would think to stop by.
“She had on that black velvet polonaise all the fashion plates are gushing over, covered in jet buttons that glinted like crushed starlight, and the tiniest little green monkey muff I ever saw,” Janey rattled on, her eyes bright with awe. “I never saw her look half so stylish. She showed up all alone, early on Sunday afternoon, thank goodness we’d lit the drawing room fire. She pulled out one of those brand new engraved card cases you see the girls from New Paris carrying, and said she’d come calling to thank us for how good you’d been to her.”
Newland laughed, stirring his wine. “Madame Olenska always talks that way about anyone who shows her the smallest kindness. She’s thrilled to finally be surrounded by people she’s related to, people who don’t look at her like she’s a ghost from a bad story.”
“Indeed she did,” Mrs. Archer said, dabbing her lips with her linen napkin. “I will say she seemed genuinely grateful to be home. Though I can’t help noticing she lays herself out to charm even a fusty old widow like me.”
“Mother doesn’t think she’s as simple as May,” Janey interjected, her eyes crinkling as she peered up at Newland from across the table.
“It’s just my old-fashioned taste,” Mrs. Archer said softly. “Dear May will always be my idea of what a young lady ought to be.”
Newland’s chest tightened a little. “Ah. They aren’t alike, not in the slightest.”
A day or two later, when he’d dropped off the stack of little gifts and handwritten messages May had sent for old Mrs. Manson Mingott, he headed over to the matriarch’s house above Fortieth Street—the one all the old conservative clans still gossiped about, as if she’d built it on the moon rather than just a few blocks north of the rest of them. The old woman greeted him with unusual warmth, her puff-ball soft hand patting his knee when he admitted he’d snuck off from the office without leave just to rush down to Florida and see May.
“Ah, so you kicked over all the traces, did you?” she cackled, her adipose chins wobbling like wobbly little jellies. “I bet Augusta and the Wellands pulled those long, glum faces of theirs, acting like the world was ending? But little May—she got it, didn’t she? She knew why you ran.”
“I hoped she did,” Newland said, his shoulders slumping a little. “But she still wouldn’t say yes to what I went down there to ask.”
“Wouldn’t she? And what was that?”
“I wanted her to agree we’d get married in April. What’s the point of wasting another whole year waiting?”
Old Catherine screwed up her face into a mock-prudish grimace, her eyes twinkling like mischievous marbles. “‘Ask Mamma,’ I’ll wager she said. The same old Mingott nonsense! They’re all born stuck in a rut, and you couldn’t dig them out of it with a pickaxe. When I built this house, everyone acted like I’d run off to California! No one had ever lived above Fortieth Street, they squawked, like the whole rest of Manhattan was just wilderness for the Indians. None of them want to be different, they’re scared of it like it’s smallpox. Thank my stars I’m just a vulgar Spicer, none of that stuffy Mingott nonsense in my bones. Only my little Ellen got that wild, fire-in-the-blood streak from me.”
She paused, still grinning at him, and tossed out a line as casual as if she was commenting on the weather: “Tell me, why in the world didn’t you marry my little Ellen, all those years back?”
Newland’s throat went dry, and he forced a laugh. “For one thing, she wasn’t around to ask.”
“No, that’s true. More’s the pity. Now it’s too late. Her life’s already all tangled up,” she said, cold and matter-of-fact, the way only very old people can talk about young people’s ruined hopes. Newland’s heart turned cold in his chest, and he spoke quickly.
“Can’t I convince you to use your influence on the Wellands? I wasn’t made for these endless long engagements.”
Old Catherine beamed, her face crinkling. “I can see that. You’ve got a quick, hungry eye. I bet when you were a little boy you always shoved to be first in line for the cake.” She threw back her head and laughed, and just then the drawing room portières rustled open.
Ellen Olenska stepped in, her cloak dusted with a fine powder of snow, her cheeks bright pink from the cold. She smiled, and her whole face lit up, and she held her hand out gaily to Newland before leaning down to kiss her grandmother’s cheek.
“Perfect timing, Ellen!” old Catherine cackled. “I was just telling him, ‘Why didn’t you marry my little Ellen?’”
Ellen looked over at Newland, that soft, knowing smile still playing on her lips. “And what did he say to that?”
“Oh, darling, I’ll leave you to pry that out of him!” her grandmother laughed. “He ran all the way down to Florida to beg his sweetheart to marry him sooner.”
“I know,” Ellen said, still looking right at him. “I stopped by your mother’s house to find out where you’d gone. I sent you a note that you never answered. I was afraid you’d fallen ill on your trip.”
Newland stammered out that he’d left in such a hurry he’d meant to write her from St. Augustine and it had slipped his mind entirely.
“And once you got down there with your pretty girl you forgot all about little old me, didn’t you?” she teased, bright and giddy, like she was deliberately putting on a show of easy indifference so he wouldn’t see how much his silence had stung. It made his chest ache. He wanted to thank her for calling on his mother, but with old Catherine’s sharp, amused eyes on them he couldn’t get the words out, too tongue-tied to be honest.
“Look at him! Rushing off to elope almost, to get that silly girl to agree to an early wedding!” old Catherine cawed. “That’s the kind of lover my own father was, handsome Bob Spicer, ran off with my mother before anyone could stop him, and got tired of her before I was even weaned! And they only had to wait eight months for me! But you’re no Spicer, young man, lucky for you and lucky for May. Only my poor Ellen got that wild, wicked Spicer blood. The rest of you are all perfect little model Mingotts.”
Newland noticed Ellen had sat down at her grandmother’s side, her eyes soft and thoughtful, no trace of that bright giddiness left. “Granny,” she said gently, “surely the two of us together can badger the Wellands into giving him what he wants.”
Newland stood up to go, and when his hand brushed hers at the door, he could feel she was waiting for him to mention the unanswered note, to say he’d cared enough to notice her missing.
“When can I see you?” he asked, low, as she walked him to the drawing room door.
“Whenever you like, but soon. I’m moving out of my little house next week.”
A sharp, soft pang went through him at the memory of all those quiet, lamplit hours he’d spent there, the low-ceilinged drawing room stuffed full of her little trinkets, every minute of them thick with a feeling he couldn’t name.
“Tomorrow evening?”
She nodded. “Tomorrow, yes. But come early. I’m going out later.”
Tomorrow was Sunday, of course, and the only place anyone in their set went out on a Sunday evening was Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s house. Newland felt a flicker of annoyance—not because he thought Ellen ought not go, not because he cared about what stuffy old van der Luydens would say, but because he knew full well Julius Beaufort would be there. He was sure she’d known that too, before she agreed to go.
“Tomorrow evening,” he repeated, and resolved he would show up late. Late enough that he could either convince her not to go at all, or show up after she’d already left, which would be the simpler, less painful choice.
But by half past eight he was at her door, ringing the bell under the wisteria vine, restless and thrumming with a tension he couldn’t shake, half an hour earlier than he’d meant to be. He expected to find the drawing room empty at first, then stopped short when he saw a whole stack of hats and overcoats in the hall, laid out by Nastasia. The coats were the strangest he’d ever seen under a respectable New York roof: one shaggy yellow ready-made ulster, the other an ancient, rusty cape cloak that looked like it had seen a hundred years of hard use, reeking of sawdust and cigar smoke, with a ragged grey scarf and a half-clerical felt hat laid on top of it.
Nastasia just shrugged fatalistically and pulled open the drawing room door for him.
There was no sign of Ellen. Instead, a tall, lean lady with faded, streaky white hair, draped in layers of fringed, plaid silk that looked like it had been sewn together from scraps, was standing by the fire, flanked by Ned Winsett and a giant, shaggy grey-haired man who moved his big paws like he was handing out blessings to a crowd. They were all staring at an enormous bouquet of crimson roses tied with purple pansies, set on the sofa where Ellen usually sat.
“Think of what these must cost in the middle of winter!” the lady sighed in a staccato little breath, as Newland walked in. Then she turned, held out her silk-mittened hand, and beamed at him. “Dear Mr. Archer—almost my cousin Newland! I am the Marchioness Manson. Ellen has taken me in for a few days while I pass through New York, fresh from wintering with the old Castile nobility in Cuba. Such delightful, spiritual people.” She gestured to the giant man. “This is Dr. Agathon Carver, founder of the famous Valley of Love Community upstate.”
Dr. Carver nodded his big lion-like head, and boomed a greeting. Ned Winsett winked at Newland from the corner.
They chatted for a few minutes, Winsett slipping out the door with a dry quip about Ellen’s house being the only oasis on their street, before Dr. Carver lumbered off to give a lecture at the Blenkers’. The Marchioness waved Newland to a seat, her little ivory fan fluttering in her gloved hand.
“I know everything you’ve done for my Ellen,” she said, leaning in, her voice dropping low like a secret. “That wise advice you gave her, that firm stand you took—you have no idea what that did. Because at that exact same moment, I was being reached out to… from the other side of the Atlantic.” She pressed the fan to her lips, glancing over her shoulder like someone might be listening at the door. “By the Count himself. My poor, foolish Stanislas Olenski. He asks only to take her back. On her own terms.”
Newland shot to his feet, cold all over. “Good God.”
“He sends his letter, his plea, through me,” the Marchioness went on, tapping her thin chest. “Think of what she’s throwing away, Mr. Archer. Acres of those crimson roses under glass in his terraced gardens in Nice. Historic pearls, the Sobieski emeralds, sables, priceless art, all the brilliant society she was adored in. And a remorseful husband begging her to come home.”
Newland stared at her, numb, realizing the woman standing in front of him was no silly old eccentric—she was a messenger straight from the hell Ellen had run so far to escape.
“Does she know? Has she seen the letter?” he stammered.
“Not yet. I wanted to see you first. I knew if I had your support, your influence over her… we could convince her it’s the right choice.”
“That she ought to go back?” Newland said, his voice sharp and violent. “I would rather see her dead first.”
The Marchioness didn’t even flinch. She tilted her head, and they both heard the soft sound of Ellen’s footsteps on the stairs coming down. She pointed a thin, purple-stained finger at the crimson roses on the sofa, her eyes glinting.
“Ellen’s here now. After all, Mr. Archer—marriage is marriage. And my niece is still a wife.”

“What on earth are you two plotting over there, Aunt Medora?” Ellen Olenska called out as she stepped across the threshold, her entrance bright as a burst of champagne fizz in the dim, candle-lit drawing room. She was dressed as if heading for the most lavish ball of the season: every thread of her gown shimmered, stitched through with faint silver sequins that caught the flame glow like they’d been woven from captured candle-beams, and she held her head high, the set of her chin that of a woman who’d long ago learned not to flinch before a room full of whispering rivals.

“Nothing but a little surprise for you, my dear,” Mrs. Manson Medora trilled, rising to her feet and pointing playfully at the massive lush bouquet of hothouse lilacs and roses set on the side table.

Ellen stopped short, her gaze locking on the flowers. No flush rose to her cheeks, but a pale, searing radiance of anger swept over her skin, quick and bright as summer lightning across a dark field. “Ah!” she exclaimed, in a sharp, high tone Newland Archer had never heard from her before. “Who is ridiculous enough to send me an anonymous bouquet? Why flowers? Why tonight of all nights? I am not a debutante waiting on a beau’s token, not a girl newly engaged to be married. But it seems some people will never stop treating me like either.”

She turned on her heel, wrenched open the drawing room door, and called for her maid. Nastasia appeared at once, and Archer listened as Ellen spoke slow, deliberate Italian, enunciating every word clearly so he could follow along. “Here—take this whole thing and toss it in the dustbin—no, wait. The poor flowers haven’t done anything to deserve that. Tell the errand boy to carry it three doors down, to Mr. Winsett’s house, the quiet dark gentleman who dined here last week. His wife has been ill in bed for weeks, those might cheer her a little. The boy is out? Then run yourself, darling—wrap yourself in my velvet opera cloak, go straight there, and not a single word about who sent them. I want this thing out of my house this very second.” She slung the heavy fur-lined cloak over Nastasia’s shoulders, shut the drawing room door sharp behind her, and leaned back against it, her lace-trimmed chest rising and falling fast. For a heartbeat Archer was sure she was about to burst into tears—then she laughed, bright and breathless, and glanced between Medora and him. “Well, have you two managed to become friends while I was fumbling with my hair?”

“That’s for Mr. Archer to say, dear heart. He waited so patiently for you to finish dressing,” Medora simpered.

Ellen laughed, lifting a hand to fluff at the piled curls of her chignon. “I gave him more than enough time, I swear—my hair simply refused to behave. But that reminds me: Dr. Carver has already left, hasn’t he? You two will be late to the Blenkers’ dinner if you don’t hurry. Newland, would you be a dear and help tuck my aunt into her carriage? She’s got so many shawls and overshoes she can barely move.”

She followed Medora out to the hall, fussed over her until she was wrapped so tight in wool and lace she looked like a travelling bundle of holiday gifts, then called after the carriage as it pulled away: “Mind you send the horses right back for me at ten!” and stepped back inside to the drawing room, where Archer found her leaning against the mantel, studying her own reflection in the mirror.

It was unheard of for a lady of New York society to call her parlour maid “darling” and lend her her own custom opera cloak to run an errand, and Archer felt a giddy, warm thrill low in his chest—here, in Ellen’s orbit, action followed feeling as fast as lightning, no stuffy rules to drag it down by the heels. She didn’t turn when he stepped up behind her, and their eyes met for a long second in the mirror glass before she whirled away, sank into the curve of the sofa, and sighed. “There’s just time for a cigarette, thank goodness.”

He handed her the silver case, lit a spill for her, and as the small flame flared up to gild her cheekbones she glanced at him, eyes dancing with amusement. “What do you think of me, throwing a silly little temper tantrum over a bouquet?”

Archer hesitated a beat, then spoke with sudden, unplanned resolve. “It makes me understand what your aunt was telling me about you, while you were dressing.”

“Of course she was talking about me. Well?”

“She said you’ve spent your whole life surrounded by splendours, amusements, excitements—things no one in slow, staid old New York could ever hope to give you.”

Ellen smiled faintly, blowing a soft ring of smoke into the air between them. “Medora is incurably romantic. It’s gotten her out of more tight spots than you could ever count.”

Archer hesitated again, and took the risk. “Is her romanticism ever paired with the truth, do you think?”

Ellen tilted her head, considering. “You’re asking if she lies, aren’t you? Well. Almost everything she says has a little kernel of truth buried under a whole lot of nonsense. But why do you ask? What’s she been spinning you this time?”

He looked away at the crackling fire, then back at her, glowing and bright across the sofa. His chest tightened so he could barely breathe—this was their last quiet evening by this fireside, he knew it, any second the returning carriage would rumble up outside and carry her away, and this moment would be gone forever. “She says Count Olenski wrote her a letter, asked her to come here and persuade you to go back to him.”

Ellen didn’t move. She sat perfectly still, cigarette half-lifted to her lips, her face no flicker of surprise crossing it—Archer had always noticed she almost never looked shocked, no matter what news landed at her feet.
