She sat so long the ash broke off the end of her cigarette and dropped to the rug, and she brushed it away absently. “She’s been dropping little hints about a letter, yes. Poor Medora, her hints are always so obvious.”

“Did she really turn up out of nowhere, all on your husband’s orders?”

Ellen pondered that, her brow softening. “It’s hard to say. She told me she had a ‘spiritual summons’ from Dr. Carver, whatever that means. I’m half sure she’s planning to marry Dr. Carver, you know—poor Medora, there’s always some new odd man she’s determined to marry. Though it’s equally possible the Cuban family she was companion to finally got tired of her chatter and sent her packing. I honestly don’t know why she showed up on my doorstep.”

“But you do believe the letter from Olenski is real?”

Ellen was quiet for another long stretch, her gaze fixed on the fire. “Well. It was only a matter of time, wasn’t it?”

Archer stood up, leaned his shoulder against the mantel. Restlessness coiled through him sharp and hot, every minute slipping away from him like sand through his fingers. “You know Medora honestly thinks you’ll agree to go back to him, don’t you?”

Ellen’s head snapped up, and a deep, dark flush crawled up her cheeks, all the way down her neck to the very edge of her low gown. She rarely blushed, and when she did it looked like it burned her, like pressing a hot iron to bare skin. “People have believed far crueler things about me.”

“Oh Ellen—forgive me, I’m a fool, a brute, I never meant to hurt you.”

She smiled, soft and a little sad. “You’re just horribly nervous. You’ve got your own troubles, don’t you? I know the Wellands are being absurdly stubborn about your long engagement, and I agree with you entirely. Back in Europe no one would ever dream of dragging an engagement on for years the way New York does. I suppose they’re just far calmer than we are.” She emphasized that last “we” with a faint, ironic lilt that hung in the air.

Archer caught the irony, but didn’t dare lean into it. She’d clearly deliberately shifted the conversation away from her own pain, and after the way his words had stung her, he felt he ought to follow her lead. But the minutes were slipping so fast he was desperate—he couldn’t bear to let polite little words build a wall between them again. “I went down to Florida to see May,” he blurted. “I asked her to marry me right after Easter. There’s no reason at all we can’t have the wedding then.”

“May adores you—couldn’t you convince her? I always thought she was too clever to buy into all those silly old New York superstitions about unlucky spring weddings.”

“She is clever. It’s not that.”

Ellen stared at him, confused. “Then I don’t understand.”

Archer’s face burned, and he rushed the words out before he could talk himself out of them. “We had a frank talk, the first one we’ve ever really had. She thinks my impatience is a bad sign. She thinks it means I don’t trust myself to keep caring for her, long enough. She thinks, in short, that I’m so desperate to marry her right now just to run away from someone else that I care for more.”

Ellen studied that, her brow furrowed. “But if she thinks that—why on earth isn’t she in a hurry too, to lock you down?”

“Because she’s not like that. She’s far nobler than that. She insists on dragging the engagement out longer, to give me all the time I need—”

“Time to give her up for the other woman?”

Archer nodded. Ellen leaned forward, her gaze fixed on the dancing flames. Somewhere on the quiet street below, the slow trot of a carriage’s horses was growing louder, coming closer. “That is very noble,” she said, her voice cracking just a little.

“It’s ridiculous, though.”

“Ridiculous? Because you don’t care for anyone else?”

“Because I never had any intention of marrying anyone but you—wait, no. Wait. That’s not right.”

Ellen glanced up, surprised. “Then why are you in such a hurry to marry May early?”

“Your carriage is here,” Archer said, not moving an inch from where he leaned against the mantel.

She half-stood, her eyes blank and distant, groping for her gloves and fan on the sofa beside her. “Yes. I suppose I have to go to Mrs. Struthers’s party.” She paused, then smiled soft, and added: “I must go where I’m invited, otherwise I’d get far too lonely. Why don’t you come with me?”

Archer’s whole body was thrumming now. He couldn’t let her go, couldn’t let the night end like this, he had to steal every last second he could with her. He ignored her question, kept his gaze fixed on the hand holding her gloves, as if he could will her to drop them. “May guessed the truth,” he said. “There is another woman. But not the one she thinks I’m running from.”

Ellen didn’t say a word, didn’t move. After a long, quiet moment, Archer crossed the room and sat down right beside her, wrapped his hand gently around hers, and uncurled her fingers slowly. The gloves and fan tumbled onto the sofa between them. She jolted back, tore her hand free, and stepped across the hearth away from him, her face tight with a frown. “Ah, don’t fall in love with me. Far too many men have already done that, and it never ends well.”

Archer’s face burned hot, he stood up so fast his knees knocked against the sofa leg. That was the harshest rebuke she could have dealt him. “I have never tried to make love to you, not the way all those other men did. And I never will. But you are the woman I would have married, if it had ever been possible for either of us.”

“Possible for either of us?” She stared at him, completely unfeigned shock on her face. “And you’re the one telling me that? You’re the one who made it impossible!”

Archer gaped at her, suddenly blind, as if a single shaft of searing light had torn through the dark fog in his head and left him reeling. “I made it impossible?”

“You, you, you!” she cried, her lip trembling like a little child’s about to cry. “It was you who made me give up the divorce! You convinced me how selfish and cruel it was, that I had to sacrifice my own wants to protect the dignity of marriage, to spare all our families the ugly public scandal, the endless gossiping. And I knew my family would soon be your family—for May’s sake, and for yours—so I did exactly what you told me to do, exactly what you proved to me was the right thing. I never even tried to hide that I did it all for you!”

She sank back down onto the sofa, huddling in the soft ripples of her fancy ball gown like a wounded masquerader, and Archer stood frozen by the mantel, staring at her, his mind blank.

“Good God,” he groaned. “When I thought—”

“What did you think?”

“Ah, don’t ask me what I thought!” he moaned, still staring at her as that same burning blush crawled all the way up her neck to her cheeks. She sat up straight, facing him with rigid, quiet dignity. “I do ask you. Tell me.”

“Fine. There were things in that letter from Olenski you asked me to read—”

“My husband’s letter?”

“Yes.”

“I had nothing at all to fear from that letter! Nothing! All I ever feared was bringing all that ugly notoriety down on the family—on you and May.”

“Good God,” he groaned again, pressing his face into his hands. The silence that fell over the room felt heavy as a gravestone, final, irrevocable. Archer felt it crushing down on his shoulders, as if all the rest of his life stretched ahead empty, no light anywhere to lift that weight off his chest. He didn’t move, didn’t lift his face from his hands. “At least I loved you,” he managed to get out, hoarse.

From the other side of the hearth, he heard a small, muffled, childlike sob. He jumped to his feet, crossed to her side in two strides. “Ellen! What madness is this? Why are you crying? Nothing’s decided that can’t be undone. I’m still free, and you’re going to be free soon, too.” He pulled her up into his arms, her face soft and wet against his, their lips meeting, and all the months of quiet pining, all the useless lies, all the pretense they’d wrapped around themselves went up in smoke like ghosts at sunrise. He couldn’t believe he’d wasted all those months tiptoeing around her when just one touch made everything make perfect sense.

She kissed him back, for a heartbeat, then he felt her go stiff in his arms, and she pushed him gently away, standing up to face him across the rug. “Ah, my poor Newland— I suppose this was always going to happen. But it doesn’t change a single thing, not really.”

“It changes my whole entire life.”

“No, no, it can’t. You’re engaged to May Welland. I’m still married.”

Archer stood up too, his face bright red, resolute. “Nonsense! It’s far too late for that kind of lie. We have no right to lie to the people we love, or to ourselves. We won’t talk about your marriage, but do you honestly think I can marry May now, after this?”

She was quiet, her thin elbows resting on the mantel, her profile reflected in the glass behind her. One of the curls from her chignon had slipped loose and hung down against her neck, and she looked haggard, almost old. “I don’t see you,” she said, slow at last, “having the heart to put that question to May. Do you?”

Archer laughed, reckless. “It’s too late to do anything else.”

“You’re only saying that because it feels like the easiest, truest thing to say right this second. But it’s not. It’s too late for us to do anything but exactly what we both already decided was right.”

“Ah, I don’t understand you!”

She gave him a small, pitiful smile that pulled tight at the corners of her mouth. “You don’t understand because you’ve never guessed how much you changed things for me, from the very first day I got here. I was so stupid, so unobservant. I didn’t even notice at first that everyone in New York was avoiding dinner invitations that had my name on the guest list, that they thought I was some kind of wicked fallen woman. I found out later how you dragged your mother to the van der Luydens’ house, how you deliberately announced your engagement at the Beaufort ball so I’d have two whole families standing up for me, not just the Mingotts, so no one dared snub me. New York felt like home, like peace and safety, and I thought everyone here was kind. But you were kinder than all of them. The pious, good people never convinced me to do the hard things, because I knew they’d never been tempted to run away the way I had. But you knew. You understood. You’d felt the whole wide shiny world outside tugging at you with all its golden hands, and you still hated the happiness that costs disloyalty, cruelty, hurting people who love you. That was something I’d never known before. It’s better than anything I ever had in Europe.”

Her words fell slow and heavy, like drops of burning lead, right into his chest. He sat bowed, his head in his hands, staring at the tip of her satin peep-toe shoe under the hem of her gown. Suddenly he dropped to his knees, and pressed a kiss to the toe of that shoe.

She leaned down, her hands resting light on his shoulders, gazing down at him with eyes so deep and dark he couldn’t look away. “Ah, don’t let us undo all the good you’ve already done! I can’t go back to that old, messy, cruel way of thinking. I can’t love you unless I give you up.”

His arms ached to pull her close, but she stepped back, and there was a whole chasm between them now, made of all the honor and goodness they both refused to betray. Then Archer’s temper snapped. “What, then? Beaufort is the one who gets

The day breathed bright and sharp, a giddy spring wind whipping through Manhattan’s cobblestones, heavy with fine dust and the faint green tang of budding park trees. Every matriarch in the extended Welland and Archer clans had dragged their long-stored furs from cedar chests, sables faded to soft ash and ermines yellowed at the cuffs, so the sharp, heady reek of camphor hanging over Grace Church’s front pews nearly drowned out the delicate, sweet lily scent billowing up from the banked altar flowers.

At the sexton’s quiet signal, Newland Archer stepped out of the vestry to stand beside his best man on the chancel steps, his boots soft on the worn stone. The signal meant the brougham carrying May and her father was just in sight, but he knew there would be a long, fussy stretch of last-minute adjustments in the church lobby, where the bridesmaids hovered already like a cluster of pale Easter blossoms. It was the tradition for the groom to stand exposed to the full gaze of the assembled guests, proof of his eagerness to marry, and Archer submitted to the ritual as resignedly as he had every other absurd, ancient custom that made a 19th century New York wedding feel like a rite pulled straight from the dawn of time. Every step of the path he’d chosen felt equally trivial, equally sharp-edged, and he obeyed his flustered best man’s last-minute instructions as piously as grooms he’d guided through the same labyrinth of rules had obeyed him years before.

He’d checked every box, he was fairly certain. Eight bouquets of white lilac and lily of the valley had been sent to the bridesmaids on time, along with gold-and-sapphire sleeve links for the eight ushers, and a cat’s-eye scarf pin for his best man. He’d sat up half the night the day before rewriting his thank-you notes for the pile of wedding gifts from his male friends and old half-forgotten flirtations. The bishop and rector’s fees were safely tucked in his best man’s pocket, his own travelling bags were already at Mrs. Manson Mingott’s house where the wedding breakfast would be held, and a private compartment had been reserved on the train that would carry him and May off to their secret honeymoon destination – hiding the location of their first night away was one of the most sacred, unbreakable taboos of the old society ritual.

“Got the ring all right?” whispered young van der Luyden Newland, the inexperienced best man, white-knuckled under the weight of his responsibility.

Archer made the familiar gesture he’d seen a hundred other grooms make: he slipped his ungloved right hand into the pocket of his dark grey waistcoat, and felt the tiny warm gold circlet, engraved inside *Newland to May, April ——, 187—*, nestled safely in its velvet case. He settled back into his pose, tall hat and pearl-grey gloves stitched with black held in his left hand, and stared at the church’s heavy oak doors. Overhead, Handel’s wedding march swelled pompously through the imitation stone vaulting, carrying with it the ghost of every other wedding he’d stood witness to in that exact spot, watching other brides drift up the nave to meet other waiting grooms.

“Just like a first night at the Opera,” he thought to himself, recognizing all the same society faces in the same familiar pews, and wondering if the day the Last Trump blew, Mrs. Selfridge Merry would still be perched there wearing that towering ostrich feather bonnet, Mrs. Beaufort still glinting in her diamond earrings with that same unchanging smile, and if they’d already reserved the best proscenium seats in the afterlife.

He had time, still, to scan the first rows of faces one by one: the women sharp with curiosity and giddy excitement, the men sulky and resentful that they’d been forced to squeeze into frock coats before luncheon and fight for plates of food at the breakfast later. “Too bad the breakfast’s at old Catherine’s,” he could almost hear Reggie Chivers grumble. “But I heard Lovell Mingott made them use his own chef, so it ought to be good if you can fight your way to the table.” And Sillerton Jackson would chime right after, all self-important authority: “My dear boy, haven’t you heard? They’re serving it at small little tables, the new English fashion.”

Archer’s gaze softened as it landed on the left-hand pew, where his mother, who’d entered on Henry van der Luyden’s arm, wept softly behind her Chantilly veil, her hands tucked deep in her grandmother’s big ermine muff. Poor Janey, his sister, he thought – even twisting her neck as far as she could, she’d only be able to see the people in the first few pews, all the dowdy, stuffy old Newland and Dagonet relatives. On the near side of the white ribbon marking the family reserved seats, Beaufort stood tall and red-faced, staring boldly out over the crowd of women with his usual arrogant stare. Beside him his wife sat swaddled in silvery chinchilla and pinned with fresh violets, and on the far side of the ribbon, Lawrence Lefferts’s sleekly combed head looked like it was standing guard over the invisible patron saint of “Good Form” that presided over every New York ceremony. Archer wondered how many tiny, unforgivable flaws Lefferts’s sharp eyes would pick out of the day’s ritual, then realized with a faint jolt he’d once cared about those stupid little rules too. All the things that had once filled his days felt like a nursery play-pretend of real life, medieval scholars squabbling over meaningless words no one actually understood. Just the night before, the whole family had had a screaming, hours-long fight over whether to display the wedding presents for guests, and Mrs. Welland had dissolved into indignant tears, swearing she’d “sooner let reporters run loose in my house” than show the gifts off – once upon a time, Archer would have had very strong, very loud opinions on the rightness of that call. Now it all felt absurd.

“And all the while,” he thought, quiet and hollow, “real people were out living real lives somewhere, weren’t they?”

“There they come!” his best man breathed, excited – but Archer knew better. The soft creak of the church door only meant Mr. Brown, the livery stable keeper who doubled as sexton in a black gown, was peeking out to survey the crowd before herding the procession inside. The door clicked shut again, and after another long, breathless interval, it swung wide open. A soft murmur rippled through every pew: “The family!”

Mrs. Welland led the way on her eldest son’s arm, her big pink face appropriately solemn, her plum-colored satin gown with pale blue side panels and blue ostrich plumes in her small bonnet earning quiet nods of approval from the crowd. But before she could settle with a great stately rustle into the pew across from Archer’s mother, every head was craning to see who came next. Wild rumors had run through the clubs the day before: old Mrs. Manson Mingott, despite her crippling weight and bad legs, was determined to be at the ceremony. The bets had run high – would she make it up the nave? She’d even sent her own carpenter over to measure the front pew, to see if they could saw off the end panel to make space for her, and for a whole day the family had watched her toy with the plan of being wheeled up the whole aisle in her massive Bath chair, enthroned right at the foot of the chancel. The whole family had been desperate to avoid that undignified spectacle, and they’d nearly handed a fortune to the servant who’d suddenly pointed out the Bath chair was too wide to fit through the iron supports of the awning that stretched from the church doors to the curb. Old Catherine had weighed tearing the awning down for half a second, but the thought of the crowds of dressmakers and newspaper reporters jostling outside, of them taking photographs of the wedding to print in the papers, had made the whole clan recoil in collective horror. Catherine had conceded to skip the church service – but only on the hard promise that the wedding breakfast would be held at her house.

Even after all that, a small group of betting holdouts still swore she’d make a surprise appearance, and a soft huff of disappointment ran through the crowd when they saw her daughter-in-law walk in instead. Then came the gaunt, mincing figure of Medora Manson, swathed in a wild mess of stripes, fringes, and floating scarves – and Archer’s heart seized up so tight he thought it might stop beating. He’d been certain Medora and Ellen Olenska were still in Washington, that they’d left four weeks earlier to get Medora away from the cult leader Dr. Agathon Carver who’d almost recruited her for his “Valley of Love” commune. No one had expected either of them to come back for the wedding. He stared hard at Medora’s strange, frilly figure, craning to see who was right behind her – but the little procession was already over. There was no one else. Ellen wasn’t there.

“Newland – I say, she’s here!” his best man hissed in his ear.

Archer jolted back to his senses. Time had slipped away on him, he realized, because the pale pink bridesmaid procession was already halfway up the nave, the bishop, rector, and two white-robed acolytes hovering by the lily-heavy altar, and the first soft chords of the Spohr symphony were spilling like scattered petals ahead of the bride. He blinked, and the blur of tulle and orange blossoms that was May drifted closer and closer. She was glowing, so bright she sent a faint soft warmth through all his cold numbness, and he straightened up, and smiled right into her eyes as she came to stand beside him.

“Dearly beloved, we are gathered together here,” the rector began.

The ring slid onto her finger. The bishop gave his final benediction. The bridesmaids shifted to fall back into line, and the organ started rumbling the first notes of the Mendelssohn march – the one no New York newlywed couple had ever walked out of the church without. “Your arm! Give her your arm!” young Newland the best man hissed, nervous as a cat, and Archer realized he’d drifted off somewhere far away, half-convinced he’d caught a glimpse of Ellen’s dark coiled hair in the crowd of onlookers, only for it to turn out to be a total stranger with a long nose, so absurdly different he wondered if he was starting to hallucinate.

He and May walked slowly down the nave together, carried along on the light ripples of the march, the bright spring day beckoning through the wide open church doors. The footman wrapped her soft white cloak around her, and Archer jumped into the brougham right beside her. May turned to him with a brilliant, triumphant smile, and their hands clasped tight under her veil.

“Darling,” Archer said – and for a split second that dark, empty abyss yawned open under his feet, and he felt himself falling, falling, even as his voice came out smooth and cheerful, light as air. “Of course I thought I’d lost the ring. No wedding is complete without the poor groom panicking over that. But you did keep me waiting, you know! I had time to imagine every terrible disaster that could possibly happen.”

To his shock, she threw her arms around his neck right there in full view of Fifth Avenue’s passersby. “But none of them can ever happen now, can it Newland? Not as long as we’re together.”

Every last detail of the day had been planned to perfection. After the wedding breakfast, they had plenty of time to change into their travelling clothes, walk down Mrs. Mingott’s wide staircase between grinning bridesmaids and weeping parents, and climb into their brougham under the traditional shower of thrown rice and satin slippers. They still had half an hour left to drive to the station, buy the latest weeklies at the bookstall like two seasoned, experienced travellers, and settle into their private compartment, where May’s maid had already stowed her dove-grey travelling cloak and the brand new, shining leather dressing bag she’d ordered all the way from London. The old du Lac aunts out in Rhinebeck had lent them their country house for the honeymoon, eager to spend a week in New York visiting Archer’s mother, and Archer had jumped at the chance, glad to skip the usual dreadful bridal suite in a Philadelphia or Baltimore hotel. May was over the moon about the country, giggling at the way the eight bridesmaids had run around trying and failing to find out their secret retreat’s location. It was deemed “very English” to have a friend lend you a country house, and it made the wedding the most talked-about, brilliant event of the whole year.

Once the train pulled out of the city, shaking off the endless stretches of wooden suburban houses to roll out into pale, greening spring countryside, talking came easier to Archer than he’d expected. May was still the bright, simple, uncomplicated girl he’d fallen in love with, gushing to him over every little silly detail of the wedding, as casual and unshaken as if she was just chatting over tea. At first he’d thought her calmness was just a mask for jittery nerves, but her clear, wide eyes held nothing but unclouded, happy innocence. She was alone for the first time with her husband – but her husband was just her favorite, most trusted comrade, and this whole great adventure of running off on a journey together was the most fun, most wonderful lark she could possibly imagine. It amazed him, that such soft, deep warmth could exist in someone with no imagination at all – no quiet, secret hunger for the things they weren’t supposed to want.

“ I was surprised, though – weren’t you? – that Aunt Medora turned up after all,” May said suddenly, pulling him out of his thoughts. “Ellen wrote that neither of them were well enough to make the journey. I do wish it had been Ellen who felt well enough to come! Did you see the exquisite old lace she sent me as a wedding gift?”

Archer had known this moment would come eventually, but he’d foolishly thought he could will it away. “Yes – I – it was beautiful,” he said, staring at her blindly, wondering if every time he heard Ellen’s name his whole carefully constructed, carefully normal world would tumble down around him like a house of cards. “Aren’t you tired? It’ll be so nice to have tea when we get to the house, I’m sure the aunts have everything perfectly ready,” he rattled on, grabbing her hand tight in his. She instantly switched tracks, chattering excitedly about the magnificent Baltimore silver tea and coffee service the Beauforts had gifted them, how perfectly it matched the trays Lovell Mingott had given them.

The train pulled into the Rhinebeck station as the spring twilight deepened, and they walked along the platform to the waiting carriage. “Ah, how kind of the van der Luydens,” Archer exclaimed, spotting the sedate, unliveried man waiting by their bags. “They sent their man all the way over from Skuytercliff to meet us.”

“I’m very sorry, sir,” the man said, apologetic. “There was a little accident at the Misses du Lac’s house yesterday – their water tank sprung a leak. Mr. van der Luyden heard about it first thing this morning, and sent a housemaid up on the early train to get the Patroon’s house ready for you instead. It’s perfectly comfortable, sir, I promise you – and the Misses du Lac sent their cook over too, so it will be exactly the same as if you were staying in Rhinebeck.”

Archer stared at him blankly. May broke in before he could speak, bright and excited. “The same as Rhinebeck? The Patroon’s house? That’s a hundred thousand times better! Isn’t it, Newland? It’s the dearest, kindest thing Mr. van der Luyden could have done for us.” She leaned forward, giddy, as the carriage pulled away, their shiny new bridal bags on the seat in front of them. “I’ve never been inside it – have you? The van der Luydens barely ever show it to anyone. But they opened it for Ellen once, you know. She told me what a darling little place it was – she said it’s the only house in all of America she could ever imagine being perfectly happy in.”

“Well, that’s what we’re going to be, isn’t it?” Archer said, laughing, bright and easy, and she smiled that boyish, uncomplicated smile of hers.

“Ah!” she said. “This is our luck starting, isn’t it? The wonderful, perfect luck we’re going to have together, always.”

"Of course we must dine with Mrs. Carfry, dearest,” Newland Archer murmured, tapping the edge of her teacup gently across the hulking, polished Britannia ware that dominated their lodging house breakfast table, as fat raindrops streaked the fog-frosted London panes behind them.
Across the table, his wife lifted her gaze with a small, anxious frown, all soft newlywed sweetness tangled in quiet uncertainty. The whole of autumnal London felt like a damp, empty desert, and the only two compatriots they knew in the sprawling city were the pair of English sisters his mother and Janey had befriended years prior at a Tyrolean inn. They had avoided reaching out to them entirely, after all: old New York’s unwritten code held it was never “dignified” to force one’s acquaintance on foreigners while traveling.
Mrs. Archer and Janey had upheld that principle with unflinching rigor across decades of European trips, their reserved, impenetrable guard so firmly in place they could boast of barely having exchanged a word with any stranger outside hotel and railway staff. Fellow Americans not pre-vetted by the Mingott, Dagonet or Chivers clans were met with even stiffer disdain, making their months abroad little more than endless, quiet tête-à-têtes—until a rainy night in Botzen, when a knock came at their door. The English lady across the hall, Mrs. Carfry, had been struck by a sudden, brutal bronchitis attack, and her sister Miss Harle had come begging for liniment. The Archer ladies, who never traveled without a full family pharmacy, not only gave them the remedy but nursed the sick woman back to health with endless little thoughtful comforts, their lady’s maid tending her through the worst of the fever.
The Archers had left Botzen never expecting to cross paths with the two sisters again. To Mrs. Archer’s mind, nothing could be more undignified than to intrude on a foreigner to whom one had accidentally done a small kindness. But Mrs. Carfry and Miss Harle, who found that New York reserve utterly incomprehensible, clung to that chance bond like a precious gift, tracking the Archer ladies down at every continental stop, showing supernatural sharpness in learning when they would pass through London en route to or from New York. The intimacy grew unbreakable: they shared a love of ferns kept in sealed Wardian cases, the craft of macramé lace, the memoirs of Baroness Bunsen, and strong opinions about the preachers at London’s most famous pulpits. By the time Newland got engaged, the families were so close that the two English ladies received a wedding invitation, and sent in return a dainty glass-enclosed bouquet of pressed Alpine flowers. Mrs. Archer’s very last parting order on the New York docks, as Newland and May boarded their steamer for their wedding tour, had been: “You simply must take May to call on Mrs. Carfry.”
They had both fully intended to ignore that gentle injunction, but Mrs. Carfry, with her usual uncanny knack for tracking people down, had found their lodgings and sent a formal dinner invitation—and it was over that very slip of cream vellum that May was now wrinkling her soft, fair brow.
“It’s all very well for you, Newland, you know them,” she said, pouting a little over her muffin. “But I shall feel so shy among a house full of people I’ve never met. And whatever am I supposed to wear?”
Newland leaned back in his chair and smiled at her, marveling at how the soft, moisture-laden London air had deepened the rose in her cheeks, softened the sharp, virginal edge of her features until she looked more Diana-like than he had ever seen her, the glow of her wedded happiness shining through like a lantern lit under clear ice.
“Wear, dearest? I thought a whole trunk full of new gowns had arrived from Paris last week,” he teased.
“Yes, of course,” she huffed, nudging the edge of her plate. “I mean I shan’t know which one to pick. I’ve never dined out in London. I won’t have them thinking I look ridiculous.”
He leaned forward, pretending to ponder her perplexity. “But aren’t Englishwomen dressing just like everyone else for evening, these days?”
“Newland, how absurd!” she laughed. “You’ve seen them troop to the theatre in old, faded ball gowns, their heads completely bare of bonnets or even lace veils!”
“Ah, but Mrs. Carfry and Miss Harle certainly won’t be wearing anything half so bold,” he reassured her. “They’ll turn up in soft silk shawls and little frilled lace caps, exactly like the ones my mother wears at home.”
“But what about the other guests?”
“None of them will be half as lovely as you, dear,” he told her, amused to see she had picked up Janey’s old, morbid obsession with dressing just right for every possible social situation.
She pushed back her chair with a sigh. “That’s sweet of you, but it doesn’t solve my problem.”
He had a sudden, bright idea. “Why not wear your wedding gown? That can’t possibly be wrong, can it?”
“Oh, dearest, if only it were here!” she groaned. “I sent it to Paris last week to have Worth alter it for the winter season, and he hasn’t shipped it back yet.”
“Then look,” he said, standing and drawing back the curtain to show the fog outside was thinning to pale silver. “We can dash for the National Gallery before the light fades, and catch a glimpse of the Turners before we have to worry about dinner at all.”
Their three-month wedding tour had been blissful, as May wrote to all her old girlhood friends, though they had skipped the Italian Lakes Newland had half dreamed of visiting—he had realized quickly he could not picture bright, athletic May among the cypress groves and sun-warmed marble. After a month of fittings in Paris with her dressmakers, they had followed her own itinerary: mountaineering in July around Interlaken and Grindelwald, then August at the quiet, tiny Norman fishing village of Etretat, where May spent whole days swimming, riding, and trying her hand at the new, wildly fashionable game of lawn tennis. Once or twice in the mountains Newland had pointed south over the peaks and said “That’s Italy over there,” and May, her feet tucked in a bed of bright blue gentians, had smiled cheerfully and said they could visit there next winter—if only he did not have to be back in New York for his law practice.
Truth be told, traveling interested her far less than he had expected, once all her new clothes were ordered. The only parts of London that captured her attention at all were the shops and the theatres, though even the theatres paled next to the Paris open-air cafes they had visited under blossoming horse chestnuts on the Champs-Élysées, where she had leaned against his side on the terrace to listen to the music while he carefully translated only the most innocent lines of the songs for her new bridal ears.
Newland had found himself slipping gently back into all the old inherited habits of thought he had dallied with in his bachelor days, chasing all those grand half-formed theories about emancipating his wife and building a marriage free of New York’s stale constraints. It was far simpler, far kinder, to treat May exactly as all his friends treated their own wives—for May had not the faintest idea that she was not free, and the only liberty she would ever choose to take would be to lay it reverently on the altar of her wifely adoration. Her innate dignity would never let that devotion feel abject, and he knew well there might come a day when she would find the strength to pull that gift entirely back, if she thought it was for his own good. But with her uncomplicated, gentle view of marriage, that crisis could only ever come if he did something visibly, unforgivably wrong—and he could not imagine ever hurting her that way. Whatever came, she would always be loyal, gallant, and unresentful, and that pledged him to the same virtues.
His artistic, intellectual life would go on outside the domestic sphere, he told himself. Coming home to her would never feel like stepping into a stuffy, airless room after a long walk in the open, and once they had children, all the empty little corners in both their lives would be filled.
These thoughts hummed softly in his head as their hansom cab trundled slow from Mayfair down to South Kensington, where the Carfrys lived. Even in London, Newland had kept to the old family tradition of traveling as a quiet, unobtrusive sightseer, affecting a haughty unawareness of the other people around him. He had run across the Duke of St. Austrey not long after arriving, and the duke had invited him to visit—but no self-respecting New Yorker would ever consider that a serious invitation, so they had avoided that acquaintance entirely, along with May’s English aunt, the banker’s wife who was still up in Yorkshire, purposefully delaying their arrival in London until autumn so they would not look pushing and snobbish to those distant unknown relatives.
“Probably no one else will even be there,” he said to May, glancing at where she sat beside him, spotless and glowing in her sky-blue cloak edged with soft swansdown, too perfect for the soot-streaked London streets. “The whole of London is empty this time of year, and you’ve made yourself far too beautiful for any ordinary dinner party.”
“I won’t have them thinking Americans dress like savages,” she replied, with a quiet, quiet conviction that struck him all at once—this was the secret armor of even the most unworldly American women, he realized, their defense against the unknown, their gentle defiance of it. That was why May had gone through the solemn, weeks-long ritual of picking and ordering her entire new Paris wardrobe, even though she would never have tied a single ribbon in her hair just to charm him.
He was right that the party was small, in the long, dim, chilly drawing room of the Carfrys’ South Kensington home: only their hostess, Miss Harle, another shawled lady and her genial vicar husband, a quiet pale young man Mrs. Carfry introduced as her nephew, and a small, dark, lively-eyed gentleman with a French name, who was the nephew’s tutor.
May floated into the dim group like a swan strayed into a twilight parlour, her skirts rustling voluminously, her cheeks pink from shyness so deep it felt almost infantile. Newland could read the helpless question in her eyes, What on earth will they expect me to talk about? and the vicar and the young tutor both immediately set to work, gallantly trying to put her at her ease.
Even so, the dinner was a languishing, quiet affair. Newland noticed May’s go-to trick for feeling comfortable around strangers: she leaned entirely into her most uncompromising New York references, talking of Fifth Avenue parties and Mingott family dinners so that, for all her loveliness, her conversation left little room for light repartee. The vicar gave up the effort fairly quickly, and the ladies soon excused themselves back to the drawing room, to the unspoken relief of everyone at the table.
The vicar left shortly after for a parish meeting, the sickly young nephew was packed off to bed, and soon Newland and the French tutor, Monsieur Rivière, were sitting alone over their port, talking more freely than Newland had talked since his last long late-night symposium with Ned Winsett back in New York.
He learned that the young tutor’s father had died young, a low-ranking diplomat, and Rivière had been meant to follow the same career, only his insatiable hunger for books had led him first to journalism, then to unpublished authorship, and finally to tutoring wealthy English boys in Switzerland. He had haunted the Goncourts’ garret in Paris, Maupassant had once told him plainly not to waste his time trying to write fiction, he had talked for hours with Mérimée in his mother’s small Paris drawing room. He had always been desperately poor, supporting his mother and unmarried sister, and his literary ambitions had all but failed—but he told Newland, with bright, passionate eyes, that he would never trade his intellectual liberty for any material comfort.
“The air of ideas is the only air worth breathing, don’t you see?” he said, lighting another cigarette. “To be able to look life in the face, no strings attached—worth living in a garret for, isn’t it? But sometimes I wonder… would there be any work for me, do you think, in New York?”
Newland stared at him, perplexed, wondering how to tell this man who had sat at the feet of all Paris’s greatest literary minds that New York, his own city, had no place for someone who lived for good conversation, not profit. He stammered out a noncommittal reply, and Rivière quickly dropped the subject, rising to lead him back to the drawing room without a trace of awkwardness.
On the drive home, Newland pondered the conversation deeply. That hour with Rivière had filled his lungs with fresh, sharp new air, and his first impulse had been to invite the man to dine with them the very next day. But when he tentatively mentioned that the tutor was a fascinating man, full of interesting talk about books and art, May roused herself from her soft, dreamy silence and looked at him with cold, quiet surprise.
“That little Frenchman? Wasn’t he dreadfully common?” she said. He could see the faint, secret disappointment in her: it was not snobbery, not quite, but the old unspoken New York sense that if you risked your dignity to host or be hosted by foreign acquaintances, you ought to be offered a better guest list than a country vicar and a private tutor. If her parents had invited the Carfrys to Fifth Avenue, they would have laid on a far grander dinner than that.
“Common? Common where?” he asked, a little sharper than he meant to.
“Why, anywhere but inside his schoolroom,” she answered. “Those sorts of people are always so awkward in society. But I suppose I wouldn’t know if he was very clever anyway,” she added, disarming him with a soft smile.
Newland hated the way she used the word “clever” almost as much as he hated the word “common” falling from her lips—but he had begun to fear his own habit of fixating on all the little things about her he did not like. After all, her perspective was exactly the same as every single person he had grown up around, a perspective he had long dismissed as negligible, unimportant, something he could ignore around the edges of his own life.
“Ah, then I won’t invite him to dine,” he laughed.
May blinked at him, bewildered. “Goodness—invite the Carfrys’ tutor? Why on earth would you do that?”
“He’s looking for work in New York,” he said.
“What sort of work? People don’t hire random French tutors for no reason,” she said, indifferent, and laughed when he told her Rivière mostly wanted to move there for the good conversation. “Newland, that’s so funny! How very French of him.”
He was, in the end, quietly relieved that she had dismissed the idea out of hand. He never would have been able to fit M. Rivière into the rigid, predictable world of old New York he was returning to. He felt a cold, sharp flash of insight then: so many of the small problems he would face in his marriage would be solved this way, passively, without any argument. He told himself, as he paid the hansom driver and followed her long train up the steps to their lodgings, that everyone said the first six months of marriage were the hardest, that they were still just rubbing off each other’s sharp little angles. But the worst of it, he thought, the quiet, unnameable ache under his happiness, was that May’s gentle, steady pressure was already wearing away at the very sharp, bright edges of his own curiosity, the very parts of himself he had most wanted to keep.

The small, sun-drenched lawn stretched smooth and bright as polished linen all the way down to the glinting wide blue sea.

The turf was hemmed with a vivid border of scarlet geranium and deep crimson coleus, and chocolate-painted cast iron vases spaced at intervals along the winding gravel path strung loops of soft pink petunia and trailing ivy geranium above the neatly raked stones. Halfway between the cliff edge and the square chocolate-stained wooden house (its verandah tin roof striped yellow and brown to mimic an expensive canvas awning), two large archery targets stood propped against a thicket of shrubbery. Opposite them, across the width of the lawn, a crisp white canvas tent had been pitched, with benches and garden seats clustered around its entrance. Ladies in gossamer summer dresses and gentlemen in grey frock coats and tall top hats milled on the grass or lounged on the seats, and every few minutes a slender girl in starched muslin would step out from the tent, bow in hand, and loose her shaft at the targets, the whole party pausing their chatter to watch its flight.

Newland Archer, leaning against the verandah rail of the house, looked down on the scene with a quiet, unnameable disorientation. Shiny blue china flower pots on bright yellow stands flanked the painted steps, each holding a spiky sharp green palm, and below the verandah ran a wide sweep of blue hydrangeas edged with more scarlet geraniums. Behind him, the French windows of the drawing rooms swayed with lace curtains, giving glimpses of glossy parquet floors dotted with plush chintz poufs, tiny velvet armchairs, and low tables heaped with little silver trinkets.

The Newport Archery Club always held its annual August meet at the Beauforts'. Archery had long reigned unrivaled besides croquet as the most genteel outdoor social amusement, and while lawn tennis was gaining in popularity it was still deemed far too rough, far too unladylike for polite gatherings. As an excuse to show off pretty gowns and graceful, artfully poised movements, the bow and arrow still held complete sway.

Archer stared down at the familiar scene wondering how life could still be going on in its old, unbroken rhythm when his own whole understanding of the world had shifted completely. Newport was the place that drove home just how deep that shift ran. Back in New York the previous winter, after he and May had settled into their new greenish-yellow house with the bow window and the Pompeian vestibule, he'd fallen back with relief into the familiar routine of his law practice, and that daily work had felt like a link to the man he'd been before his wedding. There had been the pleasant thrill of picking out a sleek, showy grey stepper for May's brougham (a gift from the Wellands), and the absorbing, long-dreamed-of work of arranging his new library, which he'd built exactly as he'd wanted against all family doubts: dark embossed wallpaper, heavy Eastlake bookcases, and solid, "sincere" armchairs and tables. He'd met his old friend Winsett again at the Century Club, run with his set of fashionable young men at the Knickerbocker, and between work hours, dinner parties, entertaining at home, and the occasional night at the opera or the theater, his life had felt like the real, inevitable, ordinary future he was supposed to be living.

But Newport was a place with no duties, no excuses, only unbroken, unthinking holiday. Archer had tried to persuade May to spend the summer on a remote wild island off the Maine coast called Mount Desert, where a handful of hardy Boston and Philadelphia families camped in rough local cottages, surrounded by wild woods, rocky shores, and reports of a rough, trapper-like freedom far from New York society's strictures. But the Wellands always summered in Newport, in one of their square, boxy cliffside homes, and Archer could give no good reasonable argument why he and May should not join them there. As Mrs. Welland tartly pointed out, May had spent months wearing herself out trying on summer gowns in Paris only to be forbidden to show them off where everyone could admire them, and Archer had no counter for that kind of logic.

May herself could not understand his vague, unspoken reluctance to fall in with what she thought was such a pleasant, sensible summer plan. She reminded him he had loved Newport in his bachelor days, a fact he could not deny, so he had lied and said he was sure he would love it far more now that they were there together. But standing on the Beaufort verandah, looking out over the bright, chattering crowd on the lawn, he knew with a cold little shiver that he would not like it at all.

It was not May's fault, poor dear. Though they had sometimes fallen slightly out of step during their travels, harmony had always settled back over them once they returned to the familiar social rules May had known her whole life. He had always known she would not disappoint him, and he had been right. He had married her, as most young men of his circle did, because he had met this perfectly charming girl just as a string of aimless, unsatisfying sentimental adventures had left him tired and disgusted. She had represented peace, stability, easy comradeship, the steady, comforting pull of an unavoidable, honorable duty. He could not say he had made a mistake in his choice, for she had fulfilled every single thing he had expected of her. It was undoubtedly a source of quiet pride to be the husband of one of the most beautiful, popular young married women in New York, especially one as sweet-tempered and reasonable as she was, and Archer had never been blind to those advantages. He had trained himself to think of that foolish, feverish last-minute madness on the eve of his wedding, the dream that he could ever run off and marry the Countess Olenska, as the last of his discarded boyish follies, and Ellen had long existed in his memory only as the softest, most plaintive of all his discarded ghosts. But all those deliberate erasures had left his mind an empty, echoing place, and the chattering, laughing people on the Beaufort lawn shocked him now, like children playing carelessly on top of a graveyard.

He heard the soft rustle of skirts beside him, and the Marchioness Manson fluttered out of the drawing room window, as festooned and bedizened as ever: her limp Leghorn hat tied to her head with yards of faded gauze, a tiny black velvet parasol on a carved ivory handle balanced absurdly over its wide brim. "My dear Newland! I had no idea you and May had arrived! You only got here yesterday, you say? Ah, business, professional duties... I understand perfectly. So many husbands can only join their wives here for weekends, you know." She cocked her head to one side, languishing at him through half-screwed eyelids. "Marriage is one long sacrifice, as I always used to remind my Ellen--"

Archer's heart gave that strange, sharp jolt it had once before, as if a heavy door had slammed shut between him and the whole rest of the world, but the split second of disorientation must have passed fast, for he soon heard Medora answering a question he must have spoken out loud. "No, I'm not staying here, I'm at the Blenkers', in their delicious little rural solitude at Portsmouth. Beaufort was kind enough to send his famous trotters for me this morning so I could catch at least a glimpse of one of Regina's famous garden parties, but I go back to country life this evening. The Blenkers, such dear original people, have rented a primitive old farmhouse at Portsmouth where they gather all the most interesting, representative people around them..." She drooped a little under the shade of her parasol brim, adding with a faint blush: "This week Dr. Agathon Carver is holding a whole series of Inner Thought meetings there. What a contrast to this giddy worldly pleasure, but I have always lived for contrast! To me the only real death is monotony. I always tell Ellen: beware of monotony, it is the mother of every deadly sin. But my poor child is going through a phase of high-mindedness, of utter abhorrence for society. You know, of course, that she has turned down every invitation to stay in Newport this summer, even her grandmother Mingott's? I could barely persuade her to come to the Blenkers' at all, if you can believe it! The life she leads is morbid, unnatural. Ah, if only she had listened to me when things could still have been different... when the door was still open... But shall we go down and watch this absorbing match? I hear your May is one of the competitors."

Beaufort strolled toward them from the tent across the lawn: tall, heavy, straining a little too tight at the seams of his tailored London frock coat, a rare orchid pinned to his buttonhole. Archer, who had not seen him in two or three months, was struck by how much he had changed. Under the harsh summer sun, his usually ruddy complexion looked heavy and bloated, and if he did not walk so straight and square-shouldered he would have looked like an overfed, overdressed old man.

There were endless rumors swirling around Beaufort. That spring he had gone on a long cruise to the West Indies on his brand new steam yacht, and word had spread that a lady who looked exactly like Miss Fanny Ring had been seen in his company at every port they stopped at. The Clyde-built yacht, fitted with tiled bathrooms and other unheard-of luxuries, was said to have cost him half a million dollars, and the enormous pearl necklace he had given his wife on his return was the very picture of the lavish expiatory gifts men gave their wives to make up for very public affairs. Beaufort's fortune was substantial enough to absorb that kind of expense, but the uneasy whispers persisted, not just on Fifth Avenue but all through Wall Street. Some said he had made terrible bad speculations in railway stock, others that he was being bled dry by one of the most greedy mistresses in the city, and to every new rumor of looming insolvency Beaufort responded with some new, even more extravagant display: a whole new row of orchid houses, a new string of racehorses, a new Meissonnier or Cabanel painting for his gallery.

He walked up to the Marchioness and Newland with his usual half-sneering smile. "Hullo, Medora! Did the trotters treat you well? Forty minutes flat, eh? Not bad at all, considering we had to go slow to spare your nerves." He shook Archer's hand, then stepped to Medora's other side, and muttered a few quiet words that Archer could not catch. Medora replied with one of her odd, jerky foreign gestures, a "Que voulez-vous?" that deepened Beaufort's frown, but he put on a perfectly convincing congratulatory smile as he turned to Archer. "You know May is going to carry off the first prize, no question."

"Ah, then the prize will stay right in the family," Medora trilled, just as they reached the tent and Regina Beaufort stepped out to meet them, wreathed in girlish clouds of mauve muslin and floating veils.

May Welland was just stepping out of the tent then. In her white dress, with a pale green ribbon around her waist and a wreath of ivy in her hat, she had the same cool, Diana-like aloofness she had worn the night she walked into the Beaufort ballroom for their engagement party. Not a single thought or heavy feeling seemed to have left a mark on her since that night, and though her husband knew she was capable of both, he marveled again at how easily every experience seemed to slide right off her.

She held her bow and arrow in her hand, stepped onto the chalk mark traced on the turf, lifted the bow to her shoulder, and took aim. The attitude was so full of classic grace that a soft murmur of appreciation rippled through the onlookers, and Archer felt that familiar warm glow of pride and proprietorship that so often tricked him into momentary happiness. Her rivals -- Mrs. Reggie Chivers, the Merry girls, a whole cluster of rosy Thorleys, Dagonets, and Mingotts -- stood behind her in a soft, lovely anxious group, brown heads and golden heads bent over their score cards, pale muslin and flower-wreathed hats blending into a tender rainbow. All of them were young and pretty, bathed in the soft glow of summer, but not one had that nymph-like easy grace his wife had when she tensed her muscles, a faint happy frown on her face, and poured all her focus into hitting her mark.

"Gad," Archer heard Lawrence Lefferts say, "not one of the lot holds the bow half as well as she does." Beaufort laughed and retorted, "Yes, but that's the only kind of target she'll ever hit."

Archer felt a burst of irrational anger. In any other circumstance, a coarse man calling his wife too nice, too unapproachable, would have felt like a compliment, proof of her purity. But Beaufort's words sent a faint shiver through his chest. What if that "niceness" carried to that perfect, unassailable degree was nothing but a negation, a thick curtain dropped tight in front of an emptiness he had never once managed to see past? He watched May walk back, flushed and calm after her final perfect bullseye, and for a second he felt like he had never once lifted that curtain to see what was behind it.

She accepted the congratulations from her rivals and the rest of the party with the simple grace that was her greatest charm. No one could ever be jealous of her triumphs, because she gave off the quiet, unmistakeable feeling that she would have been exactly as serene and happy if she had missed every shot. But when her eyes met her husband's, her face lit up with the clear, unmistakeable pleasure she took in his approval.

Mrs. Welland's wicker pony carriage was waiting for them, and they drove off among the dispersing line of other carriages, May holding the reins and Archer sitting at her side. The late afternoon sunlight still gilded the bright lawns and shrubbery, and up and down Bellevue Avenue rolled a steady double line of victorias, dog-carts, landaus and vis-à-vis, carrying well-dressed ladies and gentlemen away from the Beaufort garden party, or home from their daily turn along the Ocean Drive.

"Shall we go visit Granny?" May suggested suddenly. "I want to tell her myself that I won the prize. There's plenty of time before dinner."

Archer agreed, and May turned the ponies down Narragansett Avenue, crossed Spring Street, and drove out toward the rocky, moorland stretch beyond the fashionable district. It was there, in this unfashionable, cheap stretch of land overlooking the bay, that Catherine the Great Mingott, who had always cared nothing for precedent and hated wasting money, had built her gaudy, many-peaked, cross-beamed cottage-orné when she was young. In a thicket of stunted oaks, her wide verandahs jutted out over the bay dotted with small islands. A winding drive led up between cast iron stags and blue glass balls half buried in mounds of geraniums, to a front door of highly polished walnut under a striped verandah roof. Behind it ran a narrow hall with a black and yellow star-patterned parquet floor, opening onto four small square rooms lined with heavy flock wallpaper, their ceilings covered in every god and goddess of Olympus that an Italian house painter could possibly dream up. One of those rooms had been turned into her bedroom when her weight grew too great to easily climb stairs, and she spent all her days in the adjoining sitting room, enthroned in a huge armchair between the open door and open window, forever waving a palm-leaf fan that the great swell of her bosom held so far from the rest of her that the air it moved only ever stirred the fringe of the anti-macassars on her chair arms.

Since she had been the one to speed up his marriage, old Catherine had treated Archer with the same cordial warmth one feels for someone you helped to get exactly what they wanted. She was fully convinced that irrepressible passion was the reason for his hasty wedding, and as someone who adored impulsiveness (as long as it did not lead to anyone wasting money unnecessarily) she always greeted him with a genial, conspiratorial twinkle and little playful allusions that May, luckily, seemed completely impervious to.

She examined the diamond-tipped arrow that had been pinned to May's dress as her prize, remarking that in her day a simple filigree brooch would have been thought more than enough, but there was no denying that Beaufort did things extremely handsomely. "Quite a proper heirloom, my dear," the old lady chuckled, pinching May's soft white arm. "You have to leave it in fee to your eldest girl." The color flooded May's face so fast it was like a flag being raised. "Well well, what did I say to make you blush so red? No daughters, only boys, eh? Good gracious, look at her blushing over her blushes! What, I can't even say that either? Mercy me, when my children beg me to paint all those gods and goddesses off the ceiling I always tell them I'm far too grateful to have people around me that nothing can shock!"

Archer burst out laughing, and May echoed him, crimson all the way to the corners of her eyes.

"Now tell me all about the party, you two, because I will never get a straight word of it out of that silly Medora," the old ancestress continued. When May exclaimed, "Cousin Medora? But I thought she was going back to Portsmouth?" Mrs. Mingott answered placidly: "Oh, she will, she will, but she has to stop here first to pick up Ellen. Ah -- you didn't know Ellen was coming to spend the day with me? Such nonsense, her refusing to come stay for the whole summer, but I gave up arguing with young people about fifty years ago. Ellen -- Ellen!" she cried in her shrill old voice, trying to lean far enough forward to catch a glimpse of the lawn down past the verandah.

There was no answer, and Mrs. Mingott rapped impatiently with her stick on the shiny parquet floor. A mulatto maid in a bright turban answered the summons, and told her mistress she had seen "Miss Ellen" walking down the path to the shore. Mrs. Mingott turned to Archer. "Run down and fetch her, like a good grandson; this pretty girl here will describe the whole party to me," she said, and Archer stood up like he was moving in a dream.

He had heard the Countess Olenska's name spoken dozens of times in the year and a half since he had last seen her, and knew all

Mr. Welland set down his knife and fork with a sharp, bewildered clink, as if he’d just heard the most absurd piece of Newport gossip in all his decades of proper society life. “A party for the Blenkers? The Blenkers?” He peered across the luncheon table at his wife, half-convinced she was playing some elaborate high-comedy prank on him.

Mrs. Welland adjusted her gilded spectacles and read the invitation out loud in the bright, exasperated tone reserved for the most unforgivable of social missteps: “Professor and Mrs. Emerson Sillerton request the pleasure of Mr. and Mrs. Welland's company at the meeting of the Wednesday Afternoon Club on August 25th at 3 o'clock punctually. To meet Mrs. and the Misses Blenkers. Red Gables, Catherine Street. R. S. V. P.”

“Good gracious,” Mr. Welland gasped, as if a second full read was required to make the absurdity of the request fully sink in.

“Poor Amy Sillerton,” Mrs. Welland sighed, tucking the card back into its embossed envelope. “You never can tell what that husband of hers will dream up next. I expect he’s only just stumbled across the Blenkers and decided they’re his latest pet cause to champion.”

Emerson Sillerton was the bane of every respectable Newport hostess’s existence: a thorn no one dared pluck, for his family tree was rooted deep in the oldest, most revered New England bloodlines. He’d had every advantage money and position could buy, as Mrs. Welland had been known to point out for hours over tea—nothing on earth forced him to waste his days digging up old potsherds as an archaeologist, to traipse around Newport in winter when all decent folk fled to Manhattan, or flout every unwritten rule of society just for the fun of it. Worst of all, he’d dragged poor Amy Dagonet, who’d been bred for a life of Paris shopping and Italian villa holidays, along on his mad adventures, filling their home with long-haired male poets and short-haired female suffragists, dragging her off to explore Yucatan tombs instead of the Riviera. No one in the Mingott circle could fathom why she put up with it, but put up with it she did, and every year the whole set was forced to draw straws to send a reluctant representative to their dreary garden parties, lest they snub a family related by blood to half of old New York.

“Miracle they didn’t schedule it on the Cup Race,” Mrs. Welland remarked. “Remember two years ago, they threw a party for that West African opera singer on the very day of Julia Mingott’s big thé dansant? At least there’s no other engagement clashing this time. Some of us will have to go, of course, we can’t slight Amy.”

Mr. Welland’s face crumpled with quiet anxiety. “Some of us, my dear—more than one? Three o’clock is the most awkward hour. I have to be right back here at half past three to take my drops, Bencomb’s new treatment won’t do a bit of good if I don’t stick to it, and if I pop in late I’ll miss my whole afternoon drive.”

“There’s no need for you to come at all, dearest,” his wife soothed, that automatic cheerful edge to her voice she reserved for her husband’s little ailments. “I’ve got calling cards to leave on the far end of Bellevue Avenue anyway, I’ll stop by around half past three, stay long enough to make Amy feel no one’s snubbed her.” She glanced over at her daughter, May, then Newland. “And if Newland’s got his afternoon sorted, May can drive you out with the ponies, test that new russet harness we just had fitted.”

Ordering every minute of everyone’s day was a sacred Welland family principle. The horror of having to “kill time”—especially for anyone who didn’t care for whist or solitaire—haunted Mrs. Welland the way the suffering of the unemployed haunts a well-meaning philanthropist. She was also fiercely committed to never visibly interfering with her married daughter’s plans, a balancing act that kept her own schedule stuffed to the brim, not a single free second left unaccounted for.

“Of course I’ll drive with Papa,” May said softly, turning that bright, sweet smile on her new husband, as if gently reminding him he’d forgotten to make plans for himself. Mrs. Welland had been fretting over Newland’s lack of foresight for the whole fortnight he’d been staying under their roof. More than once, when she’d asked what he meant to do with his afternoon, he’d laughed and said he planned to “save it instead of spend it,” and once, when she and May had been dragged off on a long round of overdue calls, he’d admitted he’d spent the whole day napping on a rock down by the beach. “He doesn’t need plans,” May had serenely assured her mother, back then. “If there’s nothing to do, he just reads a book. Just like his father.” And the subject had been dropped.

But as the day of the Sillerton party drew near, May kept trying to find something fun for Newland to do to keep him from being bored while she was occupied: a tennis match at the Chiverses’, a sail on Julius Beaufort’s fancy new cutter. “I’ll be home by six, Papa never drives later than that,” she promised him, and brightened immediately when Newland said he planned to hire a runabout and drive up the island to a stud farm, to look at that second horse they’d been hunting for May’s brougham for weeks. It was such a sensible, proper plan May shot her mother a triumphant little look, as if to say *See? He knows how to fill his hours just fine.*

The idea had popped into Newland’s head the very moment he’d heard about the Sillertons’ invitation, and he’d hidden it like a secret, half-afraid someone would talk him out of it. He’d already booked the runabout ahead of time, a light little cart with two steady old trotters that could still cover eighteen miles without breaking a sweat, and at two o’clock he bolted from the luncheon table, jumped in the carriage, and drove off.

The day was perfect: a sharp north wind puffed little tufts of white cloud across a sky so blue it hurt to look at, the Atlantic glinting bright and silver below. Bellevue Avenue was all but empty at that hour, he dropped the stable boy off at Mill Street, then turned down the Old Beach Road and trotted across Eastman’s Beach, that giddy, wild excitement thrumming in his veins he remembered from half-holidays at school, when he’d sneak off to explore nowhere in particular.

He’d known the second he’d heard about the Sillerton’s party that the whole Blenker household would be dragged off to Newport for the event. The Marchioness Manson would be there, no doubt, and surely Ellen Olenska would have gone along too, leaving their tumbledown little cottage empty. He couldn’t even say for sure he wanted to speak to her. Ever since he’d spotted her on the cliff above the bay, he’d been consumed by this irrational, aching craving just to see the place she lived, to walk the same paths she walked, to carry home the memory of how the sky and the sea curved around her little patch of land, to make the rest of the empty world he was trapped in feel a little less hollow. It was a hunger he couldn’t name, not even to himself.

He stopped for five minutes at the stud farm, pretended to test the horse even though he saw at a glance it was far too lame for May’s brougham, just to prove to himself he wasn’t rushing. Then at three o’clock he shook the reins and turned down the side roads leading to Portsmouth. The wind had died down, a faint haze hanging over the horizon promising a fog would creep up the Saconnet River when the tide turned, but all the fields and woods around him were bathed in soft, honeyed golden light. He trotted past weathered grey farmhouses tucked in apple orchards, hayfields heavy with cut grass, villages with white steeples pricking the softening sky, stopped to ask directions from a group of farmhands working in a field, then turned down a narrow lane banked high with goldenrod and tangled brambles.

At the end of the lane, he caught the glint of blue river water, and to the left, half hidden by a clump of oaks and maples, stood a long, weather-beaten house, its white paint peeling off the clapboards in great flaking strips. An open shed for farm wagons stood by the gate, he tied his two trotters there, and walked up the path. The front lawn had grown wild, half turned back into hayfield, but off to the left there was an overgrown box hedge garden full of wilting dahlias and rusty old rose bushes, circling a rickety trellis summerhouse that had once been painted white, topped by a chipped wooden Cupid that had lost his bow and arrow, still pointing uselessly out over the garden.

He leaned against the gate for a long while, no sound coming from the open windows of the house, an old grizzled Newfoundland dog napping on the porch too lazy to even lift his head. This quiet, decaying little place was the home of the loud, bohemian Blenkers, and he felt his whole body go soft at the thought that Ellen Olenska had walked these very paths.

He drifted across the wild lawn, towards the summerhouse, and spotted a flash of bright pink half-hidden on the rickety wooden bench inside: a silk parasol. It drew him like a magnet, he was certain it was hers. He picked it up, the carved handle made of some rare, scented sandalwood that released a faint warm perfume when he ran his thumb over it. He lifted the handle to his lips.

Then he heard the rustle of skirts through the box hedge. He froze, hands still curled around the parasol handle, not looking up, waiting for her.

“Oh, Mr. Archer!” a loud, cheerful young voice exclaimed.

He looked up to find the biggest, blondest, most blowsy of the Blenker daughters standing there, her muslin dress bedraggled, a faint red mark on her cheek from where she’d been napping against a pillow, her eyes half foggy with sleep. “Gracious, where did you come from? I was fast asleep in the hammock! Everyone else went off to that party in Newport. Did you ring the bell?”

Archer was flustered, his face hot. “I—no, I was on my way up island to look at a horse, and I drove over on the chance I’d find your mother and your guests here. The house looked empty, so I thought I’d sit and wait a while.”

Miss Blenker was shaking the last of the sleep off, staring at him with obvious, delighted interest. “The house is empty! Mum’s gone, the Marchioness Manson too, everyone but me. I couldn’t go, I’ve had a terrible sore throat, and Mum was scared the drive home in the dark would make it worse. Isn’t that the worst luck? Though I wouldn’t have minded a bit if I’d known you were coming,” she simpered, leaning closer.

Archer cut in before she could start flirting in earnest. “Wait—Madame Olenska? She’s gone to Newport too?”

Miss Blenker blinked at him, surprised. “Madame Olenska? Didn’t you know she was called away? Oh, that’s my best parasol! I lent it to that silly Katie yesterday, it matched her ribbons, the idiot must have dropped it here. We Blenkers are all such messy Bohemians, you know!” She plucked the parasol right out of his hand, unfurling the frilly pink silk over her head. “Yes, Ellen got a telegram from Boston yesterday, she left last night, said she might be gone two whole days. We all call her Ellen, she’s so sweet, doesn’t stand on ceremony. Don’t you love the way she does her hair? She looks just like that actress Mrs. Scott-Siddons when she recites *Lady Geraldine’s Courtship*, doesn’t she?”

Archer stared right through her, the pink parasol bobbing over her giggling head suddenly the cruelest joke. The place he’d driven miles to find, the little cottage he’d daydreamed of sharing a quiet minute with Ellen in, she wasn’t even here. The parasol he’d just pressed to his lips wasn’t even hers.

“Did the telegram say if it was bad news?” he asked, his voice tight. “I’m going to Boston tomorrow, I could look in on her if she needs anything.”

Miss Blenker barely even cared anymore, she was already looking away towards the lane. “Oh, that’s so kind! She’s staying at the Parker House. Must be dreadfully muggy there this weather.”

Archer barely heard the rest of what she said. He fumbled through polite small talk, firmly refused her repeated invitations to stay for high tea when the rest of the family got home, untied his trotters from the shed, and climbed back into the runabout. As he turned the cart around at the end of the lane, he glanced back, and saw Miss Blenker standing at the gate, waving that silly pink parasol at him. His mind was already made up. He was taking the first train to Boston tomorrow, no matter what the unbreakable rules of old New York had to say about it.

The next morning, Archer stepped off the Fall River train and stepped straight into the thick, steaming breath of a mid-summer Boston. Streets near the station reeked of spilled beer, burnt coffee, and overripe fruit rotting in vendor crates, and crowds of men in rolled-up shirt sleeves wandered past with the loose, unselfconscious ease of boarders trundling down a boarding house hall to the shared bathroom. No European city could ever look this unpolished, this soaked through with unruly domesticity, no matter how high the heat rose: maids in calico gowns lolled on the front steps of wealthy brownstones, and the Common still bore the litter and sticky sweet traces of a Masonic picnic that had wrapped the night before.

If he had ever tried to imagine Ellen Olenska in an impossible, out-of-place scene, he could never have dreamed up a setting more ill-suited to her quiet, otherworldly grace than this heat-drunk, unkempt, half-abandoned city. He hailed a cab and drove straight to the Somerset Club for breakfast, eating with the sharp, unhurried appetite of a man who has spent weeks half-starved, working his way through a slice of cold melon first, then flipping idly through the morning paper while he waited for toast and scrambled eggs. A bright, thrumming new energy had hummed under his skin ever since he’d told May the night before that urgent last-minute business required him to catch the Fall River boat that night, and head back to New York two days early. The pretense had been easy, almost alarmingly so: the forged urgent office note he’d left conspicuously on the hall table before he left Portsmouth had justified every part of his changed plan, and for one fleeting, uncomfortable moment he’d thought he was pulling the same kind of slippery, clever trick Lawrence Lefferts used to sneak off on his little assignations. But the guilt slid right off him; he was in no mood for overthinking.

After breakfast he lit a cigarette and skimmed the *Commercial Advertiser*, and nodded and exchanged small talk with two or three acquaintances who wandered in. It was the same old world, he thought, but he had the giddy, disorienting sense that he had slipped through a crack in time and space, that he was moving through it half outside the rules he had followed his whole life. He checked his watch, saw it was half past nine, and stood up to head for the writing room. There he scribbled a short note, paid a messenger to take a cab straight to the Parker House, and wait for a reply. He settled back behind another newspaper, counting out every minute it would take a cab to drive across the city.

“The lady was out, sir,” a waiter’s voice murmured suddenly at his elbow.

Archer stammered, “Out?” as if the word was in a foreign tongue he could not parse. He stumbled to the hall. That could not be. She could not be out that early. Hot, sharp anger pricked at him at his own stupidity: why had he not sent that note the second he stepped off the train? He grabbed his hat and stick and stepped back out onto the street, and the whole city suddenly felt vast, empty, as unrecognizable as a foreign land he had never visited before. He hesitated on the doorstep for a long second, then set off for the Parker House anyway. What if the messenger had gotten bad information? What if she was still there?

He cut across the Common, and there on the first bench under an oak tree, he saw her. She held a grey silk sunshade tilted over her head—he had somehow always stupidly imagined her with a pink one, he realized, shocked at his own foolishness. She sat listless, like she had nowhere in the world to go, her profile soft and drooping, her hair knotted low at the nape of her neck under her dark hat, her long, slightly wrinkled gloved hand curled loosely around the parasol handle. She turned, and saw him.

“Oh,” she said, and for the first time he saw a flash of pure startled surprise on her face, which softened a second later into a slow, wondering, contented smile. “Oh,” she murmured again, the note softer this time, and without standing up she shifted to make space for him on the bench.

“I’m here on business—just got here,” Archer babbled, and for no reason he could name he faked his own shock at seeing her. “But what on earth are you doing in this wilderness?” He could barely hear his own voice over the thud of his heart; it felt like he was shouting across miles of empty space, terrified she would vanish before he could reach her.

“I? Oh, I’m here on business too,” she answered, turning her head so they were face to face. Her voice barely registered, except as a shock: he had forgotten entirely how it sounded, low and warm, with a faint, rough softness catching on every consonant.

“You do your hair differently,” he blurted, his heart hammering so hard he thought it might burst through his ribs, as if he had said something irrevocable and foolish.

“Differently? No—only that I do the best I can with no Nastasia around to help.”

“Nastasia? But she’s not with you?”

“No. I’m alone. Two days wasn’t long enough to drag her all the way out here.”

“You’re alone—at the Parker House?”

She flashed him that old familiar glint of playful malice. “Does that strike you as dangerous?”

“No, not dangerous—”

“But unconventional? I see. I suppose it is.” She paused for a beat, the faint edge of irony lingering in her eyes. “I hadn’t thought of it, actually. Because I just did something far more unconventional. I refused to take back a very large sum of money that belonged to me.”

Archer sprang to his feet and paced a few steps away. She furled her parasol, sat absently drawing little patterns in the gravel at her feet, and he walked back to stand in front of her a minute later.

“Someone came here to meet you? With that offer?”

She nodded.

“And you refused—because of the conditions?”
