“I refused,” she said, after a quiet pause.

He sank back down beside her. “What were the conditions?”

“Oh, they were not harsh. Just that I would come back, and sit at the head of his dinner table now and then.”

Silence settled between them. Archer’s chest felt like it had slammed shut, all the words he wanted to say tangled up somewhere he could not reach them. “He wants you back—at any price?”

“Well. A very high price, at least. Far more money than I have ever known what to do with.” He hesitated, beating around the question he knew he had to ask. “You came here to meet him, didn’t you?”

She stared, then burst out laughing. “Meet him? My husband? Here? At this time of year he’s always at Cowes or Baden.”

“He sent someone else?”

“Yes.”

“With a letter?”

She shook her head. “No, just a message. He never writes. I don’t think I’ve gotten more than one letter from him in all our years of marriage.” A faint flush rose to her cheeks, and the heat of her embarrassment burned in Archer’s own face, bright and hot.

“Why does he never write?”

“Why should he? That’s what secretaries are for.”

Archer’s blush deepened. She said the word so simply, as if it had no hidden, shameful weight at all. For a second he almost asked if he had sent his secretary, but the memory of the single brutal letter Count Olenski had ever sent his wife rose sharp in his mind, and he bit the question back. He took a breath, and plunged on. “And this person—this emissary?”

“The emissary,” Madame Olenska smiled, “could leave any time for all I care. But he insisted on waiting till this evening… just in case… on the chance I might change my mind.”

“And you came out here to think that chance over?”

“I came out for a breath of air. The hotel is far too stifling. I’m taking the afternoon train back to Portsmouth.”

They sat silent for a long while, not looking at each other, but straight ahead at the people strolling past on the path. Finally she turned her eyes back to his face. “You haven’t changed at all.”

He nearly answered *I was, till I saw you again*, but instead he stood abruptly, glancing around the sweltering, untidy park. “This is horrible. Why shouldn’t we go out on the bay for a little while? There’s a breeze, it will be cooler out on the water. We could take the steamboat down to Point Arley.” She glanced up at him, hesitating, and he pushed on. “No one will be on that boat on a Monday morning. My train back to New York doesn’t leave till this evening. Why shouldn’t we go?” He looked down at her, and suddenly the words broke out unbidden. “Haven’t we done absolutely everything we possibly can to stay apart, already?”

“Oh,” she murmured. She stood up, unfurled her sunshade, glancing around at the park like she was asking permission of the trees to leave, to step into something they had both been craving for months. Then her eyes returned to his face. “You mustn’t say things like that to me.”

“I’ll say anything you want. Or nothing at all. I won’t open my mouth unless you tell me to. What harm could this possibly do to anyone? All I want is to listen to you, to hear what you’ve been doing all these months.”

She pulled out a tiny gold-faced watch on an enamel chain.

“Don’t calculate the time,” he burst out. “Give me the day. I want to get you away from that man waiting for you at the hotel. What time was he supposed to arrive?”

Her cheeks flushed again. “Eleven.”

“Then we have to go right now.”

“You don’t have to be afraid—if I don’t come back with you.”

“Nor you, if you do. I swear I only want to hear about you. It feels like a hundred years since we last spoke. It might be another hundred before we ever get this chance again.”

She still wavered, her anxious eyes fixed on his face. “Why didn’t you come down to the beach to fetch me, the day I was at Granny’s house?”

“Because you never looked around. Because I swore I wouldn’t bother you unless you looked for me.” He laughed, struck by how childish the confession sounded.

“But I didn’t look around on purpose.”

“On purpose?”

“I knew you were there. I recognized the ponies pulling your carriage the second you drove up. So I went down to the beach.”

“To get as far away from me as you possibly could?”

She repeated the words, soft and quiet. “To get as far away from you as I possibly could.”

Archer laughed, bright and boyish and giddy with satisfaction. “Well, you see it didn’t work. I might as well tell you now—all the business I came here for was just to find you. Now hurry, we have to go or we’ll miss our boat.”

“Our boat?” She frowned, confused for half a second, then smiled. “Oh, but I have to go back to the hotel first. I have to leave a note for him.”

“Write as many notes as you want. You can write them right here.” He pulled out his notecase and one of the new stylographic pens he had bought just a week before. “I even have an envelope. You see? Everything is predestined. There, rest the paper on your knee, I’ll get this pen working for you in a second. They’re fussy, you have to jostle the ink flow—wait—” He banged his hand holding the pen against the back of the bench, hard, “It’s like shaking down the mercury in a thermometer. Just a little trick. Now try.”

She laughed, bending over the sheet of paper he’d laid on his notecase, scribbling the note. Archer walked a few paces away, staring through unfocused, glowing eyes at the passersby, who paused to gawk at the unheard-of sight of a fashionable lady writing a note on her knee, on a public park bench. Madame Olenska slipped the sheet into the envelope, wrote a name on the front, and tucked it into her pocket. Then she stood up, too.

They walked back toward Beacon Street, and right outside the Somerset Club Archer spotted the same plush-lined herdic cab that had carried his note to the Parker House, its driver taking a break to splash cold water on his forehead at the corner hydrant. “I told you it was all predestined! Here’s our cab right now!” They both laughed, stunned at the miracle of hailing a public cab at ten o’clock in the morning in a city where cab stands were still considered a weird, foreign novelty. Archer checked his watch: they had plenty of time to stop by the Parker House before heading for the steamboat wharf. They rattled over hot, rutted cobblestones and pulled up in front of the hotel doors.

Archer held out his hand for her letter. “Shall I take it in for you?” But she shook her head, hopped out, and vanished through the glass doors. It was barely half past ten, but Archer’s chest twisted with anxiety—what if the emissary was already there, impatient, waiting, and had cornered her before she could leave? He paced up and down the sidewalk in front of the cab. A Sicilian boy with eyes exactly like Nastasia’s tried to shine his boots, an Irish matron tried to sell him peaches, and every few seconds the swinging hotel doors opened to let out another hot man in a tipped-back straw hat, all looking identical to every other hot man in the whole country, he thought.

Then suddenly a face emerged that did not look like all the others. He only caught a flash of it, as he turned at the end of his pacing path: among all the lank, weary, round-faced, mild hotel guests, this young man was pale, sharp, alive in a way no one else around him was, unmistakably foreign. Archer reached for a thread of memory, but it snapped, and the stranger vanished into the crowd. He figured that was the emissary. Panic clawed at his throat. If she did not come out in the next minute, he was going in after her.

The doors swung open again, and she was at his side. They climbed back into the herdic, and as it rattled off toward the wharf he pulled out his watch: she had been gone exactly three minutes. The cab’s loose windows clattered too loud for talking, and they bumped over disjointed cobblestones all the way to the dock.

Sitting side by side on a bench on the half-empty steamboat, they found they barely needed to speak at all. All the things they had to say hummed unspoken between them, in the blessed, quiet relief of finally being released from the world that had kept them apart, finally entirely alone. As the paddle wheels began to turn, and the wharves and warehouses receded through the haze of summer heat, it felt to Archer like all the old familiar world of habit, of obligation, of the rules of New York society that had hemmed them both in for so long, was receding with it. He wanted to ask Ellen if she felt it too: that they were sailing off on some long voyage from which they might never return. But he was afraid to speak it, afraid to shatter the delicate, fragile trust she had in him. For months the memory of their single kiss had burned on his lips, but now that she was here beside him, drifting out into this unknown new world, they had reached a deeper, softer kind of nearness that the wrong word, the wrong touch, could shatter entirely.

The boat cleared the harbour and turned toward open water, and a fresh cool breeze curled around them, the bay stretching out ahead in long oily swells, then ripples tipped with fine spray. The sultry fog still hung low over Boston behind them, but ahead lay a whole new world of ruffled blue water, and distant green headlands with lighthouses glinting in the sun. Madame Olenska leaned back against the boat rail, drinking in the cool air with her lips parted. She had wrapped a long sheer veil around her hat, leaving her face uncovered, and Archer could see the soft, tranquil, unguarded gaiety on her face. She took their little adventure completely in stride: no fear of unexpected meetings, no foolish giddy elation that would make this feel like a sordid assignation.

When they reached the inn, Archer had hoped they would have the bare little dining room entirely to themselves, but they found a loud, cheerful group of schoolteachers on holiday, and his heart sank at the thought of having to shout their conversation over all their noise. “This is hopeless. I’ll ask for a private room,” he said, and Madame Olenska made no protest, waiting while he spoke to the landlord. The small room opened onto a long wooden verandah, the sea rolling in right beneath the windows. It was bare and cool, a table covered in coarse checkered cloth holding a jar of pickles and a blueberry pie tucked under a wire food cage. No more innocent little private room had ever sheltered a pair of people who the whole world thought had no business being alone together. Archer saw the quiet, amused reassurance in the smile she gave him as she sat down across the table from him. This woman who had run away from her cruel husband, who society had gossiped about relentlessly, had mastered the art of taking things in stride—but the quiet, simple, unapologetic calm of her composure brushed away all the stuffy old conventions, and made it feel like the most natural thing in the whole world for two old friends who had spent months aching to speak, finally to be entirely alone together, with no one watching.

They lunched slow, salt wind seeping through the cottage’s warped kitchen window to ruffle the edge of the linen tablecloth, long soft stretches of silence filling the gaps between their rushes of speech. Now that the fragile spell of their long separation had finally broken, they had a thousand tangled things to say, but half the time the words faded away entirely, leaving only that quiet, throbbing duet of shared quiet that needed no translation. Archer never once turned the conversation to his own tangled affairs with May, not out of any conscious plan, but because he dared not miss a single syllable of the story of the year and a half they had spent apart. Ellen leaned across the table, chin resting on her laced-clasped hands, and told him everything.

She had grown tired of what New York insisted on calling “society.” The city had been so kind to her, almost oppressively warm in its welcome when she came home, but once the first novelty wore off, she had found herself too fundamentally “different” to care for the trivialities everyone else there chased. So she had decided to try Washington, where rumor said you met all sorts of people, all sorts of opinions that didn’t fit the staid New York mold. More than likely, she would settle there for good, make a quiet little home for poor Medora, who had worn out the patience of every other relation just when she most needed someone to watch over her, to yank her out of the path of the foolish predatory marriages she kept stumbling into.

“But Dr. Carver—aren’t you afraid of him? I hear he’s been staying with you out at the Blenkers’.” Archer frowned.

Ellen laughed, bright and unconcerned. “Oh, the Carver danger’s long past. He’s a very clever man, you know. He wants a rich wife to fund all his grand social experiments, and Medora’s just a handy, good-looking advertisement as a newly converted follower, nothing more.”

“Converted to what?”

“All sorts of wild, bright new social schemes. And do you know, they interest me far more than the blind, unthinking conformity to someone else’s old traditions that I see all our New York friends clinging to. It seems so stupid, doesn’t it? Men sailed all the way across the ocean to discover a whole new continent, only to turn it into a pale, cheap copy of the old one they left behind.” She smiled across the table at him. “Do you really think Christopher Columbus would have braved all that storm and danger just to go to the opera with the Selfridge Merrys?”

Archer’s face burned. “And Beaufort—do you say these sorts of things to Beaufort?” he asked, abrupt and sharp with sudden unnameable jealousy.

“I haven’t seen him in months. But I used to, once. And he understood.”

“Ah, that’s what I’ve always known. You don’t really like us. You like Beaufort because he’s nothing like us.” He glanced around the bare, whitewashed room, out at the empty stretch of beach, the stark row of village houses strung along the shore like discarded beads. “We’re so damnably dull. No character, no color, no spark at all. I wonder,” he burst out before he could stop himself, “why you don’t just go back to Europe.”

Her dark eyes darkened further, and he braced for an indignant, angry retort. But she sat quiet, turning the words over slowly in her head, and a cold lump of fear settled in his throat that she would say she wondered that too.

At last she spoke, soft and even. “I believe it’s because of you.”

There was no vanity in the confession, no deliberate flattery to stroke his pride. It was as calm and matter-of-fact as if she had told him the tide came in at noon. Archer flushed all the way to the roots of his hair, dared not shift an inch or breathe too loud—her words felt like a rare, fragile butterfly that the smallest movement would send fluttering away, lost forever, if he startled it.

“At least,” she went on, “you’re the one who made me see that under all that dull, stuffy New York surface, there are things so fine, so sensitive, so delicate, that even the people I cared for most back in my old life abroad feel cheap next to them. I don’t know how to put it into words right,” she frowned, her brow furrowed with quiet distress, “but it feels like I never understood before how much that is hard, and shabby, and base we pay for even the most exquisite little joys in life.”

*Exquisite pleasures—just to have known them at all is something!* He ached to say it, but the quiet, aching appeal in her eyes kept the words locked behind his teeth.

“I want to be perfectly honest with you,” she said, “and perfectly honest with myself. I’ve hoped for this chance for so long, to tell you how much you’ve helped me, what you’ve made me see in myself that I never knew was there—”

Archer stared at her, brows drawn tight, and laughed a rough, bitter laugh that cut her off. “And what do you think you’ve made of me?”

She paled, just a little. “Of you?”

“Yes. Because I’m far more your making than you could ever be mine. I’m the man who married one woman because another one told him he should.”

A faint, fleeting flush rose to her cheeks. “I thought—you promised—you wouldn’t say things like that today.”

“Ah, you are just like every other woman! None of you will ever see a bad, messy business all the way through!”

She dropped her voice soft, gentle. “Is it a bad business, though—for May?”

He stepped to the window, drumming his fingers against the raised wooden sash, and every nerve in his body hummed with the tender wistfulness she had put into her cousin’s name.

“That’s the thing we have to always think of, isn’t it?” she pressed on, the words coming slow and pained, as if she was dragging them up from somewhere deep. “By your own showing, anyway. If it’s not worth giving things up, missing things, just to keep other people from disillusionment and misery—then everything I came home for, everything that made my old life abroad feel so bare and empty because no one there cared for those soft, fine little things—all of that is just a sham. Just a dream.”

He turned, not moving an inch from where he stood by the window. “And in that case, there’s no reason on earth you shouldn’t go back, is there?”

Her eyes clung to his, desperate, begging. “Oh—isn’t there any reason at all?”

“Not if you staked everything on my marriage working out,” he said, savage and raw. “My marriage isn’t going to be some pretty, perfect sight that’s worth you staying here for.”

She said nothing, and he pushed on. “What’s the point? You showed me my first glimpse of what a real, honest life could be, and at the very same second you told me I had to go on living the sham one. It’s more than any human can bear. That’s all there is to it.”

“Oh, don’t say that—when I’m bearing it too!” she burst out, tears brimming over her lids. Her arms lay loose along the edge of the table, and she sat there holding her face up to his gaze, reckless, as if she was standing at the edge of a precipice and no longer cared what she let him see. That face laid her whole soul bare, no masks, no pretense. Archer stood frozen, speechless, overwhelmed by the truth that had just dawned on him.

For answer, she let the tears spill over and roll slow down her cheeks.

Half the width of the small room still lay between them, neither of them making the smallest move to cross it. Archer felt a strange, quiet indifference to touching her right then—he would hardly have noticed the shape of her body at all, if one of her hands, splayed out on the tabletop, hadn’t drawn his gaze, the same way it had once back in that little Twenty-third Street house, when he had stared at it so he wouldn’t let himself look at her face. Now his imagination spun around that hand like water circling the edge of a vortex, but he still made no move to step closer. He had known the sort of love that fed on kisses and caresses, but this passion he felt now, this passion that was stitched right into his bones, was far too deep to be satisfied by something as superficial as a touch. His only terror was doing anything at all that might wipe the memory of her words from the air between them. His only thought was that he would never have to feel so alone again.

But after a moment, the heavy weight of waste and ruin settled over him. They were here, together, shut away from the whole world, safe for the first time in years—and yet chained so tight to the separate destinies they had chosen, they might as well have been half a world apart.

“What’s the use, if you’re just going to go back eventually?” he broke out, the words hiding a raw, desperate cry beneath them: *How am I ever supposed to keep you here?*

She sat perfectly still, lids lowered over her wet eyes. “Oh, I’m not going anywhere yet.”

“Not yet? So some day, then? Some day you’ve already counted out in your head?”

At that, she lifted her eyes, clear and bright as the sun on the sea. “I promise you. Not as long as you hold out. Not as long as we can look straight at each other, like this.”

He dropped back into his chair. The unspoken part of her answer was crystal clear, as if she had spoken it out loud: *If you so much as lift a finger to cross this line, you’ll drive me straight back to all the horrors of my old life in Europe, all the temptations you can only half guess at*. He understood it perfectly, and the thought kept him anchored to his side of the table, held him in a kind of soft, sacred, moved submission.

“What a life you’re going to have,” he groaned.

“Oh,” she smiled, soft and wobbly from the tears, “as long as it’s a part of yours.”

“And mine a part of yours?”

She nodded.

“And that’s all we’ll ever get? For either of us?”

“Well,” she said, gentle, “it’s all there is, isn’t it?”

At that, he sprang to his feet, forgetting everything but the soft sweet shape of her face. She stood up too, not to meet him or to run away, just quiet, as if the hardest, scariest part of their talk was done, and now she only had to wait. She was so still that when he drew close, her outstretched hands didn’t push him away, they guided him. Her hands fell into his, her arms loose, keeping him just far enough away that her surrendered, unmasked face could say all the rest that didn’t need words.

They might have stood that way for minutes, or hours—neither of them could tell. But it was long enough for her silence to communicate every last thing she had to say, for him to understand that the only thing that mattered was not to let this be the last time they ever saw each other like this. He would leave their future in her hands, he decided, only asking that she hold tight to it, for both of them.

“Don’t—don’t be unhappy,” she said, her voice cracking as she pulled her hands gently away.

“You won’t go back? You won’t leave and go back to Europe?” he asked, as if that was the one possibility he could not survive.

“I won’t go back,” she promised. She turned, pulled the door open, and led the way out into the public dining room.

The loud, chattering schoolteachers were gathering up their parcels, getting ready to straggle down the path to the wharf. Across the stretch of sunlit beach, the white steamboat waited tied to the pier, and far across the glinting blue water, the rooftops of Boston loomed, soft and hazy on the horizon.

Back on the boat, surrounded by chattering strangers, Newland Archer settled into a quiet tranquility so unexpected it left him half breathless, as if some soft, warm weight had wrapped around his shoulders to hold him steady.

By every unspoken rule of the world he’d always lived in, the day they’d just spent together in Boston was a ridiculous, wasted failure. He had not so much as pressed his lips to Ellen Olenska’s hand, had not wrung a single whispered promise of future meetings from her. For a man sick with unsatisfied longing, about to part from the woman he loved for an unknown stretch of time, he was almost embarrassingly, humiliatingly calm, almost comforted. It was the perfect, unforced balance she’d struck that did it: she had not betrayed their mutual loyalty to the people who loved them back home, but she had not lied to either of them about the pull thrumming between them. The balance was no calculated performance, he’d seen that in her stammering half-sentences, in the tears she’d swiped away before he could comment. That tender awe swelled in him now that the risk of crossing a line they’d both agreed not to step over was past, and he thanked the fates no silly urge to play the part of a dramatic lover had ever tempted him to push her further than she was willing to go. Even after they’d clasped hands for a final goodbye at Fall River Station, and he’d turned to walk away alone, he carried the quiet, unshakable certainty that he had gained far more from their meeting than he’d ever sacrificed.

He wandered back to his club, settled alone in the deserted library, replaying every single second of their hours together over and over in his head. It grew clearer with every turn of his thoughts: if Ellen finally chose to return to Europe, to go back to her husband, it would not be because her old glittery, empty life tempted her, not even with the new terms the Count was offering. No. She would only go if she feared she was becoming a temptation to Archer, if she worried they would both slip and fall away from the quiet, honorable standard they had set for themselves together. She would choose to stay near him as long as he never asked her to come any closer. It rested entirely with him, to keep her there, safe, close, but never pushed over the edge.

Those thoughts stayed with him all through the train ride home, wrapping him in a soft golden haze that blurred every face around him until his fellow passengers looked like distant, half-seen phantoms. He half believed if he tried to speak to them, they would not understand a single word he said. He woke the next morning to the swelter of a stifling New York September, the heat-wilted faces of the passengers streaming past him still blurred behind that same golden glow — until one face pulled itself free of the crowd, sharp and familiar, forcing its way to the front of his consciousness.

It was the young man he’d spotted the day before, walking out of Boston’s Parker House, the one who did not look like any ordinary American hotel guest. The same odd, faint tug of half-recognition pricked at him now. The young man stood squinting around the station, the dazed, lost air of a foreigner abandoned to the rough unkindness of American transit, then stepped toward Archer, lifted his hat, and spoke in heavily accented English: “Surely, Monsieur, we met in London?”

Archer grasped his hand at once. “Ah, of course! In London! You did make it over, after all?” he exclaimed, staring at the sharp, haggard little face of young Carfry’s French tutor, M. Rivière.

“Oh, I made it, yes,” Rivière smiled, his lips tight. “But not for long. I return the day after tomorrow.” He stood there, gripping his light valise in a neatly gloved hand, his gaze anxious, perplexed, almost pleading as he looked Archer over. “I wonder, Monsieur, since I’ve had the luck to run into you, if I might —”

“I was just about to suggest it,” Archer cut in. “Come to luncheon, won’t you? Downtown — stop by my law office first and I’ll take you to a very decent restaurant nearby.”

Rivière looked genuinely surprised, touched. “You are too kind. I was only going to ask how to find a carriage. There are no porters here, no one seems willing to listen —”

“I know. Our American stations must shock you. You ask for a porter and they hand you chewing gum. Come along, I’ll extricate you, and you really must let me buy you lunch.”

Rivière hesitated for a barely perceptible beat, then thanked him profusely, saying he already had a lunch engagement that he could not break. But by the time they reached the sunlit, comparatively quiet street, he asked if he might call at Archer’s office that afternoon instead. Archer, with nothing but slow midsummer leisure on his hands at the firm, scribbled his address down and set an hour. Rivière thanked him over and over, tipped his hat, and climbed aboard a passing horse-car, leaving Archer to walk the rest of the way to his office.

He arrived sharp on the dot, freshly shaved, his clothes pressed neat, but his face still drawn and serious. Archer was alone in the office, and before he could even offer his guest a seat, Rivière spoke abruptly: “I believe I saw you, sir, yesterday in Boston.”

The line was insignificant, and Archer was about to nod in easy assent, until the strange, urgent intensity in his visitor’s gaze caught him up short, checking the words on his tongue.

“It is extraordinary, very extraordinary,” Rivière continued, “that we should meet under the circumstances I find myself in now.”

“What circumstances?” Archer asked, half wondering if the young man had run out of money.

Rivière studied him for a moment, tentative. “I did not come to New York looking for work, as I mentioned I might when we met in London. I am here on a special mission —”

Archer’s eyes widened. A flash of connection snapped between the two meetings in his mind, the whole scene lighting up like a struck match. He paused, taking in the new information, and Rivière fell silent, apparently aware the half-sentence was enough.

“A special mission,” Archer repeated slowly.

The young Frenchman held his palms open, lifted them an inch from his sides. The two men stared at each other across the desk until Archer roused himself to say, “Do sit down.” Rivière bowed, settled into a chair on the far side of the desk, and waited again.

“It was about this mission that you wanted to consult me?” Archer asked at last.

Rivière nodded. “Not on my own behalf. On that score, I have already made up my mind. I should like — if I may — to speak to you about the Countess Olenska.”

Archer had known the words were coming for minutes, but when they landed, they sent blood rushing to his temples as if he had walked face-first into a bent-back hidden branch in a thicket. “And on whose behalf,” he said, keeping his voice as even as he could, “do you wish to do this?”

Rivière met his gaze unflinchingly. “I might say hers, if it did not sound like an unacceptable liberty. Shall I say instead: on behalf of abstract justice?”

Archer studied him, half ironic. “In other words, you are Count Olenski’s messenger?” He saw his own dark flush reflected in the Frenchman’s sallow face. “You are not here to deliver his message to *you*, Monsieur. If I came to you, it is on entirely other grounds.”

“What right do you, as his emissary, have to be on any other ground?” Archer retorted. “An emissary is an emissary.”

Rivière considered that for a moment. “My mission to the Countess Olenska is over. It has failed.”

“I can’t help that,” Archer rejoined, his tone still sharp with irony.

“No, but you can help —” Rivière paused, twisting his hat in his carefully gloved hands, staring at the lining before lifting his eyes back to Archer’s face. “You can help, Monsieur, I am convinced, to make it equally a failure with her family.”

Archer pushed his chair back and shot to his feet. “Well, by God I will!” he exclaimed. He stood there, hands stuffed in his pockets, glowering down at the little Frenchman, who had also risen, his head still a few inches lower than Archer’s line of sight. Rivière paled even further, to the very edge of what his sallow complexion could manage.

“Why the devil,” Archer exploded, “did you think — if you’re appealing to me because of my connection to Madame Olenska — that I would take a view contrary to the rest of her family?”

Rivière’s face softened into absolute distress, the usual sharp cleverness draining away until he looked entirely defenseless. “Oh, Monsieur —”

“I can’t imagine,” Archer continued, “why you would come to me, when there are others far closer to the Countess to speak to. Still less why you thought I’d be more receptive to the arguments I assume you were sent over to deliver.”

Rivière took the onslaught with a disarming humility. “The arguments I want to present to you, Monsieur, are my own, not the ones I was sent over with.”

“Then I see even less reason to listen to them.”

Rivière stared back at his hat for a long beat, as if considering if the words were enough of a hint to leave. Then he spoke with sudden, sharp decision. “Monsieur — will you tell me one thing? Is it my right to be here that you question? Or do you perhaps believe the whole matter to be already closed?”

His quiet insistence made Archer feel clumsy, loud, foolish for his bluster. He reddened slightly, dropped back into his chair, and gestured for Rivière to sit again. “I beg your pardon. But why isn’t the matter closed?”

Rivière gazed back at him, his face tight with anguish. “You do, then, agree with the rest of the family that, faced with the new proposals I brought, it is hardly possible for Madame Olenska to refuse to go back to her husband?”

“Good God!” Archer exclaimed. Rivière let out a low, quiet murmur of confirmation.

“Before I saw her, at Count Olenski’s request, I met with Mr. Lovell Mingott, spoke with him for hours before I traveled to Boston. I understand he represents his mother’s view, that Mrs. Manson Mingott’s influence guides the whole family.”

Archer went still, feeling as if he was clinging to the edge of a sliding precipice. The realization that he had been entirely excluded from these negotiations, that no one had even bothered to tell him they were happening, barely registered next to the sharper shock of what he was learning. He saw in a flash that the family had stopped consulting him because their deep, unspoken tribal instinct warned them he was no longer on their side. He remembered May’s offhand comment, on the drive home from the archery party at Mrs. Manson Mingott’s: *Perhaps, after all, Ellen would be happier with her husband*. That throwaway line had been nothing more than a test, to see which way the wind blew. He’d exclaimed in outrage at the time, and his wife had never mentioned Ellen’s name to him since. The result of that little test had been reported back to the family, and Archer had been quietly, tacitly locked out of their counsels. He admired the unbreakable family loyalty that made May fall in line, and knew she would never have done it if her conscience protested — but she almost certainly shared the family’s view that Ellen would be better off as an unhappy titled wife than a shunned, separated free woman, and there was no point in discussing the matter with Newland, who had a stubborn habit of suddenly refusing to take the most basic, assumed social rules for granted.

Archer lifted his head, met his visitor’s anxious gaze. “Don’t you know — is it possible you don’t know — that the family is starting to doubt if they even have the right to advise the Countess to refuse her husband’s latest proposals?”

“The proposals you brought?”

“The proposals I brought.”

Archer almost snapped that none of this was any of his business, but the humble, stubborn tenacity in Rivière’s gaze made him hold his tongue, and he asked another question instead: “What is your object in telling me all this?”

Rivière did not hesitate for a second. “To beg you, Monsieur — to beg you with all the strength I have — not to let her go back. Oh, don’t let her!”

Archer stared at him, increasingly stunned. There was no mistaking the sincerity of his distress, the iron of his determination. He had clearly decided he would throw away every last thing he had rather than stay silent on the matter. “May I ask,” Archer said slowly, “if that is what you told the Countess Olenska, when you met with her?”

Rivière flushed, but his gaze did not waver. “No, Monsieur. I accepted my mission in good faith. I truly believed — for private reasons I do not need to trouble you with — that it would be better for Madame Olenska to get back her social position, her fortune, the standing her husband’s name gives her. I would never have taken the job otherwise.”

“Well then —?”

Their eyes locked for a long, long moment.

“Ah, Monsieur, after I saw her, after I sat and listened to her speak, I knew she was far better off here.”

“You knew?”

“Monsieur, I carried out my mission faithfully. I laid out the Count’s arguments, stated all his offers, did not add a single comment of my own. The Countess listened patiently, she saw me twice, considered everything I came to say with perfect impartiality. And it was over the course of those two talks that I changed my mind, that I started to see things differently.”

“What made you change?”

“Simply seeing the change in *her*,” Rivière said.

“The change in her? You knew her before, then?”

Rivière’s color rose again. “I used to see her in her husband’s house in Europe. I have known Count Olenski for many years. You can imagine he would not send a total stranger on a mission this sensitive.”

Archer’s gaze wandered away from him, to the hanging calendar on the wall, its top printed with the rugged features of the President of the United States. That this conversation was happening anywhere under the jurisdiction of that man’s government felt as absurd and impossible as any fantasy he could have dreamed up. “The change — what sort of change?”

“Ah, Monsieur, if I could put it into words!” Rivière paused. “*Tenez* — it was the realization of something I had never considered before: that she is an American. And if you are an American of her kind — of your kind — the sorts of compromises other societies accept, shrug off as part of the give and take of polite life, become unthinkable. Simply unthinkable. If her relations here understood exactly what the Count’s life with her was like, their opposition to her returning would be as absolute as her own. But they seem to think the Count’s wish to have her back is proof of some great, desperate longing for domestic happiness.” Rivière paused, then added softly. “It is not nearly that simple.”

Archer stared back at the calendar, then down at the scattered papers on his desk. For a second or two he could not trust himself to speak. He heard Rivière push his chair back, realized the young man had stood up. When he looked up again, he saw that his visitor was as moved as he was.

“Thank you,” Archer said simply.

“There is nothing to thank me for, Monsieur. It is I who —” Rivière broke off, his voice thick, like speech had become difficult for him too. “I should like to add one thing, though. You asked if I am in Count Olenski’s employ. I am, at this moment. I returned to his household a few months ago, for reasons of private necessity that could happen to any man with sick, elderly dependents to support. But from the moment I walked in here today to say these things to you, I consider myself no longer in his service. I will tell him as much when I return, and give him my reasons. That is all, Monsieur.”

Rivière bowed, stepped back, and Archer stood to meet his outstretched hand. “Thank you,” Archer said again, as their palms clasped.

Every year on the fifteenth of October, Fifth Avenue’s brownstone shutters creaked open for the first time since the summer exodus to Newport, wool carpets stiff with camphor unfurled across oak floors, and triple layers of heavy silk window curtains were hung to block the autumn drafts seeping off the Hudson. By the first of November, that annual household ritual was complete, and New York society drifted back into town to lay their visiting cards on hall tables and take stock of one another again. By the fifteenth of the month, the season was in full blast: opera posters were plastered to gas lampposts, dinner invitations stacked high on mantels, and dance dates scribbled in the leather backs of young ladies’ diaries.

And as regular as clockwork, Mrs. Archer always declared at this exact point that New York was very much changed. Perched on her high embroidered armchair as a happy non-participant in the bustle, she enlisted Mr. Sillerton Jackson and Miss Sophy Jackson to help her trace every new crack snaking across the surface of the old familiar social order, every strange, unruly weed pushing up between the neatly planted rows of “proper” social vegetables. As a boy, Newland Archer had loved waiting for this annual pronouncement, listening to her list the tiny, quiet signs of disintegration his own careless, young gaze had never noticed. To Mrs. Archer’s mind, New York never changed except for the worse, and Miss Sophy agreed with her whole heart. Mr. Sillerton Jackson, worldly old gentleman that he was, suspended his judgment and listened to their lamentations with amused impartiality – but even he never denied the city was shifting under their feet.

Two years into his marriage to May, Newland found himself forced to admit the same.

All this came up over Mrs. Archer’s Thanksgiving dinner, the table heaped with bronze chrysanthemums and slices of spiced pumpkin pie. It was her custom, on the day the whole city was officially enjoined to count its blessings, to take a mournful, un-bitter stock of the world around her, and wonder what exactly there was to be grateful for. Certainly not the state of fashionable society, which the new progressive rector of St. Matthew’s, Dr. Ashmore, had thundered against that very morning in a fiery Thanksgiving sermon pulled straight from the book of Jeremiah. He spoke constantly of the “trend” of modern high society, and Mrs. Archer found it both terrifying and thrilling to feel she was part of something so tangible and measurable, like a crack running down the wall of your own house.

“There is no doubt of it, that trend is very marked,” she said, tapping her knitting needles together.

Miss Sophy tittered that it was odd to preach of such things on Thanksgiving, and Mrs. Archer dryly replied that Dr. Ashmore simply meant they ought to give thanks for all the old, good things that were left.

Archer had always smiled at his mother’s annual vaticinations, but this year, as they ticked through the list of new oddities, he could not deny the trend was impossible to miss.

“The extravagance of the dresses!” Miss Sophy exclaimed. “Sillerton took me to the opera opening night, and Jane Merry’s gown was the only one I recognized from last year – and even that had a new front panel sewn into it. I know for a fact she bought it from Worth only two years ago, my seamstress always goes to remade her Paris gowns before she wears them, after all.”

“Ah, Jane Merry is one of us,” Mrs. Archer sighed. No one her age dreamed of wearing a new Paris gown straight off the Customs boat, the way every woman in town was doing now, instead of locking them away in camphor chests for a full year to “mellow.” Miss Sophy recalled that in her youth, wearing the absolute latest fashion was considered vulgar, and in Boston, proper ladies stored their Worth gowns for two full years before wearing them. She told the story of old Mrs. Baxter Pennilow, who ordered twelve new Paris dresses a year as a standing habit, and when she died after two years of illness, her family found forty-eight gowns still wrapped in tissue paper, unworn, that the girls wore to symphony concerts for years without ever looking out of date.

Mrs. Archer conceded Boston was more conservative, but keeping a new French gown for at least a season was a safe, sensible rule. “Beaufort started this reckless new nonsense, making Regina put every new garment on her back the second they dock,” she said, and Miss Sophy trailed off, not wanting to say in front of Janey Archer that poor Regina now risked looking just as gaudy and common as the social climbers she once outshone. Sillerton Jackson supplied the quip smoothly: “Like her rivals,” and the ladies murmured in agreement.

The tone shifted dark, fast, when Mrs. Archer brought up Beaufort’s rumored bad speculations. Everyone had heard the whispers, and no one wanted to confirm them out loud. The whole table fell into a gloomy quiet. No one had ever liked Julius Beaufort, and no one minded gossiping about his messy private affairs – but the thought that he might drag his wife’s respected Dallas family into financial dishonor was too shocking even for his enemies to enjoy. Old New York turned a blind eye to hypocrisy in private lives, but business demanded total, spotless honesty. It had been decades since a well-known local banker had disgraced himself through fraud, but no one had forgotten how his entire family had been erased from society overnight. Not all the power of the combined Dallas clan would save poor Regina if the rumors were true.

The talk drifted back to safer topics, but every new thing they named only confirmed Mrs. Archer’s sense that the trend was speeding up faster every day. “I know, Newland, that you let dear May go to Mrs. Struthers’s Sunday evenings –” she began, and May cut in brightly, laughing, “Oh, everyone goes there now! She was even invited to Granny Mingott’s last reception.”

Archer smiled to himself, thinking that this was exactly how New York did its quiet social revolutions. They conspired to ignore the change until it was well and truly settled, then pretended it had always been that way. There was always a traitor inside the citadel, handing over the keys – once everyone had tasted Mrs. Struthers’s easy hospitality, no one was going to sit at home and pretend to be scandalized that her champagne was once advertised as shoe polish.

“I know, dear, I know,” Mrs. Archer sighed. “Amusement is all anyone cares about these days, I suppose. But I have never quite forgiven your cousin Madame Olenska for being the very first person to countenance Mrs. Struthers.”

A hot, vivid blush flared across May’s face so suddenly it surprised Archer as much as every other guest at the table. “Oh, Ellen –” she murmured, in that exact same soft, disapproving, apologetic tone her parents would have used to say “Oh, the Blenkers,” that bohemian crowd of writers and artists the family refused to acknowledge. Archer stared at her, a strange cold tingle running down his spine – for a second she felt like a stranger, perfectly molded to every unspoken rule of the world around her, a part of him he had never seen before. His mother insisted that someone like Ellen, who had grown up in old European aristocratic circles, ought to be the one helping them guard their social lines, not blurring them. May’s blush did not fade, it stayed bright and unmistakeable, heavy with some secret meaning beyond simple disapproval of Ellen’s social missteps. “I do not think Ellen cares for society at all,” she said, groping for something neutral. “No one really knows what she does care for.”

Everyone in the family knew Ellen was no longer in their good graces. Even old Catherine Manson Mingott, her most loyal champion, had been unable to defend her refusal to return to her husband Count Olenski. The Mingotts would never air their disapproval in public – their sense of family solidarity was too strong. They had simply, as Mrs. Welland put it, “let poor Ellen find her own level” – and to their shock and horror, that level was down in the dim, messy world of the Blenkers, where “people who wrote” held untidy parties and no one cared a fig for old social rules. Everyone agreed it was a fatal mistake on Ellen’s part to refuse to go back to her husband; after all, a young woman’s place was under her husband’s roof, especially when she had left it under circumstances no one cared to dig too deeply into.

“Madame Olenska is a great favorite with all the gentlemen,” Miss Sophy said, sweet and sharp, dropping her little dart gently. Mrs. Archer mournfully agreed that that was exactly the danger a woman like Ellen was always exposed to, and the ladies gathered up their silk trains to drift towards the drawing room, where the soft carcel gas globes glowed above the velvet sofas, leaving Archer and Sillerton Jackson alone in the Gothic library.

Settled in front of the crackling grate, smoking a perfect cigar to make up for the mediocre dinner, Sillerton Jackson grew portentous, and loose-lipped. “If the Beaufort crash comes,” he said, “there are going to be disclosures. Ugly ones. He has not spent all his money on Regina.”

Archer dismissed it at first, saying he thought Beaufort would pull himself out of the hole, but Sillerton insisted Beaufort had gone to beg favours from the city’s most powerful men that very day – and even if they tided him over this time, poor Regina might end up stuck in some shabby European spa for bankrupts for the rest of her life.

Archer said nothing, his mind wandering. What had that blush of May’s meant, when they mentioned Ellen? Four whole months had passed since that midsummer day he had spent with Madame Olenska, and he had not seen her once. He knew she had returned to Washington, the tiny quiet house she shared with Medora Manson. He had written her once, a short note asking when they might meet again, and she had replied even more briefly: Not yet. No word had passed between them since. He had built a little secret sanctuary in his own mind, where Ellen reigned as the centre of his real life – all the books he read, all the thoughts he could not say out loud, all the feelings that nourished him, were kept there. The rest of his days, his actual life, felt thin and unreal, like he was wandering through a house that was not his, bumping into familiar furniture of tradition and prejudice like an absent-minded man who no longer recognized his own home.

Sillerton cleared his throat, dropping his next, far heavier bombshell. The family had cut Ellen’s allowance drastically the second she confirmed she would not return to Olenski. She had also forfeited the large marriage settlement that came with her wedding, money Olenski had been fully ready to sign over to her the second she came back to him. Worse, every last penny Medora Manson owned was tied up in Beaufort’s speculations. If Beaufort crashed, Ellen and Medora would be completely penniless. The only person who could give her enough money to live on was old Catherine Mingott – and everyone knew how fiercely she hated to part with her gold. The rest of the family had no reason at all to keep Ellen in New York, away from her husband.

Archer jumped to his feet, his fist slamming down hard on the edge of the walnut writing table, making the brass double inkwell rattle in its sockets. He was burning with helpless, furious rage, and he knew he was on the verge of doing something stupid – but he remembered Sillerton was his mother’s guest, and old New York never allowed hospitality to be broken by a fight. He suggested curtly that they go rejoin the ladies.

On the drive home in the dark carriage, May remained oddly, stubbornly silent, that heavy blush still clinging to her face like a threat he could not name. When they got home, she turned to walk down the hall to her bedroom instead of following him into the library, and he called her back, grumbling that the lamp was smoking. She came back, bright, pleasant, using the cool, firm tone her mother had taught her to humour fussy, difficult relatives. He stared at her young, beautiful face in the lamplight, and felt a cold little horror curl in his chest – he had decades and decades of this quiet, empty life stretching out in front of him.

“I have to go to Washington next week,” he said suddenly. “For a few days. There is a patent case coming up before the Supreme Court.” He rattled off the name of the inventor, all the tiny little plausible details, smoothly, as if he had learned the glib trick from Lawrence Lefferts himself. May nodded, listening attentively, and when he finished she said simply that the change would do him good. “And you must be sure to go and see Ellen while you are there,” she added, looking him straight in the eye with a perfectly cloudless, bright smile, as if she was reminding him not to neglect some tedious family duty.

Archer understood the message instantly, written in the silent unspoken code every well-bred New Yorker of their generation had been raised on. She knew everything. She knew the family was pressuring Ellen to go back to her husband, she knew he was the one who had encouraged her to stay, she knew he was not going to Washington for the patent case at all. She would not make a scene, would not yell, would not accuse him. She was giving him her full, explicit approval to go, and urging him to tell Ellen exactly what her choice to stay in New York, away from her husband, would cost her – the gossip, the poverty, the total social ruin. She turned the wick of the smoking lamp down, lifted off the glass globe, and blew out the flame gently. “They smell less if you blow them out,” she explained, with her bright, practical housekeeping air, pausing on the threshold to wait for his goodnight kiss.

The very next day, Wall Street hummed with soft, reassuring rumours that Julius Beaufort’s precarious finances were not nearly as dire as the gossip of the weekend had made them out to be. There were no hard facts, no signed statements, but the air thrummed with hope: everyone knew the bold, brash banker had powerful friends in high places, and word spread fast that he’d called on them, and they’d come through. That evening, when Mrs. Regina Beaufort stepped into her usual opera box with her old, bright social smile and a glinting new emerald necklace strung around her throat, the whole of New York’s tight-knit old guard exhaled in unison.

Their unspoken rule was ironclad: anyone who broke the sacred code of financial probity had to be cast out, no exceptions. But no one wanted to be forced to turn the Beauforts away. Their gilded, endlessly festive ballroom was the glittering heart of the season, and losing them would leave a hollow no other household could fill. Plenty of people who didn’t care a fig for moral rectitude were already mourning the loss of their best entertainer before the man had even formally collapsed.

Newland Archer had already made up his mind. He was going to Washington. He’d timed the trip to line up with the patent lawsuit he’d vaguely mentioned to May, the perfect excuse to slip away and see Countess Ellen Olenska, to finally say all the words he’d been swallowing down for months. But on Tuesday, his senior partner Mr. Letterblair told him the case was going to be postponed for weeks. Still, he walked home that afternoon dead set on leaving the very next evening. May knew next to nothing about his work, had never shown the faintest curiosity about his cases, and would never notice a delayed court date, or remember the names of two random strangers litigating over a invention. He couldn’t put off seeing Ellen any longer. There was too much at stake, too much he had to tell her.

When he walked into his office Wednesday morning, Letterblair’s face was ashen. Beaufort hadn’t pulled it off after all. He’d only spread those reassuring rumours to lull his depositors into calm, drawing thousands in new payments to his bank right up until the night before, when the ugly truth finally leaked out. Now there was a full-blown run on the bank, and its doors would shatter and close before the day was out. The tricks he’d pulled were cruel, cowardly, and set to be one of the most shameful failures in Wall Street’s short, rough history.

“I’ve seen scandals in my time,” Letterblair stammered, white as a sheet, “but nothing this bad. Everyone we know is going to get burned. And what on earth do we do about his wife? I pity old Catherine Manson Mingott more than anyone — she always believed in him, made him her favourite, and at her age, who knows what this shock will do to her? And she’s related to all of you, through the Dallas line. Her only chance would be to leave him, but who on earth is going to tell her that? Her place is at his side, and thank goodness she never seemed to notice how rotten his private affairs were.”

A clerk knocked, and handed Archer a note sealed in May’s familiar neat, rounded handwriting.
*“Dearest Newland, please come uptown as soon as you possibly can. Granny had a slight stroke last night. She somehow found out about the bank disaster before anyone else could warn her. Uncle Lovell is away shooting, and Papa was so overcome by the thought of the disgrace he spiked a fever and can’t leave his bed. Mamma needs you dreadfully — please go straight to Granny’s house the second you can get away.”*

Archer passed the note to Letterblair, and ten minutes later was lurching north in a packed horse car, switching at 14th Street for one of the wobbly, high-roofed Fifth Avenue omnibuses that jolted over cobblestones like a storm-tossed ship. It was past noon by the time it dropped him outside old Catherine’s brownstone.

Mrs. Welland was hovering by the ground-floor sitting room window, her face drawn, and waved him in with a haggard, relieved gesture. May was waiting for him in the hall, pale but smiling. The space had that strange, tight hush all normally immaculate houses get the second illness invades: furs and winter wraps were heaped in piles on the hall chairs, the doctor’s bag and overcoat sat on the side table, and half a dozen unopened calling cards were stacked beside them, forgotten. Dr. Bencomb had just left his second visit, May told him, and the outlook was far more hopeful than it had been at dawn — Granny’s ferocious will to live was already pulling her back from the edge.

Behind closed doors and heavy yellow damask curtains, Mrs. Welland whispered the full, horrifying story in shocked undertones. The night before, just after Catherine had finished her usual post-dinner game of solitaire, the doorbell had rung. A lady so swathed in veil the servants didn’t recognise her at first had demanded to see her. The butler realised who it was when she spoke, opened the sitting room door and announced “Mrs. Julius Beaufort”, then left the two of them alone for an hour. By the time Catherine’s bell rang, Regina had slipped out unseen, and the old woman was sitting huge, still and white in her armchair, gesturing to be helped to her bedroom. She’d seemed fully in control then, but at three that morning the servants had found her propped up on her pillows, one small plump hand hanging limp, a lopsided smile on her face.

The stroke was mild, the doctor said. She could talk, make her wishes clear, and was already regaining movement in her face. When they pieced together her slurred, fragmented sentences, they realised what Regina had done: she’d come begging for old Catherine to bail her husband out, to use all her influence to make the entire family cover up his crime, to not “desert” them, to pretend none of the hundreds of ruined depositors existed.
“‘Honour’s honour, and honesty’s honesty, in Manson Mingott’s house, and it’ll stay that way till they carry me out feet first,’” the old woman had stammered to her daughter, thick-tongued from partial paralysis. “And when she said ‘But Auntie, my name’s Regina Dallas’ — I told her it was Beaufort when he hung those jewels round her neck, and it’s Beaufort now he’s covered her in shame.”

Mrs. Welland wept softly, distraught at having to face such unvarnished disgrace. Archer sat by the window, staring blankly out at the empty street. They’d called him more for moral support than anything else: telegrams were out to Lovell Mingott, footmen were running notes out to all the family members in the city, and all anyone could do was sit in the dim, curtained room and murmur about the ruin of the Beauforts and Regina’s unforgivable audacity.

The older ladies traded stories of old scandals, agreeing that any proper wife of a disgraced man would vanish off the face of the earth with him, never dare show her face or beg her family to cover for her. They clucked about Regina’s emerald necklace, rumoured to have been sent over on approval from the jewellers the very afternoon she wore it to the opera, and wondered if they’d ever get paid for it.

Archer listened half-distractedly. His belief in unbreakable financial honesty for old New York families was as deeply ingrained as anyone’s, but his mind kept drifting to the half-packed suitcase waiting for him at home, to the train to Washington he was meant to board that very next evening.

Then Mrs. Lovell Mingott stepped back into the room, frowning.
“She’s asking for Ellen. I already wrote to her and Medora, but no — she says to send a telegram, right now, tell Ellen to come home alone.”

No one spoke for a long beat. Mrs. Welland sighed in resigned acceptance, and May stood up to gather the scattered newspapers off the floor.
“Of course we have to do what Granny wants,” she said, turning back to them with that bright, unshakable smile, her voice clear as two tapped silver bells as she spoke Ellen Olenska’s name. “Newland’s here, he can drop the telegram at the office right away. You were telling me you had to run out for work errands anyway, dearest, right? It’ll be just in time for tomorrow’s morning train.” She sat down at Granny’s tiny rosewood writing desk, scribbled the telegram out in her large, neat, childlike hand, blotted it carefully, and handed it to him.
“What a silly little shame,” she said softly, turning back to her mother and aunt, “that you’ll just miss each other on the road. Newland has to leave for Washington tonight for that Supreme Court patent lawsuit, after all. We can’t ask him to put the whole firm’s important work on hold, not when Granny’s already doing so much better, and Uncle Lovell will be home tomorrow.”

Mrs. Welland agreed at once, of course — Granny would never be the one to ask him to neglect his duties. Archer heard her murmur, as he walked out the door, “But why on earth would she make us telegraph for Ellen?” and May’s clear, steady voice answering, “Perhaps she wants to remind her, once and for all, that her duty is to go back to her husband the Count.”

The front door clicked shut behind Archer. He stepped out into the crisp afternoon air, the telegram crinkling slightly in his hand, and turned toward the telegraph office. Somewhere south of the city, the tracks to Washington stretched out before him. Somewhere north, Ellen was already packing her bags to come home.

“Ol—ol—how d’you spell that ridiculous name, anyway?” the sharp, bright young telegraph clerk prodded, as Archer pushed May’s hastily scrawled telegram across the dented brass ledge of the Western Union counter.
“Olenska. O-l-e-n-s-k-a,” he repeated, plucking the slip back to print out the foreign syllables clear above his wife’s messy cursive, his chest tight at the shape of the word: the name he’d been chasing across half a dozen gilded drawing rooms, half a dozen quiet unspoken moments, for months now.
“Funny name for a New York telegraph office, wouldn’t you say? Leastways not this part of town,” an amused, familiar voice drawled at his elbow. Archer turned to find Lawrence Lefferts looming over him, stroking his impeccable waxed moustache, pretending he hadn’t been craning to peek at the name on the telegram slip. “Hey Newland, was just hoping I’d run into you here. Heard old Mrs. Mingott had a stroke, was heading over to her place when I saw you turn down this street and followed. Figured you’d come from there?”
Archer nodded, pushing the telegram under the lattice of the counter.
“Terrible bad, huh?” Lefferts went on, leaning in a little too close. “Wiring to the family, right? Guess it must be serious if you’re sending for Countess Olenska, of all people.”
Archer’s jaw locked so hard his teeth ached. He had a wild, feral urge to slam his fist right into that smug, vain, handsome face, to shut Lefferts’s gossiping mouth for good where Ellen’s name was concerned.
“Why do you say that?” he bit out.
Lefferts, the most famous coward in New York high society when it came to ungentle disagreements, raised his eyebrows in a pointed, ironic glance towards the telegraph clerk still hovering behind the counter. The look said plainly: Nothing is worse form than throwing a fit in public, Archer. You’ll look like a fool.
Archer couldn’t have cared less about form at that moment – but the thought of trading barbs about Ellen Olenska with Lawrence Lefferts, of letting that man drag her good name through public mud even for a second, made the whole fight feel unthinkable. He paid for his telegram without another word, and the two men stepped out onto the sun-dappled, gritty New York sidewalk together. By then he’d clamped his temper back down tight. “Mrs. Mingott’s already much better. Doctor says there’s no cause for concern at all.” Lefferts poured over him with flowery, relieved platitudes, then prattled on about the awful, ruinous rumours circling Beaufort.

By that afternoon, the news of Beaufort’s total financial collapse was splashed across every paper in the city. It overshadowed every line printed about Manson Mingott’s stroke, and only the tiny handful of people who knew Regina Beaufort had snuck over to beg the old matriarch for a secret loan the night before attributed her illness to anything other than old age and too many rich desserts.
All of New York was shrouded in the dark, giddy hum of the Beaufort scandal. As old Mr. Letterblair liked to grumble, no case half this bad had crossed his desk in his whole career, not even dating back to the first Letterblair who’d founded the firm. The bank had kept taking deposits for a full day after its total ruin was inevitable, and half its clients came from the oldest, most respected clans in the city – so Beaufort’s betrayal felt twice as sharp, twice as cynical. If Regina Beaufort hadn’t gone around telling everyone the collapse was “the true test of friendship,” public anger at her husband might have softened into pity for her. But between that and the news of her secret midnight visit to old Catherine, everyone agreed her cynicism was even worse than her husband’s – and no one could even make the old excuse that she was “a foreigner” to let her off easy. The only small comfort for people who hadn’t lost their own savings was reminding each other that Beaufort was a foreigner, but even that didn’t help much when a Dallas from South Carolina was running his mouth about how Beaufort would be right back on his feet soon. There was nothing for it but for society to agree to cut the Beauforts off entirely, shut their doors to them forever – except for all the poor hapless victims of the crash: Medora Manson, poor old Miss Lannings, and all those other well-born foolish ladies who everyone clucked their tongues at, saying if they’d only listened to Henry van der Luyden none of this would have happened.
“The best thing the Beauforts can possibly do,” Mrs. Archer pronounced over her tea that night, as if she were diagnosing a sick child and prescribing a tonic, “is move down to that little property of Regina’s in North Carolina. Beaufort’s always kept a racing stable, he’d do very well breeding trotting horses. He’s got every trait of a good successful horse dealer, after all.” Everyone nodded in agreement, but no one bothered to actually ask the couple what their real plans were.

The next day, old Mrs. Mingott was almost fully recovered. She got her voice back enough to order every last person in the house never to mention the Beauforts in front of her again, and when Dr. Bencomb showed up, she snapped at him to explain why the whole family was making such a ridiculous fuss over her health. “If people my age insist on eating chicken salad late at night, what do they expect?” she huffed. The doctor promptly adjusted her diet, and soon the whole “stroke” was rebranded as nothing more than a bad case of indigestion.
But even with her full health back, old Catherine wasn’t quite the same sharp, unfeeling matriarch she’d been before. Old age had blunted her very limited compassion for other people’s troubles, and she had no trouble at all erasing the whole Beaufort disaster from her mind. For the first time though, she was fixated on her own symptoms, and suddenly overflowing with soft sentiment for the members of her family she’d spent decades ignoring.
Mr. Welland, of all people, was now her new favourite. She’d brushed him off for years, laughing at his wife’s claims that he was a secret man of great force and intellect if he’d ever just chosen to exercise it. Now his reputation as a dedicated, meticulous valetudinarian made him endlessly fascinating to her, and she sent an imperial summons that he come over the second his temperature was normal so they could compare their restricted diets. No one, she announced, could ever be too careful about checking their temperature.

Twenty four hours after the telegram was sent to Ellen Olenska in Washington, a reply came back saying she’d arrive the next evening. The Newland Archers happened to be lunching at the Wellands’ that day, so the whole family immediately fell to debating who would meet her at the Jersey City train station. The Welland household operated like a ramshackle frontier outpost, perpetually short on resources, so every little obstacle felt enormous. They agreed Mrs. Welland couldn’t possibly make the trip, because she was accompanying her husband over to old Catherine’s that afternoon, and the family brougham couldn’t be spared – if Mr. Welland got “upset” seeing his mother-in-law for the first time after her attack, they might need to rush him home at a moment’s notice. The Welland sons were all downtown at work, Lovell Mingott would be rushing back from a shooting trip right that same evening and his carriage was reserved to pick him up, and no one could possibly ask May to go alone across the Hudson ferry to Jersey City, not even in her own carriage, on a cold dark winter evening.
But if no one met Ellen at the station, they’d be horribly inhospitable, going directly against old Catherine’s explicit orders to welcome her home. It was just like Ellen, Mrs. Welland’s tired sigh implied, to dump the whole family in this mess. “It’s one crisis right after another,” she grieved, in one of her rare little bouts of rebellion against fate. “The only reason I think Mamma might be even sicker than Dr. Bencomb is letting on, is this sudden mad urge to have Ellen come all the way here immediately, no matter how much trouble it causes everyone.”
The words were out before she could think, and Mr. Welland pounced on them immediately, turning pale and laying down his fork. “Augusta,” he said, “do you have any actual reason to think Dr. Bencomb is less reliable than he used to be? Have you noticed him slacking off on my treatments, or your mother’s?”
Mrs. Welland went white as a sheet as she realized what a mess she’d made, what endless cascades of anxious trouble her careless comment would cause. She laughed, took a second helping of scalloped oysters, and climbed right back into her usual cheerful armour. “Darling, what on earth made you think that? I only meant, after the very firm stand Mamma took that Ellen’s duty was to go back to her husband in Europe, it’s so strange she’s suddenly got this whim to see her now, when there are half a dozen other grandchildren she could have summoned instead. We can’t forget that wonderful as her vitality is, Mamma is a very old woman.”
Mr. Welland’s brow stayed furrowed, his anxious mind already latching on to that last line. “Yes, your mother is very old. For all we know, Bencomb might not be nearly as good at treating elderly patients. You’re right dear, it’s always one thing after another. In ten or fifteen years I suppose I’ll have the pleasant job of hunting down a new doctor. Much better to make the change before you’re absolutely forced to.” With that very Spartan, sensible decision made, he picked his fork back up.
“All the same,” Mrs. Welland said, pushing back from the lunch table and leading everyone into the dark back drawing room, all draped in purple satin and cluttered with malachite knickknacks, “I still don’t see how we’re going to get Ellen here tomorrow evening. I do like to have every single thing settled at least twenty four hours ahead of time.”

Archer tore his eyes away from a tiny, gaudy painting of two cardinals drinking wine, set in an octagonal ebony frame studded with onyx medallions. “I’ll go pick her up,” he offered. His heart was thudding so hard against his ribs he could feel it in his ears. “I can slip away from the office early enough to drive May’s brougham to the ferry, and meet her train.”
Mrs. Welland sighed in unbridled relief, and May, who’d drifted over to the window, turned back to smile at him, bright and full of wifely approval. “See, Mamma? Everything is all sorted out, more than twenty four hours ahead of time,” she said, leaning down to kiss her mother’s worried forehead.
May’s brougham was waiting out front to drive Archer to Union Square, where he could catch a Broadway streetcar down to his office. As she settled into her corner of the carriage, she spoke softly. “I didn’t want to upset Mamma with more new problems, but how on earth are you going to meet Ellen tomorrow, when you’re supposed to leave for Washington tomorrow? You’re off to argue that big patent case before the Supreme Court, remember?”
“Oh, I’m not going,” Archer said.
“Not going? What on earth happened?” Her voice was clear as a bell, brimming with perfectly calibrated wifely concern.
“The case got called off, postponed.”
“Postponed? How odd. I got a note from Mr. Letterblair to Mamma this morning, saying he was heading off to Washington today to argue that very patent case. You said it was the patent case you were going for, didn’t you?”
Archer felt heat creep up his face. She was pressing him so insistently, so unlike the soft, compliant May he knew, that he felt himself blushing for her, for breaking all of society’s unspoken rules of delicate politeness. “No, the case isn’t postponed. My trip is,” he said, cursing all the extra little details he’d spilled when he first told everyone he was going to Washington, remembering some old line he’d read somewhere that mediocre liars pile on details, but the very cleverest ones don’t bother. It hurt him far less to lie to May than it did to watch her pretend she hadn’t already caught him in the lie. “I’m going later, that’s all. Lucky for your family’s convenience, I guess,” he added, letting a little sharp sarcasm creep into his voice.
He could feel her staring at him, so he turned his head to meet her eyes, determined not to look like he was hiding something. Their gazes locked for a long, heavy second, and in that look they each read far more of what the other was hiding than either of them ever wanted to admit out loud.
“Yes,” May agreed, her tone bright and perfectly even, “it’s such a happy coincidence that you’re free to meet Ellen after all. You saw how grateful Mamma was that you offered.”
“Oh, I’m very happy to do it,” Archer said.
The carriage rolled to a stop, and as he jumped down to the sidewalk, May leaned over to rest her hand on his. “Goodbye, dearest,” she said. Her eyes were so bright, so vivid a blue, that he wondered later if they’d been glistening with unshed tears.
He hurried across Union Square, muttering the same line to himself under his breath, over and over, like a half-holy, half-wicked prayer: It’s two whole hours from the Jersey City station to old Catherine’s house. Two whole hours. Maybe even more.

His wife’s dark blue brougham—wedding varnish still glinting soft and unmarred even under the sleet streaking the ferry ramp—met Archer at the dock, wrapping him in plush, hushing warmth as it bore him toward the Pennsylvania terminus in Jersey City. It was a sombre, snow-heavy afternoon, and hissing gas lamps already pooled gold light across the vast, echoing station as he paced the platform waiting for the Washington express. He half-remembered the wild talk of visionaries he’d heard at the club: that one day a tunnel would burrow under the Hudson, that ships would cross the Atlantic in five days flat, that flying machines, electric lighting, and wireless telephones would turn all their old Arabian Nights fables into fact. He could not find it in himself to care if any of those marvels came to pass, so long as the tunnel stayed unbuilt for today.

Drunk on a giddy, schoolboy glee that felt nothing like his usual staid self, he pictured every beat of their coming meeting: Ellen stepping down from the train, the crowd of strangers blurring away until only her face stood out, her arm slipping through his as he guided her to the carriage, the slow, careful crawl down the slippery wharf past skittish slipping horses, overloaded carts, and teamsters shouting hoarse orders, then the sudden hush of the ferry, their brougham sitting still on the deck as snow settled soft on its roof and the river slid under them, carrying them as if the whole world were rolling off toward the other side of the sun. There were a hundred perfect, eloquent things he ached to say to her, all lining up neat and burning on the tip of his tongue.

The train rumbled closer, clanging and groaning like some great overburdened beast staggering back to its lair, and Archer shoved through the throng, craning into every high carriage window until suddenly Ellen’s pale, surprised face was right before him, and that old familiar mortification pricked at him: he had, once again, forgotten exactly what she looked like, how the light caught the faint little freckle under her eye, how her mouth curved just so when she was caught off guard. They reached for each other, their hands lacing, and he drew her arm tight through his. “This way— I have the carriage waiting,” he said.

Everything unfolded exactly as he’d dreamed. He helped her heft her bags into the brougham’s footwell, babbled reassurances about her grandmother’s improving health, walked her through the sad, sordid details of the Beaufort collapse, and his chest ached soft when she breathed that quiet, gentle “Poor Regina!” The carriage worked its way free of the tangled traffic around the station, crawling down the icy wharf ramp past swaying coal carts, spooked horses, dishevelled express wagons, and a plain empty hearse lurching past. Ellen’s eyes flew shut, and she clutched his hand so tight her nails bit into his skin. “If only that doesn’t mean—poor Granny!”

“Oh no, no, she’s much better, she’s perfectly well, really—look, we’ve passed it now,” he hurried to soothe her, as if putting distance between them and the dark omen could erase every shadow that loomed over them. She did not let go of his hand, and when the carriage lurched across the gangplank onto the ferry he leaned over, unbuttoned the tight brown glove covering her other hand, and pressed a kiss to the soft, bare skin of her palm as if he were venerating a sacred relic. She pulled her hand back with a faint, knowing smile, and he blurted, “You never expected me today, did you?”

“Oh, no.”
“I was halfway to boarding a train for Washington to come see you. I’d made all the arrangements—we very nearly crossed paths on the rails.”
She gasped, as if that narrow escape was something far more terrifying than any accident.
“Do you know,” he went on, “I almost forgot what you looked like? Not that I don’t think of you every hour— it’s that it’s always like this. Every time you walk back into my life, it happens all over again, like I’m meeting you for the very first time.”
“Oh, I know,” she murmured. “It’s the same for me.”
“Am I new, every time, for you?” he insisted.
She nodded, her gaze drifting out the window at the snow streaking the dusk beyond the glass.

“Ellen—Ellen—Ellen,” he whispered, and she did not answer. He sat quiet, watching her profile blur against the darkening sky, wondering how little they really knew of each other after those four long, empty months apart. The precious minutes were slipping away fast, and every eloquent speech he’d rehearsed had vanished, leaving him only to brood over the strange, sharp magic of their proximity: sitting so close their shoulders nearly touched, and yet still separated by a thousand uncrossable miles of other people’s lives, other people’s rules.

“What a pretty carriage! Is it May’s?” she asked, suddenly turning back to him.
“Yes.”
“Then it was May who sent you to fetch me? How kind of her.”

The words stung, sharp as a slap, and the secret he’d buried, never meaning to speak aloud, burst out of him unbidden: “Your husband’s secretary, M. Rivière, came to see me the day after we met in Boston.” He’d meant to tuck that visit away forever, but the reminder that they were tucked inside his wife’s brand new wedding carriage prodded a petty, defensive anger out of him, and he wanted to see if she hated that reference to her old life half as much as he hated hers to May.
