But Ellen did not look the slightest bit surprised, and he immediately realized: Rivière must have written to her about it.
“M. Rivière came to see you?” she asked, simple as anything.
“Yes. Aren’t you shocked?”
She hesitated a beat. “Why would I be? He told me in Boston he knew you, that he’d met you in England once, years ago.”
“Ellen— I have to ask you one thing. I wanted to after I saw him, but I couldn’t put it in a letter. It was Rivière who helped you get away, when you left your husband, wasn’t it?” His heart was beating so hard he could barely breathe, waiting for her reaction.
“Yes. I owe him a great debt,” she answered, no tremor in her quiet, steady voice.

Her tone was so natural, so almost unconcerned, that all his roiling, defensive anger fizzled right out of him. Once again, her sheer unvarnished simplicity made him feel silly and wrapped up in stuffy convention, even when he thought he’d flung every last rule to the wind. “You are the most honest woman I have ever met,” he exclaimed.
“Oh no— but I’m probably one of the least fussy,” she laughed, soft in her throat.
“Call it what you like. You look at things exactly as they are.”
“Ah, I’ve had to. I’ve had to look the Gorgon right in the face.”
“Well, it didn’t blind you! You saw she’s just an old bogey, like all the rest of the monsters everyone’s scared of.”
“She doesn’t blind you. She just dries all your tears up.”

The answer stopped the pleas on his lips cold, coming from a depth of pain and experience he could not even fathom. The ferry lurched hard, its bows slamming against the slip piles so hard the brougham staggered, flinging Archer and Ellen against each other. He was trembling so hard he could barely move, his arm wrapping tight around her shoulders to steady her. “If you’re not blind, then you have to see that we can’t keep doing this. Being together, and not together. It can’t last.”
“What can’t?”
“You never should have come today,” she said, her voice changed, sharp with a quiet grief. And then she turned, flung her arms around his neck, and pressed her mouth to his in a kiss that burned right through all his carefully laid restraint. Just then the carriage jolted forward, a gas lamp at the slip’s mouth flooding gold light through the window, and she drew back, both of them sitting motionless and silent as the brougham inched through the tangled line of carriages leaving the ferry.

As they pulled out onto the street, Archer began to speak fast, urgent, like he was trying to convince both of them. “Don’t shrink back into your corner like that, you don’t have to be afraid of me. A stolen kiss in a carriage isn’t what I want. Look— I’m not even trying to touch your sleeve. I understand you don’t want whatever this is to dwindle into some sordid, hole-and-corner love affair, sneaking around behind everyone’s backs. I couldn’t have spoken like this yesterday, when all I could think of was the fire of wanting to see you. But now you’re here, and you’re so much more than I remembered, and what I want from you is so much more than a few snatched hours between weeks of thirsty, empty waiting, that I can sit perfectly still right beside you, holding that vision in my head, just quietly trusting it will come true.”

She was quiet for a long moment, then whispered so softly he barely heard it: “What do you mean, trusting it to come true?”
“You know. Us, together, for good, don’t you?”
“Your vision of you and me, running away?” She let out a sudden, hard, bitter laugh. “You chose a fine place to ask me that, didn’t you?”
“Because we’re in my wife’s brougham? We can get out and walk, if you like. A little snow won’t hurt us.”
She laughed again, softer this time. “No, I shan’t get out and walk. I need to get to Granny’s as fast as I possibly can. And you’ll sit beside me, and we’ll stop chasing visions, and look at the realities right in front of us.”
“I don’t know what you mean by realities. The only real thing in the world to me is this, right now, you and me.”

She was silent for a long stretch, as the carriage rumbled down a dim side street before bursting out into the bright, searching glow of Fifth Avenue’s gas lamps. “Is it your idea, then, that I should live with you as your mistress— since I can’t be your wife?” she asked, plain and unflinching.

The crudeness of the question shocked him. The word was one no woman in his polite, sheltered circle ever dared say out loud, even when they danced closest to the topic. He noticed Ellen said it like it was just any other word, and his stomach twisted, wondering how often it had been thrown at her, casually, in that cruel gilded prison she’d fled from. He floundered, fumbling for words. “I want— I want to run away with you to a world where words like that, categories like that, don’t exist. Where we’re just two people who love each other, who are the whole of life to each other, and nothing else on earth matters.”

She drew a long, shaky sigh that ended in a soft laugh. “Oh my dear— where is that country? Have you ever even been there? I know so many people who tried to find it, once. Believe me, they all got off by mistake at wayside stations: Boulogne, Pisa, Monte Carlo. It wasn’t any different from the old world they left. Just smaller, dingier, full of strangers judging you twice as hard.”
“The Gorgon really has dried all your tears up,” he said, quiet and hurt.
“She opened my eyes too. People say she blinds you, but it’s not true. She glues your eyelids open, so you can never sink back into that soft, blessed innocent darkness ever again. Isn’t there a Chinese torture that does that? There ought to be. Ah, believe me, that perfect little country you’re chasing is a very miserable, small place when you actually get there.”

The carriage had crossed Forty-second Street now, May’s sturdy brougham horse eating up the pavement northward like a champion Kentucky trotter. Archer choked on the sense of wasted minutes, of all his pretty dreams dissolving into nothing. “Then what, exactly, is your plan for us?”
“For *us*? But there is no *us*, in that sense! The only way we can stay close to the parts of each other that are real, that are good, is if we stay far away from each other. If we don’t, we’re just Newland Archer, the husband of Ellen Olenska’s cousin, and Ellen Olenska, the cousin of Newland Archer’s wife, sneaking around behind the backs of the people who trust us, making fools of ourselves.”
“Ah, I’m past all that,” he groaned.
“No you’re not. You’ve never been past it. But I have,” she said, in a strange, hollow voice, “and I know exactly what it looks like there.”

He sat dazed, heavy with a wordless, gnawing pain, fumbling in the dark of the carriage for the little call bell that signalled the coachman to stop. He remembered May always rang it twice to pull over. He pressed the bell, and the carriage drew up sharp to the curb.
“Why are we stopping? This isn’t Granny’s,” Ellen exclaimed, confused.
“No. I’m getting out here,” he stammered, yanking the door open and jumping down onto the cold pavement. He saw her startled face in the glow of the street lamp, her half-moved hand reaching out to detain him. He closed the door, leaned in the window for a second, voice dropping low so the coachman wouldn’t overhear. “You’re right. I never should have come today.” She leaned forward, about to speak, but he’d already called out the order to drive on, and the carriage rumbled away, leaving him standing on the empty corner.

The snow had stopped, and a sharp, stinging wind had sprung up, lashing his face raw. He felt something stiff and cold on his lashes, and realized he was crying, the wind freezing his tears right to his skin. He thrust his hands deep in his coat pockets, and set off at a fast, sharp pace down Fifth Avenue, back toward his empty house.

The gas lamps in their brownstone drawing room glowed soft and amber over empty velvet armchairs the evening Archer drifted down before dinner, the whole house wrapped in the quiet hush that had settled over their household since Mrs. Manson Mingott fell ill. Every family social engagement had been put on hold for weeks, so he and May were dining alone. He knew May was somewhere overhead, he’d heard the faint rustle of her skirts through the floorboards as he dressed, and since she was always the punctual one, her absence struck him as odd. Lately he’d taken to clinging to these tiny, trivial little puzzles of domestic life as a lifeline, something to tie his whirling, half-dreaming thoughts firmly back to the real world. He understood his father-in-law now, the strange, endless absorption Mr. Welland had for sorting stamps or polishing ivory cane handles; long ago, he suspected, even the mild Mr. Welland had had his own escapes, his own visions of roads not taken, and had built a whole army of small, boring, familiar domestic rituals to defend himself from them.

When May finally stepped into the room, he thought she looked almost wan. She was wearing the tight-laced, low-necked dinner dress even the most informal Mingott gatherings required, her fair hair coiled high in its usual neat stacked braids, but her face looked faded, drained of its usual glow. Still, she turned that soft, tender smile on him, her bright blue eyes still holding that same luminous warmth he’d fallen in love with years before.
“Where did you vanish to, dear?” she asked. “I was waiting for you at Granny’s, and Ellen turned up alone, said she’d dropped you on the walk home because you had to rush off for urgent business. Nothing’s wrong, is there?”
“Only a handful of letters I’d forgotten, needed to send them out before the post went at dinner time,” he said, fumbling the easy lie.
“Ah,” she said, quiet, a beat later. “I wish you could have stopped by Granny’s, that is, if the letters weren’t too pressing.”
“They were,” he said, surprised by her quiet insistence. “Besides, I had no idea you were going over there this afternoon.”

She turned to the gilded mantel mirror, lifting her long arm to tuck a stray lock of hair back into its intricate coiling, and Archer was struck by how languid, how almost lifeless her movement felt, as if the slow, unchanging monotony of their days had seeped all the bounce right out of her. Then he remembered: that morning, as he’d left the house, she’d called down the stairs that she would meet him at her grandmother’s so they could drive home together. He’d called back a cheery yes, and then gotten so wrapped up in thoughts of Ellen he’d forgotten the promise entirely. Guilt pricked at him, but irritation nipped right at its heels. They’d been married nearly two years now, and he was tired of living in this tepid, endless half-honeymoon—no fire, no passion, but all the quiet unspoken expectations of a newlywed pair, no room for any deviation from the script. If May would just say out loud whatever small grievances she was tucking away, he could laugh them off, but she’d been raised to tuck every imagined slight, every quiet hurt, behind that Spartan, perfect social smile.

He asked after Mrs. Mingott instead, to distract himself, and May said the old woman was steadily recovering, but she’d been very upset by the latest news about the Beauforts.
“What news?” Archer asked.
“They’re not leaving New York at all. I hear he’s taken some kind of insurance job, and they’re hunting for a tiny little house to rent somewhere on the edge of the city.”

There was no point even commenting on how absurd the whole situation was, and they drifted into the dining room where the silver candelabra glowed over the white linen. Their conversation stayed within all their usual narrow, safe boundaries, but Archer noticed May never once mentioned Madame Olenska, never alluded to Ellen being at Granny’s that afternoon, and he was half grateful, half uneasy, like there was a silent secret hanging between the two of them that neither would ever dare name.

They retired to the library for coffee after dinner, Archer lit a cigar and pulled a volume of Michelet down from the shelf. He’d gotten into history lately, ever since he noticed May would always ask him to read aloud if she caught him with a book of poetry. It wasn’t that he disliked the sound of his own voice, but her soft, earnest, surface-level little comments would strip all the warmth and romance right out of any verse he tried to read. Back in their engagement days, he realized, she’d just echoed every opinion he gave her; now that he’d stopped feeding her lines to parrot, she was making up her own safe, unchallenging thoughts, and they killed every bit of joy he might have gotten out of a book.

Seeing he’d chosen history, she dragged her workbasket over, pulled an armchair right up to the green-shaded student lamp, and pulled out the half-finished embroidered cushion she was stitching for his sofa. She was no great needlewoman—her big, capable hands were made for riding horses, rowing boats, spending hours out in the fresh air—but every other wife he knew stitched cushions for their husbands, and she didn’t want to leave out even that tiny little gesture of devotion. She sat bent over the work frame, her ruffled elbow sleeves slipping down to show her firm, round arms, her sapphire engagement ring glinting over the thick wide gold of her wedding band as she slowly, carefully stabbed her needle through the canvas. The lamplight fell full on her clear, unlined brow, and a cold quiet dread settled in Archer’s chest: he would never be surprised by her, not in all the decades ahead. No sudden new idea, no unplanned wild emotion, no messy unkindness or unexpected vulnerability. She’d spent every last scrap of her romance and poetry on their short courtship, and now she was just slowly, steadily ripening into an exact copy of her mother, and by some quiet invisible magic, she was trying to turn him into a copy of mild, contented Mr. Welland too.

He stood up so fast he nearly knocked over his cigar ashtray. “What’s wrong?” May asked, lifting her head at once.
“The room is stifling. I need a little air.”

He’d insisted the library curtains run on a sliding rod, not nailed permanently to a gilt cornice and looped up under layers of lace the way they were in the drawing room, so he wrenched them both back and pushed the sash all the way up, leaning out into the sharp, icy November night. He could see the glow of a hundred other gas lamps from the windows of the brownstones stretching out in every direction, the dark slant of roofs and chimneys, and for a second he could almost feel all the other lives unfolding outside their four walls, other cities, other worlds far beyond the tight little bubble of New York high society he’d been trapped in. His head cleared instantly, and he could finally breathe.

After a few minutes he heard May’s soft voice behind him. “Newland! Do shut that window. You’ll catch your death.”

He pulled the sash down and turned back, and the words almost slipped out of his mouth: I already have caught my death. I’ve been dead for months. A wild, terrible thought flashed across his mind, sharp and bright: what if it was her who was dead? Young, healthy women her age died all the time, didn’t they? If she died tomorrow, he would be free, no chains, no unspoken promises, no endless soft obligations. The thought was so overwhelming, so fiercely fascinating, that he didn’t even register how monstrous it was at first. It was just a new possibility his starved, sick soul could cling to.
May glanced up, and her eyes went wide when she saw the wild, distant look on his face. “Newland! Are you ill?”

He shook his head and stumbled back to his armchair. As he passed her, he laid a light hand on her soft hair. “Poor May,” he said.
“Poor? Why poor?” she laughed, the sound tight and strained.
“Because I’ll never be able to open a window without you worrying yourself sick over me,” he said, laughing back.

She was quiet for a long moment, head bowed over her stitching. “I won’t ever worry,” she said very low, “as long as you’re happy.”
“Ah, my dear,” he sighed, “and I’ll never be happy unless I can open the windows when I want to.”
“In this weather?” she chided softly, and he buried his head back in his history book with a sigh.

Six or seven days drifted by. Archer heard not a single word from Ellen Olenska, and he noticed no one in the family ever spoke her name in front of him, as if she’d been deliberately erased from all their conversations. He didn’t try to seek her out; it would have been impossible, with her camped out at Mrs. Mingott’s guarded sick bedside, every servant and relative keeping watch. Somewhere deep under all his drifting, half-formed thoughts, that quiet resolve he’d made leaning out the library window stayed firm, and it made waiting easy, no need to rush.

Then May came to him one afternoon, bright and pleased, to say Mrs. Manson Mingott had asked to see him. The request was no surprise, the old woman was recovering steadily, and she’d always openly said he was her favorite out of all her grandsons-in-law. May gave him the news with obvious pride, happy that the old matriarch thought so highly of her husband.
“Shall I come with you this afternoon?” Archer offered, automatically.
May’s face lit up, but she shook her head at once. “Oh no, you’d much better go alone. Granny gets bored seeing the same people back to back, it tires her out.”

His heart was hammering so hard he could hear it in his ears when he rang the bell at the Mingott house. This was exactly what he’d wanted: an afternoon alone at the house, no one else there, a chance to steal a minute alone with Ellen. All he wanted to ask her was the date of her return to Washington, and once he had that answer he would know exactly what to do next. But when he stepped into the yellow damask parlor, it was only the mulatto maid waiting for him, her white teeth gleaming, who pushed open the sliding doors and ushered him straight into Mrs. Mingott’s bedchamber.

The old woman was propped up in a vast, throne-like armchair next to her bed, no books or workbasket anywhere in reach—conversation had always been her only hobby, and she’d never bothered feigning interest in needlework. There was barely a trace left of the slight distortion her stroke had left, only a paler face, darker shadows tucked into the folds of her multiple chins. She held out one of her tiny soft hands, nestled in the wide curve of her lap like a pampered little pet, and ordered the maid not to let anyone else in. If any of her daughters called, they were to tell her she was asleep.
“Am I perfectly hideous, at my age?” she asked gaily, patting at the muslin kerchief crossed over her voluminous purple dressing gown.
“My dear, you’re handsomer than you’ve ever been,” Archer laughed back.
She threw her head back and cackled. “Ah, but not as handsome as Ellen, eh?” she winked at him, mischievous. “Was she so very pretty the day you drove her up from the ferry that she had to kick you out of the carriage halfway home? Back in my day, young men didn’t abandon pretty women unless they were forced to!” She chuckled, then added, half querulous, “It’s a pity you didn’t marry her, you know. I always told her so. It would have spared me all this endless family drama. But when did children ever bother to spare their grandmothers worry?”

Archer wondered if her illness had blurred her wits, but then she leaned forward, bright and sharp as ever. “Well, it’s all settled now, no matter what the rest of the family says. Ellen’s staying here with me. She hadn’t been in the house five minutes before I would have crawled on my knees to keep her, if I could just still see where the floor was under all these chins.” She told him how the whole family had talked her over before Ellen arrived, convinced her to cut off her granddaughter’s allowance, pressure her to go back to her cruel husband Count Olenski, even when Olenski’s lawyer came over with very generous settlement offers. “Marriage is marriage, money is money, both are useful things,” she said, “but the second I laid eyes on my sweet bird again, I said no. I’m not shutting her back in that cage. She’s staying here to nurse her old granny as long as I’m around, and I’ve already told Letterblair to give her every cent of her proper allowance, no strings attached.”

Archer’s veins were thrumming so hard he could barely think, confused if the news was joy or a sharp new ache. But slowly, a warm, giddy relief spread through him. Ellen had chosen to stay, not to run back to Washington, not to run back to Olenski. She must have realized it was impossible to give him up. The old woman said the family was going to fight them, claim that a sick weak old woman had been manipulated by Ellen, and she couldn’t fight all of them one by one. So Archer would have to do it for her.
“Me?” he stammered.
“Of course you,” she said, her round eyes suddenly sharp as penknives, her tiny pale nails clutching at his wrist like bird claws. “You’re Letterblair’s new partner. You’re the only one in this whole family who never ranted on about how it was Ellen’s duty to go back to her husband. I knew I could count on you.”

He reassured her he would handle it, and the old lady sighed, settling back into her cushions, satisfied. Then Archer asked when he would finally get to see Ellen.
Mrs. Mingott cackled, her eyes crinkling shut. “Not today. One visitor at a time, thank you. Ellen’s out. She took my carriage to go call on Regina Beaufort.” She paused to let the news sink in. “Walked straight up to me the day after she got here, put on her best bonnet, and told me cool as you please she was going to see the wife of that scoundrel Beaufort. I told her as much, and she looked right back at me and said ‘Well, I’m married to a scoundrel too, and all the family wants me to go back to him.’ Floored me, that line. And I let her have the carriage, even when I looked out the window and saw it wasn’t raining a drop. I’ve always admired courage more than anything else, and both those women have plenty of it.”

Archer bent down and pressed a kiss to the tiny soft hand still resting on his.
“Eh—eh—eh!” the old lady cackled, mocking, “Whose hand do you think you’re kissing there, young man? I hope it’s not my granddaughter’s behind my back!” He was nearly out the door when she called after him, grinning, “Give Ellen her granny’s love, but don’t you dare tell her a single word about our little conversation.”

Archer reeled like he’d taken a physical blow the second old Catherine’s news landed. Of course Ellen Olenska would have rushed back from Washington the moment her grandmother summoned her—but choosing to move back into Mrs. Mingott’s home, especially now that the old matriarch had all but recovered her health? That made no sense, not at first glance.

Archer didn’t doubt for a second her choice had nothing to do with money. He knew exactly how small the allowance Count Olenski had grudgingly granted her at their separation was; without the top-up from her grandmother, it barely covered the cost of a modest life by Mingott family standards, and now that Medora Manson, her long-time companion, had lost every last penny of her own fortune, that pittance would scarcely feed and clothe the two women. Ellen was reckless with generosity, wildly impulsive with money the way people who’d once lived in the lap of impossible luxury often were, but she had never cared a fig for the trivial comforts her relations considered non-negotiable. Lovell Mingott and Mrs. Welland had spent years bemoaning that a woman who’d known the cosmopolitan splendors of Olenski’s European estates could be so cavalier about “proper” New York standards. Besides, Archer knew perfectly well her husband had cut off that tiny allowance months prior, and she hadn’t lifted a finger to curry favor with her grandmother in all that time. There was only one possible reason she’d changed her mind.

He didn’t have to dig far to find it. On the ferry ride back from New Jersey, she’d told him they had to stay apart, had said it with her face pressed tight to his breast, no coquetry, no games—fighting their fate as hard as he was, clinging to her fragile resolve not to betray all the people who trusted them. But in the ten days since she’d slipped back into New York, she must have noticed his silence, the fact that he’d made no move to track her down. She must have guessed he was gearing up to cross a line there was no coming back from, and the fear of her own weakness, of the moment she’d finally cave and run away with him, had pushed her straight back to the one place she thought could keep them both safe.

An hour earlier, when he’d rung Mrs. Mingott’s doorbell, he’d thought his path was laid out clear as day. He’d meant to corner Ellen alone, and if he couldn’t, badger her grandmother for the exact date and train number of her return to Washington. He’d planned to meet her on that train, ride with her as far as she’d let him go—his own secret daydream was Japan, a world away from every stuffy New York rule. He’d leave a note for May that burned every last escape route, make his choice irrevocable. He’d thought he was braced for that plunge, even giddy to take it. But the second he heard Ellen was staying in New York, his first stupid, unbidden reaction was relief.

By the time he tramped home down Fifth Avenue, that relief curdled into a slow, creeping loathing for the life that now stretched out in front of him. It wasn’t unknown, that path. Every man of his class knew the code: the little lies, the carefully arranged alibis, the elaborate game of pretense they called “protecting a woman’s honor,” taught to them in novels and over after-dinner cigars by their elders. But when he’d played at that game before, he’d been a free, unmarried man, amused and detached. Now the role turned his stomach. It was exactly the life he’d always secretly mocked Thorley Rushworth for living: a never-ending string of small, smiling lies, by day and by night, in every caress and every quarrel, every word and every silence.

Society might have given women a pass for that kind of performance, wrote their truthfulness off as secondary to the needs of their husbands, let them hide behind moods and “nerves” if they got caught. But New York never laughed at a deceived wife. Men who chased other women after they married were written off as weak, pathetic, the kind of man no one invited to dinner twice. Archer had always thought men like Lawrence Lefferts, the city’s most notorious philanderer, were despicable. But Ellen was nothing like any other woman, he was nothing like any other man—their situation was unique, wasn’t it? They answered to no one’s judgment but their own?

His feet hesitated on the corner of his own street, and he kept walking, further down the frost-edged avenue. Ahead of him loomed the dark, shuttered bulk of the Beaufort house, once the most dazzling spot on Fifth, its steps carpeted and awned every night, carriages lined two deep waiting to drop off guests. He’d stolen his first kiss from May in that dead, dark conservatory at the side of the house, had seen her step into the glow of the ballroom chandeliers on their engagement night, tall and silver as a young Diana. Now the whole place was dark as a grave, save for a flicker of gas in the basement and one lit upstairs window, the blind left pulled crookedly up. Parked out front was Mrs. Manson Mingott’s carriage.

Archer’s chest tightened. He’d been moved to his core by old Catherine’s story of Ellen choosing to stand by the disgraced Mrs. Beaufort when every other proper New York lady had cut her dead. But he knew exactly what the gossips at the Union Club would say the second word got out Ellen was calling on the Beauforts. He paused, staring up at that one lit window, and the front door creaked open.

Ellen stepped out, her sealskin coat pulled tight, a faint glow of hallway light behind her. She turned to murmur a goodbye to whoever was holding the lamp for her, the door clicked shut, and she came down the steps.

“Ellen,” he whispered.

She jumped, and just then two young men in smart overcoats and silk mufflers rounded the corner: Lawrence Lefferts and young Reggie Chivers, clearly on their way to the group trip to see *Romeo and Juliet* at the opera house everyone was gossiping about. They spotted the two of them on the curb, and discreetly veered across the avenue, the exact unspoken gentlemanly solidarity Archer had always gone along with to spare other men embarrassment. Now it made him sick to his stomach, this quiet conspiracy to hide secrets that would ruin lives. He reached for Ellen’s hand, and the warm weight of her gloved fingers in his chased away every mean, petty thought of the gossip they’d just avoided.

“I’ll see you now—we can be together,” he blurted, hardly knowing what the words meant.

“Ah,” she said softly. “Granny told you I’m staying?”

“Tomorrow I have to see you,” he said, his voice sharp with a hunger he couldn’t hide. “Somewhere completely alone.”

She wavered, stepping toward her waiting carriage. “But I’ll be at Granny’s, for the time being, anyway.”

“Somewhere no one we know will find us.”

She gave a faint, wobbly laugh that grated on his raw nerves. “In New York? There are no hidden churches, no quiet monuments where no one knows us.”

“The Art Museum, up in the Park,” he explained quickly. “Half past two. I’ll be waiting by the front door.”

She didn’t answer, just slipped up into the carriage. As it pulled away, he saw her lean forward, and thought he caught the flutter of her hand waving in the dark. He stared after the carriage, his head spinning with tangled, contradictory feelings, half elated, half horrified at the cheap, secretive turn their romance had already taken. But he knew, deep in his bones, that she would come.

---

They’d dodged the crowded main galleries full of kitschy historical canvases no one could stop staring at, and wandered all the way back to the dusty, almost entirely abandoned wing holding the Cesnola collection: broken, weathered fragments pulled from the ruins of ancient Troy, no visitors to disturb them. They sank onto a divan curled around the central steam radiator, staring at the ebonized wood display cases full of shards of clay, discolored bronze, and half-melted glass.

“It’s so strange,” Ellen said, her voice soft in the quiet. “I’ve lived in New York for months and never came here before.”

“Someday it’ll be a grand, famous museum,” Archer said.

“Maybe,” she said absently, wandering across the room to stare at one of the cases. Archer stayed seated, watching her, the girlish lightness of her step under her heavy furs, the heron wing tucked into the brim of her fur cap, the two dark curls coiled like flattened vines just above her ears. Every little detail of her made his chest ache. He stood up and joined her at the case, full of tiny unidentifiable objects: little pots, hair pins, trinkets no living person could name the use for anymore.

“It feels so cruel,” she said. “After a while nothing matters. Not any of the things that were once so precious and necessary to people who are long forgotten. Now we just squint at them through magnifying glasses and mark their labels ‘Use unknown.’”

“Nothing matters to those old people, maybe,” he said, reaching to brush a stray strand of hair back from her face. “But everything that matters to you, matters. Right now. Meanwhile.”

She turned and walked back to the divan, and he sat down beside her, his heart hammering. Far down the corridor, he heard the faint echo of the guard’s boots, and he felt every second of their stolen time slipping through his fingers.

“You knew what I was going to do, didn’t you?” he said quietly. “You knew I was planning to show up in Washington, and run off with you. That’s why you stayed here. You were afraid.”

She stared down at the little fur muff in her lap, her hands shifting restlessly inside it. “Yes. I was afraid.”

“This is better, isn’t it?” she sighed, after a long, quiet moment. “We hurt fewer people this way. Isn’t this what you’ve always wanted, in the quiet part of your head?”

“To have you here, close enough to touch and too far to ever reach? To sneak around like we’re doing something shameful? It’s the exact opposite of what I want,” he said, his voice sharp with frustration. “I told you exactly what I want the other day.”

“Then you think this is worse?”

“A thousand times worse,” he said. “I could lie to you, but it’s detestable. I hate every second of it.”

“Oh, I do too!” she burst out, and the relief flooded out of her on a deep, shaking breath.

“Then what on earth do you think is better?” he said, jumping to his feet. The guard rounded the corner then, ambling past them like a ghost through a necropolis of mummies and sarcophagi, and they both stared pointedly at the nearest display case until he vanished back down the corridor.

“I promised Granny I’d stay with her,” Ellen said, not meeting his eyes. “Because I thought I’d be safer here.”

“Safer from me?”

“Safer from hurting people irreparably,” she whispered, a tear spilling over her lashes and catching in the mesh of her veil. “Don’t let us end up like everyone else, lying and hurting the people who’ve been good to us.”

“What people want us to do, that isn’t us,” Archer said, reaching for her hands through her muff.

“Shall I come to you, just once?” she said, her voice small and clear, like she was daring to say something she’d been too afraid to admit even to herself. “And then go home.”

The blood rushed so fast to his head he thought he might faint. “Home? To your husband?”

“What else is there?” she said, lifting her wet, troubled eyes to his. “I can’t stay here and lie to everyone who’s helped me build a new life.”

Archer stood frozen, staring down at her, every argument dying on his lips. He knew if he said yes, if he convinced her to spend that one night with him, he could easily talk her out of ever going back to Olenski, easily twist that moment of softness to get everything he wanted. But that raw, unflinching honesty in her face made the lie impossible. He couldn’t trap her that way. If he let her come to him, he had to let her go again. And that was a fate worse than any other.

“Ellen,” he said, his voice breaking. “Wait. Don’t go.”

“I have to go,” she said, yanking her little watch out from under her coat, standing up fast. He caught her wrist, and for a second they stared at each other, breathless, almost like enemies. “I’ll see you. Two days from now.”

“Dearest,” he whispered, and all the fight went out of him. Their eyes held, and the pale, sad glow of her face flooded with a soft, shining light, a love so bright it made his chest ache with awe. He had never seen love made visible before, not like that.

“Oh, I’m late, goodbye,” she gasped, pulling her wrist away and hurrying down the long gallery before he could say another word. At the door, she turned for half a second to wave, and then she was gone.

Archer walked home in a daze. Darkness had fallen by the time he let himself into his own house, staring at the familiar coat hooks and umbrella stands in the hall like he was looking at them from the other side of a grave. The maid told him May had gone out in the carriage after luncheon and still wasn’t back, and he sagged into his library armchair, staring at the red glow of the dying fire, no thoughts in his head, just a heavy, quiet amazement that felt like he was suspended in the grip of fate. This was what had to be, he kept repeating to himself. This was what had to be.

The door opened, and May walked in.

“I’m dreadfully late—you weren’t worried, were you?” she said, pressing her hand lightly to his shoulder in one of her rare soft caresses. She was paler than usual, bright with a strange, unaccustomed energy, her cheeks flushed deep pink. “I went to see Granny, and Ellen walked in just as I was leaving. We sat and talked for what felt like hours. I haven’t had a real, proper talk with her in forever.”

Archer leaned back against the mantelpiece, out of the light of the student lamp, watching her. She was trying so hard, he realized, trying to push down the quiet, unspoken resentment she’d always held for Ellen, trying to be fair, trying to be the wife he wanted her to be. He almost told her everything, right then, almost threw himself on her mercy.

“The family has been so hard on her, I know,” May rambled on, breathless from the long speech. “She’s different, from all of us. She’s seen so much more of the world. I don’t want to judge her unfairly.”

Archer laughed a sharp, impatient little laugh, and the open space for honesty between them clicked shut again.

“Time to dress for dinner,” he said, moving toward the stairs.

May stepped forward fast, impulsively, wrapping her arms around his neck and pressing her warm cheek to his. “You haven’t kissed me today,” she whispered, and he felt her tremble in his arms.

“At the court of the Tuileries,” said Mr. Sillerton Jackson, his eyes crinkling with that soft, reminiscent smirk of a man who had hoarded decades of society’s juiciest secrets, “such things were pretty openly tolerated.”

The heavy, beeswax-polished dark walnut dining room of the van der Luydens’ Madison Avenue townhouse hummed with the hush of unassailable old New York decorum, and it was barely twenty-four hours since Newland Archer had wandered the quiet galleries of the Metropolitan Museum, staring at faded medieval tapestries that had tangled his thoughts tight around Ellen Olenska. Mr. and Mrs. van der Luyden had only just drifted back to the city from their rural Skuytercliff estate, where they’d fled precipitately the second news of Beaufort’s catastrophic financial failure had broken. Society had begged them to return: when the entire fabric of their narrow, ordered world was fraying, their quiet, unassailable authority was the only thing that could stop vulgar upstarts like Mrs. Lemuel Struthers from scrambling up the social ladder to claim Regina Beaufort’s vacant place. As Mrs. Archer had put it, they “owed it to society” to show their faces at the Opera, to open their doors, to stand in the breach as they always had. So they had unpacked their linen, unmuffled the long-shuttered rooms, and sent out invitations for two small dinners and one evening reception.

This particular evening, their guests were Sillerton Jackson, Mrs. Archer, Newland, and his wife May, all bound for a winter premiere of *Faust*. No dinner under the van der Luydens’ roof ever deviated from ritual: even with only four guests, the first course was served sharp at seven, every subsequent course unfolding at a deliberate, unrushed pace so the gentlemen would have time to linger over their cigars before the opera’s first act. Archer had barely seen May since the previous night: he’d left at dawn for his office, buried under a pile of trivial, tedious paperwork, then been detained by a senior partner long past his usual departure time, arriving home so late that May had already driven ahead to the van der Luydens’ and sent the carriage back empty. Now, across the bank of soft pink Skuytercliff carnations and the glint of their massive, heirloom silver plate, she looked pale, almost languid, but her eyes were bright, and she chattered with a bright, exaggerated animation that felt forced to his watchful eye.

The subject that had prompted Jackson’s Tuileries allusion had been brought up, Archer suspected, very much on purpose by their hostess. The Beaufort failure, and Regina Beaufort’s unapologetic refusal to shrink into obscurity after it, was every drawing-room moralist’s favorite topic that winter, and once the whole sorry affair had been thoroughly picked over and condemned, Mrs. van der Luyden turned her soft, disapproving gaze on May. “Is it possible, dear, that what I hear is true? I was told your grandmother Mingott’s carriage was seen idling outside Mrs. Beaufort’s door this week.” She did not use Regina’s first name, as if the disgraced woman no longer merited the courtesy.

May’s cheeks flushed bright pink, and Mrs. Archer cut in hastily. “If it was there, I am certain it was without poor Catherine’s knowledge.”

“Ah, you think—?” Mrs. van der Luyden paused, sighed, and glanced over at her husband.

“I am afraid,” Mr. van der Luyden said gently, “that Madame Olenska’s kind heart may have led her into the imprudence of calling on Mrs. Beaufort.”

“Or her taste for peculiar, unorthodox people,” Mrs. Archer added dryly, her eyes drifting, all innocent, to rest on her son’s face.

“I am so sorry to think it of Madame Olenska,” Mrs. van der Luyden murmured, and Mrs. Archer sighed right back. “Ah, my dear—after you invited her to stay at Skuytercliff not once, but twice!”

That was the exact moment Sillerton Jackson leaned in, pleased to have the floor. “At the Tuileries,” he repeated, as every eye in the room turned to him, “the standard was excessively lax in some respects; if you’d asked where Morny’s money came from, or who paid the debts of half the court beauties, no one would have batted an eye.”

“I hope, dear Sillerton,” Mrs. Archer said, a little sharp, “you are not suggesting we should adopt such loose standards here?”

“I never suggest anything,” Jackson returned, imperturbable. “But Madame Olenska’s foreign upbringing may well make her less particular about small social lines than the rest of us.” The two elder ladies sighed in unison.

“Still, to have kept her grandmother’s carriage waiting on the doorstep of a known defaulter!” Mr. van der Luyden protested, and Archer guessed he was remembering, and resenting, the hampers of rare carnations he’d himself sent to Ellen’s little Twenty-Third Street house not long before.

“Of course I have always said she looks at the whole world quite differently than we do,” Mrs. Archer summed up.

A hot flush rose to May’s forehead. She looked straight across the table at her husband, and spoke before anyone else could. “I am sure Ellen only meant to be kind.”

“Imprudent people are often kind,” Mrs. Archer replied, as if kindness was no excuse at all, and Mrs. van der Luyden murmured, “If only she had consulted someone before she acted—”

“Ah, that she never did!” Mrs. Archer rejoined.

A small signal passed between the van der Luydens: their hostess nodded slightly at Mrs. Archer, and the three ladies, their silk skirts glimmering in the gaslight, swept out of the dining room to retire to the drawing room. The gentlemen stayed behind to light their special short cigars, reserved for opera nights—they were so fine that even Archer found himself mourning the strict punctuality that would force them to stub them out in less than an hour.

Once they reached the opera house and the first act of *Faust* wrapped, Archer slipped away from their party, weaving to the back of the gentlemen’s club box. From there, he peered over the shoulders of the Chivers, Mingott, and Rushworth men packed in the rows ahead, staring at the exact same horseshoe curve of boxes he’d gazed at two years prior, on the night he had first met Ellen Olenska. He’d half-expected her to appear in old Mrs. Mingott’s usual box, but the velvet seat stayed empty, and he sat motionless, his eyes fixed on the empty space, until Madame Nilsson’s pure, soaring soprano broke out into “*M’ama, non m’ama…*”

He turned to the stage, where the same familiar set of giant painted roses and frilly pansies framed the scene: the same large, blonde Marguerite succumbing to the small, dark, persuasive Faust. His eyes drifted away from the singers, down the curve of the horseshoe to where May sat between Mrs. van der Luyden and his own mother, exactly as she had sat, two years prior, between Mrs. Lovell Mingott and her newly arrived “foreign” cousin. She was all in white, and he suddenly recognized the fabric: the blue-white satin and hand-stitched old lace of her wedding dress.

It was custom in old New York for brides to wear their wedding gowns to formal events for the first year or two of marriage; his own mother still kept hers wrapped in tissue paper, hoping Janey would wear it one day, though poor Janey was fast approaching the age where pearl grey poplin and no bridesmaids would be deemed far more “appropriate.” Archer realized May had barely pulled the gown out of her closet since their return from their European honeymoon, and the shock of seeing her in it made him compare the woman sitting there to the bright, blushing girl he had watched two years prior, all soft blissful anticipation, playing with her bouquet of lilies of the valley. Her outline was a little fuller now, as her statuesque frame had always promised it would be, but her upright, athletic carriage and the transparent, uncomplicated innocence of her expression had not changed a bit. The quiet languor he’d noticed creeping over her these last weeks made her seem even more fragile, more childlike, and the memory of the passionate generosity hidden under that calm stillness hit him hard. He remembered the way she’d looked at him when he’d begged to announce their engagement at the Beaufort ball, the soft, steady voice in the Mission garden, saying “I couldn’t have my happiness made out of a wrong—a wrong to someone else.”

A wild, uncontrollable longing seized him. He would tell her the truth. He would throw himself on that legendary generosity, beg for the freedom he had once been too cowardly to take, and run away with Ellen.

Newland Archer was a quiet, self-controlled man, who had spent his whole life conforming to the strict unwritten rules of his small, closed society. The thought of making a spectacle, of doing something “bad form” that would make Mr. van der Luyden frown and every man in the club box whisper behind his back, usually made him recoil. But in that moment, he forgot the club box, he forgot the van der Luydens, he forgot every single habit and convention that had wrapped around him like a soft, warm blanket his whole life. He walked along the semi-circular passage at the back of the opera house, and opened the door of the van der Luydens’ box like he was stepping through a gate into an unknown new world. “*M’ama!*” the triumphant Marguerite trilled from the stage, and everyone in the box looked up in shocked surprise—no one ever entered a box mid-solo, it was one of the most unbreakable social rules there was.

He slipped between Mr. van der Luyden and Sillerton Jackson, leaned over his wife, and whispered low in her ear: “I’ve got a beastly headache. Don’t tell anyone, but come home with me, won’t you?”

May gave him one quick, perfectly comprehending glance. He watched her whisper an excuse to his mother, who nodded sympathetically, then murmur her apologies to Mrs. van der Luyden, and rise from her seat just as Marguerite fell into Faust’s arms. As he helped her on with her opera cloak, he caught the shared, knowing little smile that passed between the three older ladies.

On the drive back to their house, May laid her hand shyly over his. “I’m so sorry you don’t feel well. I do wish they wouldn’t overwork you so terribly at the office.”

“It’s not that,” he said, confused, letting the carriage window down on his side. He sat staring out at the gas-lit brownstones passing by, feeling her presence beside him like a quiet, watchful question, and refused to tear his eyes away from the street. When they reached their door, she caught the hem of her dress in the carriage step, and stumbled against him. “Did you hurt yourself?” he asked, steadying her with his arm.

“No, but my poor dress—look, it’s torn!” she exclaimed, bending to brush mud off the stained satin hem, then following him up the steps into the hall. The servants had not expected them home so early, and only a single glimmer of gas lit the upper landing. Archer climbed the stairs, turned the light up, and lit the two gas brackets flanking the library mantelpiece. The curtains were drawn, and the warm, familiar glow of the room wrapped around him, like running into the face of an old friend on a secret errand. He noticed how pale May was, and asked if he should fetch her a glass of brandy.

“Oh no,” she said, a faint flush rising to her cheeks as she took off her opera cloak. “But hadn’t you better go to bed at once?” she added, when he opened the silver cigarette box on the table and pulled out a cigarette.

Archer threw the cigarette down on the table, and walked over to his usual spot by the fireplace. “No. My head isn’t that bad.” He paused, the weight of what he was about to say hanging thick in the air. “There’s something I want to say. Something important, that I have to tell you right now.”

She dropped into an armchair, and lifted her head to look at him. “Yes, dear?” she said, so gently that he was surprised she did not look even a little confused by his sudden urgency.

“May—” he began, standing a few feet away from her, as if that small stretch of floor between them was an uncrossable abyss. His voice echoed weirdly in the soft, quiet hush of the room, and he repeated, “There is something I have to tell you… about myself.”

She sat perfectly still, not a single lash flickering. She was still very pale, but her face had a strange, serene tranquility, as if she was drawing strength from some secret, inner source he had never suspected. Archer pushed aside all the stock, conventional lines of self-accusation that were crowding to his lips. He would be blunt, no excuses, no lies. “Madame Olenska—” he started.

At the name, May lifted her hand, as if to silence him. The gaslight glinted off the simple gold band of her wedding ring. “Oh, why should we talk about Ellen tonight?” she asked, with a tiny, soft pout of impatience.

“Because I ought to have spoken a long time ago.”

Her face stayed perfectly calm. “Is it really worth the trouble, dear? I know I haven’t always been fair to her—none of us have, I think. You’ve understood her better than the rest of us, of course. You’ve always been so kind to her. But what does any of it matter, now that it’s all over?”

Archer stared at her, blank and reeling. Could the fog of unreality that had wrapped around his own brain somehow spread to hers? “All over? What do you mean?” he stammered, his words thick and indistinct.

May looked back at him, her eyes clear and bright as glass. “Why—since she’s going back to Europe so soon. Granny approves, and understands, and has arranged to make her completely independent of her husband, no strings attached.” She paused, and Archer grabbed the corner of the mantelpiece in a convulsed hand, holding himself up as his whole world tilted off its axis. “I thought you’d been kept late at the office this evening about all the business arrangements. It was all settled this morning, I believe.” She lowered her eyes under his unseeing, stunned gaze, and a faint, fleeting flush crossed her face.

He understood that his own face must look unbearable, and he turned away, resting his elbows on the mantel shelf and covering his face with his hands. Something thundered and clanged in his ears; he could not tell if it was the rush of his own blood, or the slow, steady tick of the mantel clock. May did not move, did not speak, for five full minutes while the clock ticked on. A lump of coal fell forward in the grate, and when he heard her rise to push it back into the fire, Archer finally turned to face her.

“It’s impossible,” he burst out.

“Impossible?”

“How do you know—what you just told me?”

“I saw Ellen yesterday. I told you I met her at Granny’s house.”

“It wasn’t then that she told you?”

“No. I got a note from her this afternoon. Do you want to see it?”

He could not find his voice, and she walked out of the room, coming back almost immediately. “I thought you knew,” she said simply, laying a single sheet of paper down on the table.

Archer reached for it, his hands shaking. The letter was only a few short lines.
*May dear, I have at last made Granny understand that my visit to her could be no more than a visit, and she has been as kind and generous as ever. She sees now that if I return to Europe I must live by myself, or rather with poor Aunt Medora, who is coming with me. I am hurrying back to Washington to pack up, and we sail next week. You must be very good to Granny when I’m gone—as good as you’ve always been to me. Ellen.*

Archer read the letter two, three times over, then dropped it on the table and burst out laughing. The sound of his own wild, hollow laugh startled him, and he remembered the night years ago when Janey had woken up terrified, catching him rocking with the same incomprehensible, helpless mirth over May’s telegram announcing that their wedding date had been moved forward.

“Why did she write this?” he asked, forcing the laugh to die in his throat.

May met his question with unshaken, soft candor. “I suppose because we talked things over yesterday—”

“What things?”

“I told her I was afraid I hadn’t been fair to her, hadn’t always understood how hard it must have been for her here, alone among so many people who were relations and yet strangers, who felt they had every right to criticize her without ever knowing the full story.” She paused. “I knew you’d always been the one friend she could count on. And I wanted her to know that you and I were the same—when it came to all the things that matter most.” She hesitated, waiting for him to speak, then added slowly, “She understood exactly why I wanted to tell her that. I think she understands everything.”

She walked up to Archer, took one of his cold hands, and pressed it quickly to her soft cheek. “My head aches too. Goodnight, dear,” she said, turning for the door, her torn, mud-stained wedding dress dragging behind her across the library rug, leaving him alone, staring at the crumpled letter on the table, every last shred of the future he’d dared to imagine for himself dissolving into smoke.

It was, as Mrs. Archer smilingly said to Mrs. Welland over their teacups that golden autumn afternoon, a landmark event in the life of any young New York couple: hosting their very first grand formal dinner.

The Newland Archers, in the months since setting up their little household, had welcomed friends often enough in casual, unplanned ways. Newland loved dragging three or four of his legal or club pals home for impromptu suppers, and May greeted them all with that bright, unflinching warmth her own mother had perfected as the mark of a good wife. Newland sometimes wondered if left entirely to her own devices, she would ever have invited a soul across their threshold unprompted, but he’d long since stopped trying to dig for the “real” May beneath the layers of tradition and social training that had shaped her since girlhood. It was simply expected that wealthy, well-connected young couples in New York did their share of informal entertaining — and a Welland married to an Archer was doubly bound to uphold that unwritten rule.

But a grand dinner, with a hired French chef, two borrowed footmen from the van der Luydens’ staff, a serving of that legendary Roman punch no casual party ever dared to serve, roses flown in specially from Henderson’s nursery, and menus printed on thick, gilt-edged Tiffany bristol card? That was something else entirely. As Mrs. Archer never tired of pointing out, the Roman punch made all the difference: not for its taste, but for what it signaled. It meant you’d served canvasback duck or terrapin, two distinct soups, both a hot and cold sweet, every woman in the room turned out in low-cut gowns with bare short sleeves, and a guest list of such gravity even the most sought-after socialites would never dream of turning you down.

It was always a thrill when a young couple sent out their first formal third-person invitations, and their summons was almost never refused. Still, everyone agreed it was a staggering triumph that the van der Luydens themselves — the highest arbiters of old New York’s respectability — had agreed to delay their long-planned trip up to their Skuytercliff estate just to attend May’s farewell dinner for her cousin, Countess Ellen Olenska.

The two matriarchs had ensconced themselves in May’s drawing-room since midday. Mrs. Archer hunched over the dining table writing out the menus in her neat, slanted hand with a sharp gold fountain pen, while Mrs. Welland supervised the placement of potted palms and polished brass standard lamps, tweaking every inch of the room for perfect effect.

Archer stumbled in late from his office, his overcoat still dusted with the crisp autumn street grime, and found them still at their tasks. Mrs. Archer had moved on to place cards for the dinner table, and Mrs. Welland was debating the merits of dragging the big gilded sofa a foot away from the wall to create a cozy private nook between the piano and the bay window.

May, they told him, was in the dining room, inspecting the massive mound of deep red Jacqueminot roses and trailing maidenhair fern piled at the center of the long table, and the arrangement of dainty Maillard bonbons set out in openwork silver baskets between the crystal candelabra. On the piano sat a huge wicker basket of rare orchids Mr. van der Luyden had sent down specially from Skuytercliff. Everything, in short, was exactly as it should be for such a momentous occasion.

Mrs. Archer ran a thoughtful eye down the guest list, tapping each name with the tip of her pen. “Henry van der Luyden — Louisa — the Lovell Mingotts — the Reggie Chiverses — Lawrence Lefferts and Gertrude — (yes, I suppose May was right to include them) — the Selfridge Merrys, Sillerton Jackson, Van Newland and his wife. (How time flies! It feels like only yesterday he was your best man, Newland) — and Countess Olenska. That’s all, I think.”

Mrs. Welland gazed at her son-in-law with soft, affectionate approval. “No one can ever say, Newland, that you and May aren’t giving Ellen the most handsome send-off she could possibly ask for.”

“Ah, well,” Mrs. Archer smiled as she tucked the list into her apron pocket, “I’m sure May wants her cousin to tell all their acquaintances abroad that we’re not quite the barbarians they make New York out to be.”

“I know Ellen will adore it,” Mrs. Welland chirped cheerfully. “She was due to arrive back this morning, wasn’t she? It will leave her with the loveliest last impression. The night before you sail is always such a dreary, lonely thing, otherwise.”

Archer turned for the door, ignoring his mother-in-law’s call for him to go peek at the dining table and make sure May didn’t tire herself out. He bounded up the stairs to his library, and found the room had been ruthlessly tidied: his legal files stacked neatly, his half-finished manuscripts tucked away, ash trays and cedar cigar boxes set out in every corner to make it perfect for the men to smoke after dinner. “It won’t be long now,” he told himself, quiet and resolute, before ducking into his dressing room to change.

Ten days had passed since Ellen had slipped out of New York. In all that time, he’d gotten no word from her save for a small sealed envelope addressed in her handwriting, left on his office desk, holding his old brass door key wrapped in soft tissue paper. It would have been easy to write it off as a polite, formal gesture — but Archer had chosen to read deeper into it. She was still fighting against the fate everyone was pressing her into: a quiet life alone in Paris, far from her cruel husband, but also far from him. Nothing, he’d reasoned, stood in his way of following her. Once he took that irrevocable step, once he showed her he’d burn every bridge here for her, he knew she wouldn’t turn him away.

That quiet confidence in their future had steeled him to play his part in the tedious performances of the present. He hadn’t written to her, hadn’t let a single slip of his tongue or flicker of his face betray the raw, miserable longing gnawing at him. To his mind, they were playing a deadly silent game of cards, and he still held all the trumps. He would wait.

There had been hard moments, of course. The day after Ellen left, his senior partner Mr. Letterblair had summoned him to his office to go over the fine print of the generous trust old Mrs. Manson Mingott had arranged to set up for her granddaughter. They’d pored over the deed for two hours, and all the while Archer could feel there was a hidden, unspoken reason he’d been asked to handle the file, far beyond his family connection to Ellen.

“Well, no one can deny that’s a very handsome arrangement for the lady,” Mr. Letterblair had said, closing the thick leather binder. “I’d go so far as to say she’s been treated very fairly, all around.”

“All around?” Archer had echoed, sharp with quiet derision. “You mean to her husband’s ‘generous’ offer to give her back access to her own money, that he’s been hoarding this whole time?”

Mr. Letterblair had lifted his bushy gray eyebrows a fraction of an inch. “My dear boy, French law is French law. She married under it, one presumes she knew what she was signing.”

“Even if she did, everything that happened after —” Archer had started, then stopped short. Mr. Letterblair had set his pen against the bridge of his wrinkled nose, looking at him with that specific sanctimonious expression virtuous old men used when they wanted young people to know they were far more worldly than they let on. “My dear sir, I’ve no wish to defend the Count’s vices. But on the other hand… I wouldn’t swear on my life there wasn’t a little tit for tat, with that young champion of hers…” He’d pulled a folded confidential report out of his desk drawer and slid it across the table. Archer had never looked at it. He’d known exactly what it contained: the lies, the carefully planted rumors, all to paint Ellen as no better than her scoundrel husband, to justify the family pushing her out of New York to save face. “It’s eminently satisfactory for all parties that we’ve reached this dignified solution,” Letterblair had finished.

A day or two later, his soul had been tested even further, when he’d gone to call on old Mrs. Mingott. She’d been in a querulous, weepy mood. “You know she’s deserted me?” she’d blurted before he could even say hello. “Don’t ask me why, she gave me a dozen different excuses. I think she just couldn’t stand the boredom of Fifth Avenue. And I can’t even blame her, not really. Olenski’s a total scoundrel, but life with him must have been far more fun than the endless dinner parties here. Not that the rest of the family will admit it — they think Fifth Avenue is heaven on earth, with the Rue de la Paix thrown in for good measure. And Ellen will never go back to him, not if you tied her down. She’s going to settle in Paris with that silly old fool Medora Manson. Well, Paris is Paris, you can keep a carriage there on almost no money. But she was as bright and gay as a little bird, and I’m going to miss her.” Two dry, parched old tears rolled down her puffy cheeks and vanished into the lace of her bodice. “All I ask is that they stop pestering me about it. I’d like to be left in peace to digest my gruel,” she’d said, winking at Archer wistfully.

That very night, back at home, May had come to him with a stack of crisp formal invitations in her hand. She hadn’t spoken Ellen’s name since that night she’d gone rushing off to Washington to find her. “I’m throwing a farewell dinner for Ellen,” she’d said, her cheeks pink. “I thought you’d be pleased, you’ve always liked her.” Archer had stared at the list of names in her hand, and suddenly he didn’t just see his gentle, predictable wife — he saw the entire, unstoppable weight of the Welland, Mingott, and Archer families, all standing behind her, solid as a brick wall. He’d given in, quiet and numb, and told her it was a wonderful idea.

When he walked into the drawing room right before dinner, May was kneeling by the hearth, prodding at the logs to make them catch fire on their new, spotless white tile surround. All the tall cut-glass lamps were lit, and Mr. van der Luyden’s orchids had been arranged in vases of pale modern porcelain and heavy knobby silver all around the room. Everyone agreed May’s drawing room was a masterpiece: the gilt bamboo jardinière brimming with primulas and cinerarias, blocking access to the bay window where old-fashioned folks would have put a bronze copy of the Venus de Milo, the pale brocade sofas and armchairs clustered around little velvet tables heaped with silver trinkets, porcelain figurines, and gilded photograph frames, the tall lamps with rosy silk shades glowing like tropical flowers among the potted palms.

“I don’t think Ellen has ever seen this room all lit up,” May said, rising flushed from the hearth, glancing around her space with unashamed pride. The brass fire tongs she’d propped against the chimney clattered to the floor with a crash, drowning out whatever Archer might have said, before the butler announced Mr. and Mrs. van der Luyden.

The rest of the guests streamed in quickly, everyone knowing the van der Luydens hated being kept waiting for dinner. Archer was making polite conversation with Mrs. Selfridge Merry, showing her the tiny, highly varnished Verbeckhoven painting of sheep Mr. Welland had given May for Christmas, when he suddenly realized Ellen Olenska was standing right at his elbow.

She was so pale her dark hair looked twice as thick and dark as he remembered, strung with several loops of chunky amber beads around her throat. It hit him all at once: this was the same little Ellen Mingott he’d danced with at children’s costume parties, all those years ago, when Medora Manson first brought her back to New York from Europe. The amber beads did nothing for her complexion, or maybe her plain dark gown was unflattering, but her face looked almost lustreless, soft and tired, and he had never loved her more than he did in that exact moment. Their hands brushed, and he heard her murmur, “Yes, we sail tomorrow on the *Russia* —” before the butler threw open the dining room doors, and May’s clear, bright voice cut through the noise: “Newland! Dinner’s been announced. Won’t you please take Ellen in?”

Ellen rested her ungloved hand on his arm, and Archer stared down at it, remembering that night they’d sat together in her tiny Twenty-third Street drawing room, his eyes glued to that exact hand for hours. All the beauty that seemed to have drained from her face had taken refuge in those long pale fingers, the faint dimples on her knuckles pressed to his sleeve. He thought to himself: I would cross an entire ocean just to hold that hand again.

Only at a dinner ostensibly thrown for a foreign visitor would Mrs. van der Luyden, the undisputed queen of New York society, accept being seated to the left of the host, the spot reserved for guests of honor. By seating Ellen in the prime seat at Archer’s right, they’d made her foreign status, her soon departure from their tight little circle, the most unmissable tribute possible. In the unwritten code of old New York, it was simply how things were done: no messy scenes, no public anger, the whole tribe rallying around a kinswoman who was about to be gently exiled, to make it look like nothing but love and loyalty. The Wellands and Mingotts would have moved heaven and earth to make everyone see how much they adored Ellen Olenska, now that she’d agreed to leave for Europe of her own free will. Archer sat at the head of his table, watching it all unfold in a daze, marveling at how smoothly they’d rewritten history: all the old grievances against Ellen silenced, her past forgiven, her reputation polished to a perfect shine by the full force of family approval.

Archer felt almost weightless, as if he was floating somewhere between the glittering crystal chandelier and the ceiling, watching the whole scene play out below him. He glanced around the table at all those soft, well-fed, placid faces, and suddenly he saw it in a flash: every single one of these people knew. They’d known about him and Ellen, for months. Every observing eye, every listening ear, they’d all quietly worked together to drive them apart, no blood spilled, no scandal, no fuss. Now the whole tribe was rallying around May, all silently agreeing to pretend none of them had ever suspected a thing. This dinner was nothing more than a performance, a pretty lie, to make sure Newland never dared to break free of the life they’d all mapped out for him.

It was the old New York way, taking life without effusion of blood: a place that dreaded scandal more than disease, that valued decency over courage, that thought nothing was more vulgar than a public scene, save for the people who caused them. Archer felt like a prisoner surrounded by an armed camp, and when he heard them all calmly gossiping about the disgraced Beaufort and his wife Regina, over plates of fresh Florida asparagus, he knew what they were doing. They were warning him, wordlessly, what would happen to him if he stepped out of line. A cold, heavy feeling settled over him, as if the heavy stone doors of the family vault were closing around him. He laughed out loud, and met Mrs. van der Luyden’s startled eyes.

“You think that’s funny?” she asked, with a tight little smile. “Of course poor Regina’s idea of staying on in New York is rather ridiculous, I suppose.” Archer muttered a quick apology.

He noticed then that the man seated on Ellen’s other side had been chatting nonstop to the woman next to him, leaving her in silence, and he saw May glance down the table quickly, making sure everything was running smoothly. The host couldn’t leave the woman beside him hanging in silence, it would be unforgivably rude. He turned to Ellen, and her pale, soft smile met his, as if she was saying, Let’s just get through this, all right?

“Did you find the journey back tiring?” he asked, surprised at how natural and calm his voice sounded. She said no, she’d barely had any discomforts at all on the train, save for that terrible unexpected heat. Archer leaned in, intense. “I’ve never been more frozen in my life than one April day on the train between Calais and Paris.” She said it was nothing, you could always carry an extra rug, every form of travel had its small hardships. He shook his head, sharp and urgent. “None of it matters. Nothing matters at all, compared to the blessedness of getting away from here.” Her face flushed, and he raised his voice loud enough for half the table to hear. “I’m planning to do a whole lot of travelling myself, very soon.” A tiny tremor ran across her face. He turned and called over to Reggie Chivers at the far end of the table: “I say, Reggie! What do you say to a trip around the world? Next month, no delays. I’m game if you are!” Mrs. Reggie piped up immediately that she couldn’t possibly let her husband leave before the Martha Washington Ball she was throwing for the Blind Asylum over Easter week, and Reggie placidly replied that he’d be busy training for the International Polo Match by then anyway.

Mr. Selfridge Merry, who’d once circled the whole globe on his private steam yacht, caught the phrase “around the world” and launched into a long monologue about the terrible shallow harbors in the Mediterranean, how once you’d seen Athens and Smyrna and Constantinople, there was nothing else worth seeing. His wife added that she was forever grateful to their doctor for making them promise never to stop in Naples, what with all the dangerous fevers there. Before long, the ladies were all rising from the table, trailing their silk gowns toward the drawing room.

Up in the library, Lawrence Lefferts immediately took center stage, as always. The talk had turned to Beaufort, of course, and even the revered Mr. van der Luyden, seated in the best leather armchair by the fire, paused to listen to Lefferts’s thundering speech about the sanctity of marriage, the terrible dangers of letting men of obscure origin and tainted wealth into polite society. “If we keep on

Newland Archer sat at his well-loved writing table in his East Thirty-ninth Street library, still dust-spattered from the glittering official inauguration of the Metropolitan Museum’s brand new gallery wing. The great, high-ceilinged spaces, crammed full of curated treasures from across centuries, thrumming with New York’s fashionable elite drifting past labeled vases and statues, had suddenly clicked open a rusted, long-forgotten lock in his memory.

“Wait a minute — this used to be one of the old Cesnola rooms,” he’d heard a stranger murmur beside him, and in an instant the whole crowded reception vanished. He was twenty years old again, perched on a hard leather divan pressed up against a clanking radiator, watching a slim figure swathed in a long sealskin cloak drift further and further away down the bare, underfurnished hallway of the old, unrenovated museum.

That faint, soft vision had roused a whole flood of half-buried memories, and he looked around the library at last with fresh eyes — the room that had held every quiet, meaningful moment of his life for thirty years. It was here, twenty-six years prior, that May had stumbled out her news of their first pregnancy with such pink, awkward circumlocution that any modern young woman would have laughed out loud at her shyness. It was here that their eldest boy Dallas, too fragile to be carted to church in the depths of winter, had been christened by the beloved, larger-than-life Bishop of New York. It was here Dallas had tottered across the unrolled rug, yelling “Dad!” for the very first time, while May and the nursemaid giggled hidden behind the door. It was here their second child Mary — the spitting image of her mother — had stammered out her engagement to the sturdiest, most reliable of Reggie Chivers’s half-dozen sons, and where Archer had kissed her through her wedding veil before they climbed into the motor to drive to Grace Church, the one unshakable, unchanged New York ritual even the shifting times had not dared to touch.

Every big family conversation had unfolded here: they’d mapped out Dallas’s school years, his little brother Bill’s messy love of wild adventures, Mary’s complete disinterest in “ladylike accomplishments” and all-consuming passion for sports and charity work. The restless, curious Dallas had spurned the expected paths of law or business to join a rising New York architect’s firm, one of dozens of the younger generation chasing brand new dreams — Central American archaeology, landscape design, restoring forgotten pre-Revolutionary houses, sneering at the tacky “Colonial” houses only newly rich suburban grocers seemed to want to build these days.

But the memory that still made his chest glow brightest, even now, was the night the Governor of New York had come down from Albany to dine, slammed his fist down on this very table, and declared “Hang the professional politicians! You’re exactly the kind of man this country needs, Archer. If we’re ever going to clean out the mess, men like you have to roll up their sleeves and help.” Archer had jumped at the call, exactly as Ned Winsett had begged him to do decades earlier — but after a single term in the State Assembly, he’d not been re-elected, and had settled gratefully into quiet, unglamorous municipal reform work, writing occasional pieces for the small weekly papers that were trying to jolt the city out of its lazy apathy. It was no grand legacy, but it was something, far more than the narrow, money-focused, society-bound lives his old peers had all settled for. He was the kind of man everyone called “a good citizen” — the first name on every new charity drive, every push to open schools for crippled children, every effort to reorganize the museum, found the Grolier Club, or launch a chamber music society. His days were full, and decent, and he’d long thought that was all a man could reasonably ask for.

He knew he’d missed the bright, wild, fleeting flower of life — but he’d come to see it as a lottery prize so unlikely, the grief of never winning it would be as silly as crying that you hadn’t drawn the one grand prize out of a hundred million tickets. When he thought of Ellen Olenska now, it was gently, abstractly, as you’d think of the heroine of a favorite old novel, or the face in a painting you’d loved your whole life. That faint, tender vision had kept him faithful to May, all their long years together. When May had died suddenly of infectious pneumonia, after nursing their youngest boy Bill back from the very edge of death, he’d mourned her honestly. Their decades together had taught him that even a quiet, unremarkable marriage rooted in shared duty was worth more than the messy, ugly battle of selfish appetites he saw in so many other couples around him. There was goodness in the old ways, he’d always thought.

His eyes drifted around the room, newly decorated by Dallas with soft English mezzotints, Chippendale cabinets, bits of hand-painted blue and white porcelain, and gentle shaded electric lamps, before landing back on the scuffed old Eastlake writing table he’d never had the heart to replace. Beside his inkstand still sat the very first photograph he’d ever taken of May: tall, willowy, round-chested, in a starched muslin dress and floppy Leghorn hat, the way he’d first seen her under the orange trees in a California mission garden. She’d stayed that generous, faithful, unchanging woman her whole life, so completely lacking in imagination that the whole world had shifted and rebuilt itself around her without her ever noticing. That bright, willful innocence had led their whole family to fall into a gentle, unspoken pretense that nothing ever changed, no one ever wanted anything outside the soft boundaries of May’s small happy world. She’d died convinced the world was full of loving, harmonious households just like theirs, certain Archer would pass all their old familiar values on to Dallas and Bill, that Mary would live just as safe and happy a life. She’d gone to her vault in St. Mark’s content, never even noticing the terrifying “trend” of the new century her mother-in-law had always ranted about.

Opposite May’s portrait hung one of Mary, loose-waisted, flat-chested, slouching a little as the new fashion required, proof that the new world had its own goodness too — kinder, more tolerant, less tightly laced than the one her mother had grown up in.

The telephone on his desk clicked, and Archer leaned over to answer it. “Long distance from Chicago for you,” the operator said. It was Dallas, of course — sent out to Illinois by his firm to hash out plans for a grand, palatial lakeside house they were building for a young millionaire with endless ideas.

“Hey Dad!” his son’s bright laugh crackled down the line, clear as if he was leaning in the armchair by their library fire. “How do you feel about sailing Wednesday on the Mauretania? Our client wants me to pop over to Italy to look at old garden designs before we finalize the plans, and he’s covering the whole trip. I’ve got to be back June first, so it’ll be a quick run — but I want you to come with me. No excuses, alright? Book the tickets tomorrow, get us a return out of Marseilles. It’ll be our last little quiet trip just the two of us, before — well, you know.”

Archer hung up the phone, and paced the room slowly. Dallas was right. They’d have a hundred happy gatherings after he married Fanny Beaufort, Archer was sure, but this last little unplanned adventure, no duties, no family, just him and his boy? He’d never get that chance again.
